- Translation: "I wanted to lay in a stock of kisses"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-058 — "I cried for having cried": her oversensitivity before Christmas 1886
- Claim: As the youngest she was not used to doing housework; after Marie left she sometimes made the bed or brought in Céline's flower pots "to please God", but if Céline did not look surprised and grateful she cried. "I was really unbearable because of my excessive sensitivity"; if she hurt someone she cried, and when she began to be consoled "I cried for having cried". Someone had told her: "You cry so much in childhood that later you'll have no tears left."
- When / where: autumn 1886, Les Buissonnets
- People: Céline
- Source: Ms A 44v–45r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je pleurais d'avoir pleuré »
- Translation: "I cried for having cried."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-059 — Christmas night 1886: the shoes in the fireplace
- Claim: Coming home from midnight Mass (where she received Communion) she looked forward to her shoes in the fireplace, a childhood custom Céline kept up. Her father, tired from midnight Mass, was annoyed to see the shoes and said words that "pierced my heart": "Well, thank goodness it's the last year!" Going upstairs to take off her hat, with tears in her eyes, Céline said: "Oh Thérèse, don't go down, it would hurt you too much to look in your shoes right away." But "Jesus had changed her heart": she held back her tears, went down, took the shoes, set them before her father and joyfully pulled out the gifts, "looking as happy as a queen". Papa laughed; Céline thought she was dreaming. She calls it "the grace of my complete conversion"; "the work I had not been able to do in ten years, Jesus did in an instant".
- When / where: night of 24–25 Dec 1886, Les Buissonnets
- People: Louis Martin; Céline
- Source: Ms A 44v–45v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Enfin, heureusement que c'est la dernière année!... »
- Translation: "Well, thank goodness it's the last year!"
- Other sources: The 1911 edited text changes her father's words to a gentler sentence: « Pour une grande fille comme Thérèse, c'est là une surprise trop enfantine; je l'espère, ce sera la dernière année. » ("For a big girl like Thérèse this surprise is too childish; I hope it will be the last year"), HA 1911 ch. V. Céline's words are also changed. See card on Pauline's edits.
- New to English readers: partial (the event is in baseline; the unedited words of Louis are not in Taylor 1912)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: The manuscript wording shows her father irritated, not gently reasoning. Valuable for a truthful portrait.

### F-OW-060 — Blood from the hand of Christ and "I thirst"
- Claim: One Sunday, looking at a picture of Christ on the Cross, she was struck by blood falling from one of his hands with no one to gather it; she resolved to stand in spirit at the foot of the Cross to receive it and pour it on souls. Christ's cry "I thirst!" rang in her heart; "I felt myself devoured by the thirst for souls", first for "great sinners".
- When / where: 1887 (a Sunday; she does not give the date), Lisieux
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 45v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je me sentais moi-même dévorée de la soif des âmes »
- Translation: "I felt myself eaten up with thirst for souls."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-061 — Study of history and science; the Imitation in her muff; Arminjon
- Claim: After Christmas 1886 she felt "an extreme desire to know" and studied history and science alone, limiting herself to a set number of hours. She knew almost all of the Imitation of Christ by heart; she carried it in her pocket in summer and in her muff in winter; at her aunt's they opened it at random and made her recite the chapter. At 14 she read Abbé Arminjon's conferences "on the end of the present world and the mysteries of the future life", lent to her father by the Carmelites; she read it at the window of her study room and copied passages on perfect love.
- When / where: 1887, Les Buissonnets
- People: Guérin family; Abbé Charles Arminjon (author)
- Source: Ms A 46v–47v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « en été, je le portais dans ma poche, en hiver, dans mon manchon »
- Translation: "in summer I carried it in my pocket, in winter in my muff"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-062 — Evening talks with Céline in the belvedere
- Claim: After Christmas the age gap disappeared; before, Céline had said Thérèse would have to grow "the height of a stool" to share her secrets, and Thérèse used to stand on the stool. Each evening in the belvedere they watched the moon rise behind the trees and talked of Heaven; she compares it to Monica and Augustine at Ostia. Her confessor allowed Communion four times a week in May, and a fifth on feasts; she did not ask, but later writes a soul "should tell her confessor the attraction she feels to receive her God".
- When / where: 1887, Les Buissonnets
- People: Céline; her confessor (Abbé Lepelletier, not named here)
- Source: Ms A 47v–48v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « il me faudrait grandir «de la hauteur d'un tabouret» »
- Translation: "I would have to grow 'the height of a stool'"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-063 — Pentecost 1887: telling her father; the little white flower
- Claim: After Vespers on Pentecost she found her father sitting by the cistern, hands joined, in the sunset. She sat beside him, eyes wet; he pressed her head to his heart: "What is it, my little queen? Tell me." Walking slowly with her head on his heart, she told him. His tears mixed with hers; he said only that she was very young; she pleaded so well that he agreed and said God did him a great honour to ask for his children. He picked a little white flower "like a lily in miniature" from a low wall, roots and all, and explained how God had made it grow. She put it in her Imitation at the chapter "That we must love Jesus above all things"; "it is still there, only the stem has broken near the root".
- When / where: 29 May 1887, garden of Les Buissonnets
- People: Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 50r–50v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Qu'as-tu ma petite reine?... confie-moi cela... »
- Translation: "What is it, my little queen? Tell me about it."
- Other sources: In the 1911 text (ch. V). The flower is preserved at the Carmel (check other parts).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-064 — Pranzini: "my first child"
- Claim: She heard of a great criminal condemned to death who seemed likely to die impenitent. She offered "all the infinite merits of Our Lord" and asked Céline to have a Mass said for her intentions, not daring to ask herself for fear of having to admit it was for Pranzini; Céline got the secret out of her and joined her. She told God she was sure he would forgive Pranzini even without confession, but asked for "a sign" for her own consolation. Despite her father's ban on reading newspapers, she read about Pranzini; the day after the execution she opened La Croix: Pranzini had not confessed, but on the scaffold he turned, took the crucifix the priest held out, and kissed its wounds three times. She hid to cry. She calls him "my first child".
- When / where: summer 1887 (execution 31 Aug 1887, Paris; news read 1 Sep 1887), Lisieux
- People: Céline; Henri Pranzini; the prison chaplain (unnamed)
- Source: Ms A 45v–46v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « les lèvres de «mon premier enfant» allèrent se coller sur les plaies sacrées !!! »
- Translation: "the lips of 'my first child' pressed themselves to the sacred wounds!"
- Other sources: In the 1911 text (ch. V). Execution date is from other sources.
- New to English readers: no for the event; partial for the father's newspaper ban and the Mass via Céline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She disobeyed (or thought she did not disobey) her father's ban on newspapers: « Malgré la défense que Papa nous avait faite de lire aucun journal, je ne croyais pas désobéir ».

### F-OW-065 — Her uncle's refusal and three days of darkness
- Claim: Her uncle forbade her to speak of her vocation before 17: letting a 15-year-old enter Carmel was against "human prudence"; Carmelite life looked in the world's eyes like "a philosopher's life"; "everyone would talk"; it would take a miracle. After some time she endured "a very painful martyrdom that lasted three days", "the deep night of the soul"; it rained in torrents all three days. On the fourth day, a Saturday, her uncle called her into his study, reproached her gently for seeming afraid of him, and said he had only asked God for "a simple inclination of the heart"; she was "a little flower God wanted to pick" and he would no longer oppose it.
- When / where: Oct 1887, Lisieux
- People: Isidore Guérin
- Source: Ms A 50v–51v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « cette vie de carmélite étant aux yeux du monde une vie de philosophe »
- Translation: "since Carmelite life looks to the world like a philosopher's life"
- Other sources: Her letters of Oct 1887 (LT) give dates; see letters cards.
- New to English readers: yes (uncle's reasons not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She notes: "in all the grave circumstances of my life, nature was the image of my soul".

### F-OW-066 — The superior of the Carmel refuses: "not before 21"
- Claim: The Carmel's superior (Canon Delatroëtte, not named in the text) would not allow her entry before 21. She went with her father and Céline to see him; he received them "very coldly": there was "no danger in delay", she could live a Carmelite life at home, "if I did not take the discipline all would not be lost"; he was only the bishop's delegate. She left the presbytery in tears under her umbrella in pouring rain and said she would go "to the Holy Father" if the bishop refused.
- When / where: Oct 1887, presbytery of Saint-Jacques, Lisieux
- People: Canon Jean-Baptiste Delatroëtte (superior; unnamed here); Louis Martin; Céline
- Source: Ms A 51v–52r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « si je ne prenais pas la discipline tout ne serait pas perdu »
- Translation: "if I didn't take the discipline all would not be lost"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-067 — Willing to be in hell so that God is loved there
- Claim: One evening, not knowing how to tell Jesus she loved him, she thought with sorrow that he could never receive an act of love from hell, "so I told God that to please him I would gladly consent to be plunged there, so that he would be loved eternally in that place of blasphemy". "When one loves, one feels the need to say a thousand follies."
- When / where: autumn 1887, Les Buissonnets
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 52r–52v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « quand on aime, on éprouve le besoin de dire mille folies »
- Translation: "when you love, you feel the need to say a thousand foolish things"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-068 — Minding two motherless little girls
- Claim: A relative of their maid died young leaving three small children; during her illness the family took in the two girls (the elder not yet six). Thérèse looked after them all day; to make them give way to each other she promised not toys but Heaven's rewards; the elder called her "la grande demoiselle".
- When / where: 1887, Les Buissonnets
- People: two unnamed little girls, relatives of the maid
- Source: Ms A 52v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « «la grande demoiselle», car c'est ainsi qu'elle m'appelait »
- Translation: "'the big young lady', as she called me"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-069 — Bayeux, 31 Oct 1887: hair put up, and the bishop
- Claim: It poured. Her father sent her by omnibus to the cathedral so her fine dress would not be soaked; there was a big funeral and everyone in black stared at her "light dress and white hat"; her father, "with patriarchal simplicity", made her climb to the top of the cathedral. Abbé Révérony was out; they wandered the streets; at a fine hotel she ate little. Révérony, seeing tears: "Ah, I see diamonds... you mustn't show them to Monseigneur!" In the bishop's study there were three huge armchairs; Révérony made her sit in the middle one "where four like me would have fitted". She spoke for herself. Révérony laughed: "you can hardly say you've wanted this for 15 years." The bishop said he must first speak with the Carmel's superior; she cried; Mgr Hugonin put her head on his shoulder and caressed her. Her father told him she had put her hair up to look older; "Monseigneur never speaks of 'his little girl' without telling the story of the hair". Her father asked Révérony, turning around: "Am I dressed well enough like this?" for the Pope.
- When / where: 31 Oct 1887, Bayeux
- People: Mgr Flavien Hugonin (bishop of Bayeux); Abbé Maurice Révérony (vicar general); Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 53v–55r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Ah! je vois des diamants... il ne faut pas les montrer à Monseigneur!... »
- Translation: "Ah, I see diamonds... you mustn't show them to Monseigneur!"
- New to English readers: partial (baseline lists "visit to Bishop Hugonin" only)
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-070 — Notre-Dame des Victoires, Paris: peace about the smile returns
- Claim: Leaving Lisieux at 3 a.m., her father sang in the train "Roule, roule, ma diligence". In Paris the only wonder that "ravished" her was Notre-Dame des Victoires: "the Blessed Virgin made me feel it was really she who had smiled at me and cured me"; from then on she could call her only "Maman". She also asked there to be kept from anything that could tarnish her purity on the trip. They consecrated themselves to the Sacred Heart at Montmartre and left Paris on 7 Nov.
- When / where: 4–7 Nov 1887, Paris
- People: Louis Martin; Céline
- Source: Ms A 56v–57r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Roule, roule, ma diligence, nous voilà sur le grand chemin. »
- Translation: "Roll on, roll on, my stagecoach, here we are on the open road."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-071 — On pilgrimage: her shyness vanished; priests are "weak and fragile men"
- Claim: On the pilgrimage (mostly nobility) she found herself "completely free" of her shyness and spoke easily with great ladies, priests and the bishop of Coutances. Titles and "de"s seemed "only smoke". Living a month with "many holy priests" she saw that "they are nonetheless weak and fragile men"; this taught her the Carmel's purpose, to pray for priests: "I understood my vocation in Italy." She noticed Abbé Révérony watching her at table, leaning over to see her and hear what she said.
- When / where: 7 Nov – 2 Dec 1887, Switzerland and Italy
- People: Céline; Louis Martin; Abbé Révérony; Mgr Germain (bishop of Coutances)
- Source: Ms A 55v–57v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « ils n'en sont pas moins des hommes faibles et fragiles »
- Translation: "they are still weak and fragile men"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-072 — Milan cathedral roof, the grumbling Frenchman, and the students of Bologna
- Claim: At Milan Céline and Thérèse climbed to the top of the marble spire while timid ladies hid their faces; they knelt with their heads on St Charles Borromeo's reliquary during the bishop's Mass. At the Campo Santo an old Frenchman said crossly, "Ah, how enthusiastic the French are!"; he complained of everything; her father offered him his seat. She found Venice "sad". Bologna became "unbearable" because of the students who lined the streets, "above all because of the little adventure that happened to me with one of them".
- When / where: Nov 1887, Milan, Venice, Bologna
- People: Céline; Louis Martin; an unnamed French pilgrim; an unnamed student
- Source: Ms A 58r–59v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « surtout à cause de la petite aventure qui m'est arrivée avec l'un d'eux »
- Translation: "above all because of the little incident I had with one of them"
- Other sources: The 1911 text drops the Bologna students passage ("étudiants" absent). Céline's testimony (other part) may say what the "adventure" was.
- New to English readers: yes (Bologna students); partial (Milan, the grumbler)
- Confidence: high that she wrote it; she does not describe the "adventure".
- Notes: Gap: she does not say what happened with the student.

### F-OW-073 — Loreto: Communion inside the Holy House
- Claim: At Loreto, Communion was given only at one altar in the basilica, outside the Holy House. "It was in the diamond itself and not in the case that we wanted to receive." Her father did as everyone did, but Céline and Thérèse found a priest about to say Mass inside the Santa Casa by special privilege; he put two small hosts on his paten for them. She also put her rosary in the Child Jesus' little bowl, and speaks of "furtively scratching the walls" to take a souvenir.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Loreto
- People: Céline; Louis Martin; an unnamed priest
- Source: Ms A 59v–60r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « C'était dans le diamant lui-même et non pas dans l'écrin que nous voulions faire la communion »
- Translation: "We wanted to receive Communion in the diamond itself, not in its case."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-074 — Into the Colosseum over the barrier
- Claim: The arena was closed off by a barrier. She thought of asking a passing workman for his ladder but did not, "he would have taken me for a madwoman". Then she found a place where the rubble reached the top of the barrier and cried to Céline, "Come quick, we can get through!" They climbed over crumbling ruins while their father called them back; Céline found the cross-marked stone where martyrs fought; they knelt and Thérèse kissed the dust and asked to be a martyr. They took some stones. Their father "could not scold us" and was proud of their courage.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Rome
- People: Céline; Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 60v–61r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Viens vite, nous allons pouvoir passer!... »
- Translation: "Come quick, we can get through!"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She mocks the guide's "petits cornichons et cupides" (for "cupids").

### F-OW-075 — St Cecilia, the catacombs, and a piece of mosaic for St Agnes
- Claim: In the catacombs they slipped away to the bottom of St Cecilia's old tomb and took earth from it; Cecilia became "my favourite saint, my intimate confidante". At St Agnes they could not get a relic for Pauline (Sr Agnès) except a small red stone that fell from an old mosaic.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Rome
- People: Céline
- Source: Ms A 61r–62r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « une véritable tendresse d'amie... Elle devint ma sainte de prédilection »
- Translation: "a real friend's tenderness... she became my favourite saint"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-076 — The audience with Leo XIII, 20 Nov 1887
- Claim: Dressed in black with lace mantillas and a Leo XIII medal on a blue and white ribbon, they attended the Pope's 8 a.m. Mass. At the audience each pilgrim knelt, kissed the Pope's foot and hand; two noble guards touched them to rise. Révérony, at the Pope's right, sent word that no one was to speak to the Pope. Thérèse looked at Céline: "Speak!" Instead of kissing his hand she joined hers: "Most Holy Father, in honour of your jubilee, allow me to enter Carmel at 15!" The Pope said, "I don't understand very well"; Révérony explained. The Pope: "Well, my child, do what the superiors tell you." With her hands on his knees: "Oh Holy Father, if you said yes, everyone would agree!" He looked at her and said, stressing each syllable, "Go... go... You will enter if God wills it!" The guards and Révérony lifted her by the arms and carried her almost to the door; the Pope put his hand on her lips and then blessed her. Céline asked a blessing for the Carmel; Révérony said crossly "The Carmel is already blessed"; the Pope repeated gently "Oh yes, it is already blessed." Earlier the Pope had put his hand on her father's head.
- When / where: Sunday 20 Nov 1887, Vatican
- People: Leo XIII; Abbé Révérony; Céline; Louis Martin; two noble guards
- Source: Ms A 62v–63v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Allons... Allons... Vous entrerez si le Bon Dieu le veut!... »
- Translation: "Come, come... You will enter if God wills it!"
- Other sources: In the 1911 text (ch. VI). Céline's and Révérony's accounts in other parts.
- New to English readers: no for the event; partial for the physical details (carried out by guards, hand on her lips).
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-077 — "The little ball": after the audience
- Claim: She had offered herself to the Child Jesus as "his little plaything", a ball of no value he could throw, kick, pierce, leave in a corner or press to his heart. "In Rome Jesus pierced his little toy; he wanted to see what was inside, and having seen it... he let his little ball fall and went to sleep." That day it rained. A few days later at Brother Siméon's, Révérony was friendly; the old Brother said, "You don't see that in Italy!" On a train platform M. Legoux, vicar general of Coutances, asked, "Well, how is our little Carmelite?"; she learned the whole pilgrimage knew.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Rome and the return journey
- People: Brother Siméon (Christian Brother in Rome); Révérony; Abbé Legoux
- Source: Ms A 64r–65r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « A Rome Jésus perça son petit jouet »
- Translation: "In Rome Jesus pierced his little toy."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-078 — Lost belt buckle at Assisi; a ride in the vicar general's carriage
- Claim: At Assisi, after the monastery of St Agnes, she lost her belt buckle; searching made her miss all the carriages except Révérony's, full of the most distinguished gentlemen. She asked for a seat; a gentleman gave his and sat by the driver. "I looked like a squirrel caught in a trap." She tried to tip the driver from her tiny purse; Révérony paid for both.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Assisi
- People: Abbé Révérony; unnamed gentlemen
- Source: Ms A 65v–66r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Je ressemblais à un écureuil pris dans un piège »
- Translation: "I looked like a squirrel caught in a trap."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-079 — Touching the nail of the Cross; women "excommunicated" in Italy
- Claim: At Santa Croce in Gerusalemme she slipped her little finger through an opening in the reliquary and touched one of the nails of the Cross: "I was really too bold!" In Florence only she could put her hand through the grille to touch rosaries to St Mary Magdalene de' Pazzi's tomb. She could not understand "why women are so easily excommunicated in Italy": they were told at every step "Don't go in here... don't go in there, you'd be excommunicated!" "Ah, poor women, how despised they are!" Yet women love God in greater numbers than men, and at the Passion they had more courage than the apostles. At a Carmelite friary she went into the inner cloister; an old friar waved her away; she pointed to the paintings to say they were pretty; he smiled and left.
- When / where: Nov–Dec 1887, Rome and Florence
- People: an unnamed old Carmelite friar
- Source: Ms A 66r–66v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Ah ! les pauvres femmes, comme elles sont méprisées !... »
- Translation: "Ah, the poor women, how they are looked down on!"
- Other sources: The 1911 text keeps the nail (with an added line that the priest gave permission) but omits the women passage ("excommuniées" absent).
- New to English readers: yes (women passage cut); partial (nail)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: The 1911 edit adds that she asked permission to touch the nail; the manuscript has no permission and calls herself "too bold". A material edit.

### F-OW-080 — Waiting for the bishop's letter; Christmas 1887 without an answer
- Claim: Pauline told her to write to the bishop; her uncle found her letter too simple and rewrote it; Pauline then told her to wait; the letter went ten days before Christmas. Every morning after Mass she went to the post office with her father. Christmas came with no answer; "Jesus did not wake up". That afternoon at the Carmel grille she saw a little Jesus holding a ball with her name on it, and the nuns sang a hymn by Pauline. At home Céline had placed a little boat in a basin with the sleeping Jesus and a ball; on the sail "I sleep but my heart watches" and on the hull "Abandon!"
- When / where: Dec 1887, Lisieux
- People: Pauline; Isidore Guérin; Louis Martin; Céline
- Source: Ms A 67r–68r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « sur la voile blanche Céline avait écrit ces mots : «Je dors mais mon coeur veille» et sur le vaisseau ce seul mot : «Abandon!» »
- Translation: "On the white sail Céline had written: 'I sleep but my heart watches', and on the boat the single word 'Abandon!'"
- Other sources: Absent from the 1911 text ("je dors mais mon coeur veille").
- New to English readers: yes (Céline's boat); partial (post office)
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-081 — New Year 1888: permission granted but delayed until after Lent
- Claim: A letter from Mère Marie de Gonzague told her the bishop's answer had come on 28 Dec but that she had decided the entry would be after Lent. She wept; "this time it was the holy ark that refused entry to the poor little dove". In the three months she decided to lead a serious, mortified life, but "I never did any penances... I always let myself be coddled in cotton wool and fattened like a little bird"; her mortifications were breaking her will, holding back a retort, doing small services unnoticed, not leaning her back when seated.
- When / where: 1 Jan – 9 Apr 1888, Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mgr Hugonin
- Source: Ms A 68r–68v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je me suis toujours laissée dorloter dans du coton et empâter comme un petit oiseau »
- Translation: "I always let myself be coddled in cotton wool and fattened up like a little bird."
- New to English readers: yes (the delay by the prioress and her self-mockery are not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-082 — Entry to Carmel, 9 Apr 1888
- Claim: The evening before, the family dined together; her father said almost nothing but looked at her; her aunt cried; cousin Marie asked her pardon for the pain she thought she had caused. On the morning she looked a last time at Les Buissonnets. At Mass everyone sobbed except her; her heart beat so violently she thought she might die. She knelt for her father's blessing; he knelt too and blessed her weeping. Inside, she was taken to the dark choir with the Blessed Sacrament exposed, and first saw "the eyes of our holy Mother Geneviève" fixed on her. She repeated, "It is for ever, for ever that I am here!"
- When / where: 9 Apr 1888 (feast of the Annunciation transferred), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin; the Guérins; Léonie; Céline; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse
- Source: Ms A 68v–69v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « pour me la donner il se mit lui-même à genoux et me bénit en pleurant »
- Translation: "to give it, he knelt down himself and blessed me, weeping"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-083 — Fr Pichon's verdict: "never a mortal sin"
- Claim: About two months after her entry, Fr Pichon came for Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur's profession. He had thought her fervour "quite childish and her way very gentle". She made a general confession with difficulty; he said: "In the presence of God, the Blessed Virgin and all the saints, I declare that you have never committed a single mortal sin." And: "if he abandoned you, instead of a little angel you would become a little demon." "My child, may Our Lord always be your Superior and your Novice Master." Soon after he left for Canada; she got one letter a year for the twelve she wrote him.
- When / where: May 1888, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Fr Almire Pichon SJ; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur
- Source: Ms A 70r; 71r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je déclare que jamais vous n'avez commis un seul péché mortel »
- Translation: "I declare that you have never committed a single mortal sin."
- New to English readers: partial (Pichon is in Wikipedia; the words and the letter ratio are not)
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-084 — The prioress's severity; the "very simple soul"
- Claim: "Our Mother" (Marie de Gonzague), often ill, had little time for her; she loved Thérèse, "yet God allowed that, without her knowing it, she was very severe; I could not meet her without kissing the ground". Her novice mistress (Sr Marie des Anges) was "a real saint" with whom she worked all day, yet "my soul did not open". An old Mother told her at recreation: "My little girl, I don't think you can have much to tell your superiors... because your soul is extremely simple."
- When / where: 1888–1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Sr Marie des Anges (novice mistress, not named here); an unnamed old nun
- Source: Ms A 70r–70v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je ne pouvais la rencontrer sans baiser la terre »
- Translation: "I couldn't meet her without kissing the floor."
- Other sources: The 1911 edited text rewrites this passage as addressed to Marie de Gonzague ("that I should be treated by you very severely") and inserts the cobweb story from Ms C (HA 1911 ch. VII). See the Pauline-edits card.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-085 — Her father's first crisis, June 1888
- Claim: Her father had had "an attack of paralysis in the legs" in May 1887; on the Rome trip he tired easily. In June 1888, "and above all the 24th", came "our bitterness"; she will not write it, "these memories are too well engraved". His "second attack" delayed her clothing; he recovered and the bishop fixed 10 Jan 1889.
- When / where: May 1887; June 1888 (Louis's disappearance to Le Havre, 23–27 Jun 1888, per other sources)
- People: Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 71v–72r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « nos amertumes du mois de Juin et surtout du 24 de l'année 1888 »
- Translation: "our bitter sorrows of June, and above all of the 24th, in 1888"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that she refers to it; she does not say what happened (Le Havre flight is from other sources).
- Notes: Gap: she withholds details on purpose.

### F-OW-086 — Clothing day, 10 Jan 1889: the snow
- Claim: She had always wished nature to be white on her clothing day; the day was mild and grey with fine rain. Her father "had never been more handsome, more dignified... it was his last feast here below". The bishop intoned the Te Deum though a priest pointed out it was only for professions. Going back into the cloister she saw her "pink little Jesus" among flowers and then snow falling: the courtyard was white. "The whole town was astonished"; people found her "odd taste" for snow.
- When / where: 10 Jan 1889, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin; Mgr Hugonin
- Source: Ms A 72r–72v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « le préau était blanc comme moi »
- Translation: "the courtyard was white like me"
- Other sources: See M-OW-05.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-087 — 12 Feb 1889: "the most humiliating of all cups"
- Claim: "On 12 February, a month after my clothing, our dear Father would drink the most bitter, the most humiliating of all cups." "On that day I did not say I could suffer more!" She calls the three years of her father's "martyrdom" "the most lovable, the most fruitful of our whole life; I would not give them for all the ecstasies and revelations of the saints." She also calls Léonie and Céline "the two poor little exiles of Caen".
- When / where: 12 Feb 1889 (Louis committed to the Bon-Sauveur asylum, Caen, per other sources)
- People: Louis Martin; Léonie; Céline
- Source: Ms A 73r–73v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « notre Père chéri boirait à la plus amère, à la plus humiliante de toutes les coupes »
- Translation: "our dear Father would drink from the most bitter, the most humiliating of all cups"
- Other sources: The 1911 text keeps "calice aussi amer" but drops "la plus humiliante" and "les deux pauvres petites exilées de Caen" (HA 1911 ch. VII).
- New to English readers: yes ("most humiliating", Caen)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She never names the asylum or the illness.

### F-OW-088 — Leading Sr Saint-Pierre to the refectory
- Claim: As a novice she offered to lead the old, crippled Sr Saint-Pierre from evening prayer to the refectory at ten to six. The sister shook her hourglass to mean "let's go". The bench had to be moved "in a certain way"; Thérèse held her by the belt. "Ah, my God! you're going too fast, I'm going to break." Slower: "But follow me! I can't feel your hand, you've let go of me, I'm going to fall; ah, I said you were too young to lead me." At table Thérèse rolled up her sleeves "in a certain way" and, unasked, cut her bread; she won her over by her "most beautiful smile". One winter night she heard distant music and imagined a lit salon with elegant girls, and looked at the moaning old nun and the brick cloister: "to enjoy worldly feasts for a thousand years I would not have given the ten minutes" of this service.
- When / where: c. 1889–1890, cloister and refectory, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Saint-Pierre de Sainte-Thérèse (old infirm nun)
- Source: Ms C 28v–30r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Ah! mon Dieu! vous allez trop vite, j'vais m'briser. »
- Translation: "Oh my God, you're going too fast, I'm going to break!"
- New to English readers: partial (in Taylor, not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-089 — Novice: the lamp taken, the chipped jug, the broken vase
- Claim: As a postulant she liked having "nice things" for her use. One evening after Compline in the Great Silence her lamp had been taken by mistake; she was glad to be deprived "even of necessary things". She came to love "the ugliest and least convenient objects": she was happy when the pretty jug in her cell was replaced by "a big chipped jug". A vase broken behind a window: the novice mistress thought it was her fault; she said nothing, kissed the floor, and promised to be tidier: "my first victory". She loved folding the mantles sisters left behind. Her one mortification from the world, not leaning her back, was forbidden "because of my tendency to stoop".
- When / where: 1888–1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: novice mistress (Sr Marie des Anges)
- Source: Ms A 74r–74v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « une grosse cruche tout ébréchée »
- Translation: "a big jug all chipped"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-090 — Profession delayed eight months; working in the refectory with Pauline
- Claim: After a year the prioress told her not to ask for profession because the superior would refuse; she had to wait eight months more. She recognised "a great self-love" in her wish, and resolved to make "a beautiful dress" of virtues first. Her first job after clothing was the refectory, with Pauline; she could not speak freely to her because of the Rule: "I was in Carmel and no longer at Les Buissonnets."
- When / where: 1889–1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Sr Agnès de Jésus (Pauline); Canon Delatroëtte (superior)
- Source: Ms A 73v–75r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « enfin j'étais au Carmel et non plus aux Buissonnets »
- Translation: "after all I was in Carmel and no longer at Les Buissonnets"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-091 — The nun who rattled like shells; dirty water in the laundry
- Claim: At evening prayer she knelt in front of a sister who made a strange noise "like rubbing two shells together"; "only I noticed, because I have an extremely sharp ear (a bit too sharp sometimes)". She wanted to turn and look; she sweated with the effort; in the end she listened to it "as if it were a delightful concert" and offered it to Jesus. In the laundry a sister opposite splashed dirty water in her face each time she lifted the handkerchiefs; she stopped herself wiping her face and "promised to come back another time to this happy place".
- When / where: c. 1890s, choir and laundry, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: two unnamed sisters
- Source: Ms C 30r–31r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « j'ai l'oreille extrêmement fine (un peu trop parfois) »
- Translation: "I have very sharp ears (a bit too sharp at times)."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-092 — Fleeing like a deserter: the fight over the keys
- Claim: As sacristan, during one of the prioress's bronchitis attacks, she went to return the keys of the communion grille. A zealous young sister (senior to her in religion) tried to take the keys so she would not wake the prioress; Thérèse, "too sly to give them up", insisted, the other pushed the door, and the noise woke the prioress. The sister made "a whole speech": "It's Sr Thérèse of the Child Jesus who made the noise... my God, how disagreeable she is!" Unable to stay silent virtuously, Thérèse fled "without drum or trumpet"; her heart beat so hard she sat down on the stairs "to enjoy in peace the fruits of my victory". "It was not bravery, was it, Mother?"
- When / where: c. 1890–1892, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; an unnamed young sister (identified by editors as Sr Marthe de Jésus? not stated)
- Source: Ms C 14v–15r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je partis sans tambour ni trompette »
- Translation: "I left without drum or trumpet" (she slipped away quietly)
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-093 — Profession retreat: the "underground passage" and the honeymoon with no view
- Claim: In her retreat before profession, Thérèse told Pauline that Jesus had led her into an "underground passage", neither cold nor hot, with no sun, rain or wind. The only light came from the lowered eyes of Christ's Face. She and her "Fiancé" said nothing to each other. She could not see any progress. She said the road gave her no consolation, and yet it gave her all consolations because Jesus chose it.
- When / where: 30–31 Aug 1890, Carmel of Lisieux, during the retreat before her profession on 8 Sept
- People: Sr Agnès de Jésus (Pauline Martin), her sister, whom she calls "Petite Maman"
- Source: LT 110 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 30–31 Aug 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-110-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-30-31-aout-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Il m'a fait entrer dans un souterrain où il ne fait ni froid ni chaud, où le soleil ne luit pas et que la pluie ni le vent ne visitent pas »
- Translation: "He made me go into an underground passage where it is neither cold nor hot, where the sun does not shine, and where neither rain nor wind comes."
- Other sources: LT 111 (to Marie): "Son voyage de noces est bien aride… la nuit lui empêche de rien admirer". LT 112: "je suis dans un souterrain bien obscur". LT 115: "Mon âme est toujours dans le souterrain mais elle y est bien heureuse".
- New to English readers: partial. The 1911 edition prints it as "Lettre IVe" to Mère Agnès, but that letter is a composite of LT 110, LT 112 and LT 115, and it is reworded (see the edit card).
- Confidence: high. Her own text.
- Notes: The same letter also has practical questions: « Faut-il écrire à M. Le pelletier et à M. Révérony que je fais profession ?... » ("Should I write to M. Lepelletier and M. Révérony that I am making profession?").

### F-OW-094 — Pauline's little glass of wine, and permission from Mother Prioress
- Claim: Thérèse reminded Pauline to go to the cellar to take her "little wine". She herself was taking a "little wine" during the retreat. On 1 Sept she wrote that she would take hers, and that she would gladly have had it that morning, but she could not find "Notre Mère" (the prioress, whose permission she needed).
- When / where: 30–31 Aug and 1 Sept 1890, Carmel
- People: Sr Agnès de Jésus (Pauline); "Notre Mère", the prioress, Mère Marie de Gonzague (the letter does not give her name)
- Source: LT 110 (P.S.), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-110-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-30-31-aout-1890/ ; LT 112 (to Sr Agnès, 1 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-112-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-1-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Surtout n'oubliez pas d'aller dans la cave prendre votre petit vin » ; « (Je vais prendre mon petit vin, le même ce matin aurait été bien de mon goût mais je n'ai pas pu trouver Notre Mère). »
- Translation: "Above all, do not forget to go to the cellar and take your little wine." / "(I am going to take my little wine. The same this morning would have been very much to my taste, but I could not find Our Mother.)"
- Other sources: none in this file
- New to English readers: yes. "petit vin" is absent from the 1911 text, and the 1911 composite letter cuts the P.S.
- Confidence: high for the words. Medium for the reason (probably a tonic allowed by the prioress). The letter does not say why they took the wine.
- Notes: She links the wine to the "sweet wine of Engaddi" that she does not drink. This is a small detail of daily life and health in the Carmel.

### F-OW-095 — A letter for her sick father "who will not understand"
- Claim: Thérèse gave Pauline a letter she had written for their father. She asked Pauline to make a draft for her if it would not do, "but I really think he will not understand". On 4 Sept she sent "the letter from Rome" (the papal blessing) for Céline to take to him. She said, "if sometimes he understood, he would be so happy". She asked whether to send him her vows to bless, placed "in the middle of the crown".
- When / where: 1 and 4 Sept 1890. Louis Martin was then in the asylum at Caen (the Bon Sauveur). The letters say only "Papa".
- People: Louis Martin, her father; Pauline; Céline
- Source: LT 112 (1 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-112-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-1-septembre-1890/ ; LT 115 (4 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-115-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-4-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Je vous donne la lettre que j'ai écrite pour Papa ; si vous trouvez qu'elle ne peut pas aller, vous allez bien vouloir me faire un petit brouillon, mais je crois bien qu'il ne comprendra pas... » ; « Peut-être Papa ne comprendra-t-il pas, mais cela n'est pas difficile à emporter et si quelquefois il comprenait il serait si heureux. »
- Translation: "I am giving you the letter I wrote for Papa. If you think it will not do, please make me a little draft, but I really think he will not understand…" / "Perhaps Papa will not understand, but it is not hard to take there, and if sometimes he understood he would be so happy."
- Other sources: Ms A tells of the profession and her father's illness in general terms.
- New to English readers: yes. These passages are absent from the 1911 composite.
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The place (Caen) is not named in these two letters. LT 137 names "l'épreuve si douloureuse de Caen" (the very painful trial of Caen).

### F-OW-096 — Confession before profession: a "little review" with M. Youf
- Claim: Before profession, Thérèse was to see M. Youf the next day. He told her to make "a little review" only of the time since she entered the Carmel. She was very happy to have received absolution on Saturday. She did not understand her retreat and "thought of nothing".
- When / where: 1 Sept 1890, Carmel
- People: Abbé Youf (the file gives only "M. Youf". His role as the Carmel's chaplain comes from outside knowledge, not from this letter); Pauline
- Source: LT 112 (1 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-112-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-1-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Demain je vais trouver M. Youf ; il m'a dit de lui faire une petite revue seulement depuis que je suis au Carmel »
- Translation: "Tomorrow I am going to see M. Youf. He told me to make him a little review, only of the time since I have been in the Carmel."
- Other sources: none in this file
- New to English readers: yes

- Notes: She asks Pauline to pray that "Jesus leave me the peace he HAS GIVEN me" (the capitals are hers). She adds that her darkness should serve to give light to souls.

### F-OW-097 — "Do you think Céline is really going to die?"
- Claim: Five days before her profession, Thérèse asked Pauline whether Céline was really going to die. She had promised Céline the day before to make profession "for both of us". She would not have the courage to ask Jesus to leave Céline on earth if that was not His will. She added: "love can make up for a long life".

- People: Céline Martin, who was ill at that time (the nature of the illness is not stated); Pauline
- Source: LT 114 (to Sr Agnès, 3 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-114-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-3-septembre-1890/

- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Croyez-vous que Céline va vraiment mourir ?... Je lui ai promis hier de faire profession pour nous deux »

- Other sources: 1911 "Lettre Ve" (1890) to Mère Agnès keeps the lines on love and the white robe but cuts the question about Céline.
- New to English readers: yes. The Céline passage is absent in 1911 ("vraiment mourir": ABSENT).
- Confidence: high for the words. The letter gives no diagnosis.
- Notes: A companion card covers her request to die on her profession day.

### F-OW-098 — Asking to die on profession day if she would offend God afterwards
- Claim: Thérèse asked Pauline to tell Jesus to take her on her profession day if she was going to offend Him after it. She wanted to carry to heaven "the white robe of my second baptism" without stain. She also wrote that she wanted, not "sensible" love, but love "felt by Jesus".
- When / where: 3 Sept 1890
- People: Pauline
- Source: LT 114, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-114-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-3-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing

- Notes: She signs this letter "le petit grain de sable" ("the little grain of sand").

### F-OW-099 — Profession practicalities: sewing, notices and the papal blessing
- Claim: Sr Thérèse de Jésus gave Thérèse a note asking her to do some work. Thérèse did not have patterns, and she thought "the linen and the Blessed Virgin" were more urgent. She asked Pauline whether to tell Abbé Domin (of the Abbey) and Mme Papinot about the profession. For Mme Papinot, she thought it better to wait until the veiling. She wanted to thank the "holy old man Simeon", whose letter (a papal blessing from Rome) had arrived.
- When / where: 1–4 Sept 1890
- People: Sr Thérèse de Jésus (a nun of the Lisieux Carmel); Abbé Domin (connected with "l'Abbaye" and "ces Dames"); Mme Papinot; "St Vieillard Siméon"

### F-OW-100 — Night before profession: "I did not have a vocation!"
- Claim: The evening before profession, doing the Stations of the Cross after Matins, "my vocation appeared to me as a dream, a chimera"; "the devil" told her Carmel was not for her and she would deceive her superiors. She called the novice mistress out of choir and, full of confusion, told her; the mistress reassured her. She also told the prioress, "who just laughed at me". Mère Geneviève told her later she had had the same trial.
- When / where: 7 Sep 1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie des Anges; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Geneviève
- Source: Ms A 76r–76v; 78v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Je n'avais pas la vocation !... »
- Translation: "I did not have a vocation!"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-101 — The faded pink candles from Papa
- Claim: For her profession, Thérèse asked Marie to light the candles of the Child Jesus statue before she went to chapter. She did not use the new pink candles. She used the old ones, which had first burned on her clothing day, when Papa (who had given them) was present. Now "the pink colour has gone". She called herself "the orphan of the Berezina".
- When / where: 7 Sept 1890, eve of profession, Carmel
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Marie Martin, her godmother); Louis Martin
- Source: LT 116 (to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 7 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-116-a-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur-7-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « elles ont commencé à brûler le jour de ma prise d'habit, alors elles étaient roses et fraîches. Papa (qui me les avait données) était là et tout était joie !... mais maintenant la couleur de rose est partie, y a-t-il encore pour l'orpheline de la Beresina des joies couleur de rose ici-bas ? »
- Translation: "They began to burn on my clothing day. Then they were pink and fresh. Papa (who had given them to me) was there and all was joy!… But now the pink colour has gone. Are there still rose-coloured joys here below for the orphan of the Berezina?"
- Other sources: 1911 Lettre Ve (7 Sept 1890) to Marie
- New to English readers: partial. The candles are in 1911, but 1911 cuts "l'orpheline de la Beresina" (ABSENT) and changes the addressee's words.
- Confidence: high
- Notes: "Beresina" refers to the 1812 retreat from Russia, used here as a family joke about being an orphan. The letter does not explain it.

### F-OW-102 — Profession, 8 Sep 1890; asleep at prayer for seven years
- Claim: She made vows in peace "like the light breeze" Elijah heard; "I felt truly queen" and asked to free souls from purgatory and convert sinners; she prayed especially "for my little Father, so tried and so holy". Her retreats were "of great aridity". "I should be desolate that I have slept (for 7 years) during my prayers and thanksgivings; well, I am not desolate... little children please their parents as much asleep as awake... doctors put their patients to sleep to operate."
- When / where: 8 Sep 1890; reflection 1895
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 75v–76v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je devrais me désoler de dormir (depuis 7 ans) pendant mes oraisons et mes actions de grâces ; eh bien, je ne me désole pas »
- Translation: "I ought to be upset that I have been falling asleep (for 7 years) during my prayer and thanksgiving; well, I'm not upset."
- Other sources: The sleeping passage is absent from the 1911 edited text.
- New to English readers: yes (the sleeping confession was cut)
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-103 — The wedding invitation: Louis Martin, "master of the lordships of suffering"
- Claim: Thérèse wrote a mock wedding announcement. God and the Virgin Mary announce the marriage of their Son Jesus to "Mademoiselle Thérèse Martin". A second paragraph has "Monsieur Louis Martin, owner and master of the Lordships of suffering and humiliation", and "Madame Martin, princess and lady of honour of the heavenly court", announcing their daughter's marriage.

### F-OW-105 — Eve of the veiling: Papa will not come
- Claim: On 23 Sept 1890, the eve of her public veiling, Thérèse wrote to Céline. The joy of seeing their father at the ceremony had been refused. Her tears were still flowing and she could "hardly hold my pen". She said "another" would explain the talk in "my uncle's parlour", but she could only speak "the language of Heaven". She quoted a letter from "the exiled Father": "Neither of your fathers will be there to offer you to Jesus."
- When / where: 23 Sept 1890, Carmel; the veiling was the next day
- People: Céline; Isidore Guérin, her uncle (his "parloir"); "le Père exilé" (identity not given in the letter, but likely Fr Almire Pichon SJ, then in Canada, from outside knowledge); Louis Martin
- Source: LT 120 (to Céline, 23 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-120-a-celine-23-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « je puis à peine tenir ma plume... et puis une autre te ferait beaucoup d'explications du parloir de mon oncle, mais ta Thérèse ne sait te parler que le langage du Ciel » ; « Ni l'un ni l'autre de vos pères ne sera là pour vous offrir à Jésus. »
- Translation: "I can hardly hold my pen… Another girl would give you many explanations about the talk in my uncle's parlour, but your Thérèse can only speak to you the language of Heaven." / "Neither of your fathers will be there to offer you to Jesus."
- Other sources: Ms A tells of her tears on the veiling day. 1911 Lettre IXe keeps the core of the letter.
- New to English readers: partial. The core is in 1911, but the uncle's parlour and the Pichon quotation are cut.
- Confidence: high for the words. The letter implies, but does not state, that the decision was made in the uncle's parlour.
- Notes: "Ni l'un ni l'autre de vos pères": her father and her director could not come.

### F-OW-106 — EDIT: 1911 adds "Papa ne viendra pas demain!" to LT 120
- Claim: The original never says in plain words that Papa will not come. It speaks of a "diamond" Jesus gave her "today". The 1911 text adds the sentence « Papa ne viendra pas demain! ». It also moves "my tears are still flowing" to "while I write to you". It cuts the uncle's parlour, the "exiled Father's" letter, and "our spouse is a spouse of tears and not of smiles".
- When / where: original 23 Sept 1890; edited 1911 Lettre IXe to Céline ("Mardi, 23 septembre 1890")
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (editor)

- Source type: own-writing compared with the edited text
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: LT 120: « ce diamant précieux Jésus me l'a donné aujourd'hui... Céline... en le recevant mes larmes ont coulé » / 1911: « ce diamant précieux, Jésus me l'a donné aujourd'hui... Papa ne viendra pas demain! »
- Translation: "this precious diamond Jesus gave me today… Céline… as I received it my tears flowed" / 1911: "…Papa will not come tomorrow!"
- Other sources: chk.py "papa ne viendra pas": FOUND in 1911 only; "le Père exilé": ABSENT
- New to English readers: yes (the difference itself)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The 1911 text is clearer but is not her wording. Writers should quote the original.

### F-OW-107 — Card for Sr Marthe de Jésus, her "little companion"
- Claim: On 23 Sept 1890 Thérèse gave a short keepsake to Sr Marthe de Jésus, "my dear little Companion", for "the most beautiful day of your life", the day she was consecrated to Jesus. She signed as "unworthy little Sister".

- People: Sr Marthe de Jésus, a fellow novice

### F-OW-108 — Taking of the veil, 24 Sep 1890: "all veiled in tears"
- Claim: The day "was all veiled in tears": her father was not there to bless her (Céline's plan to bring him to the grille was blocked by "human prudence"); Fr Pichon was in Canada; the bishop was ill and did not come. "That day Jesus let me be unable to hold back my tears, and my tears were not understood."
- When / where: 24 Sep 1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Céline; Louis Martin; Fr Pichon; Mgr Hugonin
- Source: Ms A 75r–75v; 77r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain

- Translation: "on my wedding day I was truly an orphan"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: The manuscript blames "la prudence humaine" (Uncle Guérin, from other sources) for not bringing her father.

### F-OW-109 — Formal letter after profession: "enemies of the Church" in Rome
- Claim: Thérèse wrote a formal letter to Sr Marie-Joseph de la Croix. She said she was now "all Jesus's" and had received the Holy Father's blessing for her profession. The religious who obtained it wrote how many the enemies of the Church were, and that in Rome "the struggle never stops for a moment" against the Pope. She asked for prayers for her father, "so tried by the cross and so admirable in his resignation".
- When / where: 28 Sept 1890, "Monastère du Carmel"
- People: Sr Marie-Joseph de la Croix (of another community; which one is not named); Louis Martin; Pope Leo XIII (as "le Saint Père")
- Source: LT 121 (to Sr Marie-Joseph de la Croix, 28 Sept 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-121-a-soeur-marie-joseph-de-la-croix-28-septembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « à Rome la lutte ne cesse pas un instant à l'égard de notre Saint Père le Pape. C'est désolant... »
- Translation: "In Rome the struggle against our Holy Father the Pope does not stop for a moment. It is distressing…"
- Other sources: LT 114–115 (the "letter from Rome")
- New to English readers: yes ("les ennemis de l'Eglise": ABSENT in 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is a rare political remark. It reports what her Roman correspondent wrote.

### F-OW-110 — Her mock wedding invitation (after Jeanne Guérin's marriage)
- Claim: Eight days after her veiling, her cousin Jeanne married Francis La Néele; watching Jeanne's attentions to her husband, Thérèse wanted to do no less for Jesus. For fun she composed an "Invitation to the Wedding of Sister Thérèse of the Child Jesus of the Holy Face", announcing "Monsieur Louis Martin, Owner and Master of the Lordships of Suffering and Humiliation" and inviting guests to the "Return from the Wedding... tomorrow, the Day of Eternity... the hour being still uncertain, you are invited to be ready and to watch".
- When / where: Oct 1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Jeanne Guérin; Francis La Néele; Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 77r–77v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Monsieur Louis Martin, Propriétaire et Maître des Seigneuries de la Souffrance et de l'Humiliation »
- Translation: "Monsieur Louis Martin, Proprietor and Lord of the Manors of Suffering and Humiliation"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-111 — "I do not see the Sacred Heart like everyone else"
- Claim: Writing to Céline, who was on a journey "là-bas", Thérèse said she did not see the Sacred Heart as everyone else did. She thought of her Spouse's heart as hers alone, as hers was His alone. She asked Céline to murmur her name "over there". She could not write to Léonie: "I don't even have time to reread myself". She signed "nov.carm.ind." (unworthy Carmelite novice), even after profession.
- When / where: 14 Oct 1890. The journey's destination is not named; the Sacred Heart link suggests Paray-le-Monial, but that is not stated.
- People: Céline; Léonie; Marie (the letter was added to "la lettre de Marie")
- Source: LT 122 (to Céline, 14 Oct 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-122-a-celine-14-octobre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « moi je ne vois pas le Sacré Cœur comme tout le monde, je pense que le cœur de mon époux est à moi seul comme le mien est à lui seul »
- Translation: "I do not see the Sacred Heart like everyone else. I think that my Spouse's heart is mine alone, as mine is His alone."
- Other sources: 1911 Lettre Xe (14 Oct 1890) is a heavy rewrite of this letter.
- New to English readers: yes ("je ne vois pas le Sacré": ABSENT in 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The same letter says "Our Mother" allowed her to "come and make my prayer with you". See the edit card for LT 122.

### F-OW-112 — EDIT: 1911 rewrites LT 122 and adds a St John of the Cross verse
- Claim: The 1911 "Lettre Xe" keeps little of LT 122. It opens with a new sentence, « Je comprends tout ce que tu souffres, je comprends tes déchirements et je les partage ». It changes "des millions d'années" to "des millions de siècles, pour toujours". It then adds a five-line verse from St John of the Cross ("Le visage incliné sur mon Bien-Aimé…") that is not in LT 122. It cuts the Sacred Heart passage, the lines on virginity and "the friends of Jesus are rare" among priests, and the message to Léonie.

- People: Mère Agnès (editor)

- Source type: own-writing compared with the edited text
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: LT 122: « des millions d'années passeront pour nous comme un jour » / 1911: « pour des millions de siècles, pour toujours! »
- Translation: "millions of years will pass for us like one day" / "for millions of centuries, for ever!"
- Other sources: 1911 text read directly
- New to English readers: yes (the difference)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The 1911 letter also takes « si je pouvais te communiquer la paix que Jésus a mise dans mon âme au plus fort de mes larmes » from LT 120 (23 Sept), not LT 122. So 1911 blends letters.

### F-OW-113 — Feast-day presents: heather pots and cakes from the Guérins
- Claim: For her feast (15 Oct), the Guérins sent Marie Guérin to wish her a happy feast. Jeanne and Marie sent two pots of heather, now placed near the Child Jesus. Her aunt sent "delicious cakes". Thérèse knew her aunt was ill. She also prayed to comfort her aunt for "the great emptiness" left by Jeanne's departure (Jeanne had just married).
- When / where: 15 Oct 1890
- People: Mme Céline Guérin (née Fournet), her aunt; Jeanne Guérin (newly Mme Francis La Néele); Marie Guérin; Isidore Guérin
- Source: LT 123 (to Mme Guérin, 15 Oct 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-123-a-mme-guerin-15-octobre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Les deux jolis pots de fleurs… sont placés auprès du petit Jésus et à chaque heure du jour réclament pour mes deux petites sœurs autant de grâces et de bénédictions que chaque bruyère contient de petites fleurs »
- Translation: "The two pretty flowerpots… are placed near the little Jesus, and at every hour of the day they ask, for my two little sisters, as many graces and blessings as each heather has little flowers."
- Other sources: LT 125 and LT 133 (her aunt's feast); LT 135 (she asks Céline for heather)
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She calls her cousins "my two little sisters". This shows how close the family ties were.

### F-OW-114 — Signature as a professed nun: "rel. carm. ind."
- Claim: From profession on, Thérèse signs "rel.carm.ind." ("unworthy Carmelite religious"). In LT 122 and LT 127 (Oct 1890, Apr 1891) she still signs "nov.carm.ind." (unworthy Carmelite novice). One 1893 note (LT 153) is signed "rel.carm." without "ind.".

### F-OW-115 — Wishing Céline might die within the year
- Claim: For Céline's feast, Thérèse wrote that it might be "the last time" Céline's feast was kept on earth: "perhaps!… what a sweet hope". She sent two "Céline" flowers (a flower that shares Céline's name), one stem, one sun, "and no doubt the same day will see them die".
- When / where: 20 Oct 1890
- People: Céline
- Source: LT 124 (to Céline, 20 Oct 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-124-a-celine-20-octobre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Céline, c'est peut-être la dernière fois que ta fête sera fêtée sur la terre !... peut-être !... quel doux espoir... »
- Translation: "Céline, perhaps this is the last time your feast will be kept on earth!… perhaps!… what a sweet hope…"
- Other sources: LT 132 (Céline flowers blooming early); LT 130 (asking Jesus to take the "lilies" out of the world early)
- New to English readers: yes (not among the 1911 Céline letters)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is a strong example of her longing for heaven at 17, applied to her sister. It does not fit the usual sweet picture.

### F-OW-116 — Aunt Guérin's feast: "the right of a Benjamin"
- Claim: For her aunt's feast on 19 Nov, Thérèse called herself the "last, smallest" daughter, who would never be outdone in tenderness, "the right of a Benjamin". She remembered looking forward to 19 Nov as a child because of the treats ("gâteries"). Being "very poor", she charged her rich royal Spouse to pay her debt.
- When / where: 17 Nov 1890
- People: Mme Guérin
- Source: LT 125 (to Mme Guérin, 17 Nov 1890), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-125-a-mme-guerin-17-novembre-1890/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « n'est-ce pas le droit d'un Benjamin d'aimer plus que les autres ?... » ; « à cause des jolies gâteries dont j'étais comblée ce jour-là »
- Translation: "Is it not a Benjamin's right to love more than the others?…" / "because of the pretty treats I was showered with on that day"
- Other sources: LT 139, LT 152 ("Votre petit Benjamin, qui est fier de son titre"); LT 181 (Marie Guérin is now "notre Benjamin")
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The "Benjamin" title runs through these letters until Marie Guérin enters in 1895.

### F-OW-117 — Lending a book, in secret, to a doubting married cousin
- Claim: After a visit from "Marguerite M.", who told the sisters intimate secrets and whose faith was "in danger", Thérèse asked Céline to lend her a book on "the mysteries of the future life". Marguerite could read books without her husband knowing. Céline should seem not to know the book. She should tell Marguerite to begin at chapter 3, "where there is a little picture". Mme Maudelonde and "my aunt" should not know. The idea "was given to me" by God, but "Th. can do nothing without Céline".
- When / where: 3 Apr 1891; the visit was that afternoon in the Carmel parlour
- People: "Marguerite M." (the file gives only this; the link with Mme Maudelonde suggests Marguerite Maudelonde, a cousin, but her married name is not given); her unnamed husband; Mme Maudelonde; Mme Guérin; Céline
- Source: LT 126 (to Céline, 3 Apr 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-126-a-celine-3-avril-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Elle nous a dit qu'elle pouvait lire des livres sans que son mari le sache » ; « de le commencer seulement au 3e chapitre où il y a une petite image »
- Translation: "She told us she could read books without her husband knowing." / "to begin it only at the 3rd chapter, where there is a little picture"
- Other sources: none in this file. The title is not given. The phrase "les mystères de la vie future" matches the subtitle of Arminjon's book, which Ms A says she loved, but the letter does not name the book.
- New to English readers: yes ("Marguerite" in this sense: ABSENT)
- Confidence: high for the facts in the letter. Low for the book's identity.
- Notes: This is a small, discreet apostolate carried out through Céline. It shows her practical sense.

### F-OW-118 — "Together we are 40 years old now"
- Claim: For Céline's 22nd birthday, Thérèse wrote her fourth birthday letter from the Carmel. She asked why Céline never asked, "Why did God not create me an angel?" She said God wanted "angels of the earth". She hoped Céline would win "a certain faded and withered Lily" back to God. She added a joke: "together we are 40 years old now", so no wonder they had so much experience.
- When / where: 26 Apr 1891
- People: Céline; the "Lys fané" (not named here; see the Loyson card for LT 129)
- Source: LT 127 (to Céline, 26 Apr 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-127-a-celine-26-avril-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « (Sais-tu qu'à nous deux nous avons 40 ans maintenant, ce n'est pas étonnant que nous ayons déjà l'expérience de tant de choses, qu'en penses-tu ?) »
- Translation: "(Do you know that between the two of us we are 40 years old now? No wonder we already have experience of so many things. What do you think?)"
- Other sources: 1911 Lettre XIe (26 Apr 1891)
- New to English readers: partial. The "angel" passage is in 1911. The joke and the "faded Lily" are cut (ABSENT).
- Confidence: high
- Notes: See the edit card on 1911 Lettre XIe.

- Source: LT 128 (to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 5 July 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-128-a-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur-5-juillet-1891/

- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Souvenir offert à ma Sœur chérie le jour de la fête du précieux sang pour sa sortie du noviciat. »

- Other sources: none

### F-OW-121 — Praying for the ex-priest Hyacinthe Loyson: "the unhappy prodigal"
- Claim: Thérèse gave Céline news of "the unhappy prodigal". He had gone to Coutances, where he repeated the lectures he had given at Caen, and he planned to tour France. People said remorse was eating at him. He went round churches with a large crucifix, and "his wife follows him everywhere". She said he was guilty, perhaps more than any sinner who was ever converted. But could Jesus not do once what He had never done? She quoted Christ's words to St Margaret Mary: "a just soul… can obtain pardon for a thousand criminals".
- When / where: 8 July 1891
- People: the ex-Carmelite Hyacinthe Loyson (not named in the letter, and outside knowledge identifies him); his wife; Céline; "Notre Mère" (ill)
- Source: LT 129 (to Céline, 8 July 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-129-a-celine-8-juillet-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Le malheureux prodigue est allé à Coutances où il a recommencé les conférences de Caen » ; « Sa femme le suit partout. »
- Translation: "The unhappy prodigal has gone to Coutances, where he started the Caen lectures again." / "His wife follows him everywhere."
- Other sources: LT 127 ("un certain Lys fané et flétri"); Ms C (she offered her last Communion for him, which is outside this file)
- New to English readers: yes ("le malheureux prodigue": ABSENT)

- Notes: She calls him "notre frère, un fils de la Ste Vierge" ("our brother, a son of the Blessed Virgin").

### F-OW-122 — "Our Mother" is still very ill (July 1891)
- Claim: Thérèse told Céline that "Our dear Mother" was still very ill. It was sad to see those we love suffer, but Jesus would not refuse to leave her with them a while longer.
- When / where: 8 July 1891
- People: "Notre Mère chérie". The prioress in July 1891 was Mère Marie de Gonzague (outside knowledge), but the letter gives no name.
- Source: LT 129, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-129-a-celine-8-juillet-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Notre Mère chérie est encore bien souffrante, c'est bien triste de voir ainsi souffrir ceux que l'on aime. »
- Translation: "Our dear Mother is still very unwell. It is very sad to see those we love suffer like this."
- Other sources: LT 130 (Mère Geneviève's prayers)
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium. The identity of the sick "Mother" is not stated.
- Notes: This shows tenderness towards the prioress, whom later accounts often cast as a hard figure.

### F-OW-123 — Mère Geneviève's feast: fish, cherries and cakes
- Claim: "Yesterday's feast was delightful, a real foretaste of Heaven." The Guérins' gifts (fish, cherries, cakes) pleased them. Mère Geneviève was touched by Céline's letter and prayed for her. Thérèse called her "such a saint".
- When / where: letter 23 July 1891; the feast was 22 July 1891
- People: Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse (a founding nun of the Lisieux Carmel); Céline; Mme Guérin

### F-OW-124 — The lawyer who came to ask for Céline's hand
- Claim: An "avoué" (a lawyer) had come to seek Céline in marriage. Thérèse wrote that he "made me laugh a lot". "He is not shy," to come for the fiancée of the King of Heaven. The poor man had not seen "the sign the Spouse has placed on your forehead".
- When / where: 23 July 1891
- People: Céline; an unnamed avoué

### F-OW-125 — Papa's white hair turning into suns

### F-OW-126 — The 1891 retreat: "wondering whether there was a Heaven"
- Claim: At the retreat a year after her profession (Oct 1891), she had "great interior trials of all kinds (even to wondering sometimes whether there was a Heaven)". The preacher, meant for "great sinners", was "appreciated only by me": "my soul was like a book in which the Father read better than I did"; he told her that her faults did not grieve God and that God was very pleased with her. "I am of such a nature that fear makes me draw back; with love, not only do I go forward, I fly."
- When / where: Oct 1891, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Fr Alexis Prou OFM (not named in the text)
- Source: Ms A 80r–80v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « jusqu'à me demander parfois s'il y avait un Ciel »
- Translation: "even to asking myself at times whether there was a Heaven"
- Other sources: The phrase is absent from the 1911 text. Baseline (Wikipedia) names Fr Prou's retreat.
- New to English readers: yes (the early doubt about Heaven, 1891, before the 1896 trial)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Important: a first doubt about Heaven five years before the famous trial of faith.

### F-OW-127 — Jeanne and Francis La Néele: the pharmacy that would not sell
- Claim: Thanking Jeanne and Francis for her feast wishes, Thérèse prayed for a little "Isidore" or a little "Jeanne". She also prayed "that the pharmacy may at last be sold". She was willing to take on herself the trials God kept for those she loved. Marie thanked Jeanne for what she sent for the "vide-poche" (a tidy for pocket items), a piece of work "Our Mother" was happy to give her.

- Translation: "I also ask that the pharmacy may at last be sold." / "even if I have to take on myself the trials He keeps for them"

### F-OW-128 — The "Céline" flower that bloomed a month early
- Claim: Thérèse wrote that the winter just past had been the harshest ever, so all flowers were late. The "Céline" flower, "the flower of my childhood", bloomed a month early. She read it as an image of suffering making Céline's soul grow. She said her desires for martyrdom were being reborn: "blood for blood and life for life".
- When / where: 20 Oct 1891; fourth feast-day letter from the Carmel
- People: Céline
- Source: LT 132 (to Céline, 20 Oct 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-132-a-celine-20-octobre-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « jamais l'hiver n'avait été si rigoureux que l'année dernière » ; « elle s'est épanouie un mois avant l'époque de sa floraison »
- Translation: "Never had the winter been as harsh as last year." / "It opened a month before its flowering time."
- Other sources: LT 124 (the "Céline" flowers)
- New to English readers: yes ("la fleur Céline": ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She reports a natural event read as a sign. It is not a miracle claim.

### F-OW-129 — A feast gift made from her father's hair
- Claim: For Mme Guérin's feast in 1891, the Carmel gave a small present made from Louis Martin's hair. "Our good Mother" had it made. Thérèse was moved to see "these poor hairs" loved by the father God had taken from them. They were given to the one who, after that father, was dearest to her. She also wrote that God had seemed to take from her in childhood a joy she had never known (a mother), and that her aunt had received a mother's tenderness for her.
- When / where: 16 Nov 1891
- People: Mme Guérin; Louis Martin (then in Caen); "notre bonne Mère" (prioress, not named)
- Source: LT 133 (to Mme Guérin, 16 Nov 1891), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-133-a-mme-guerin-16-novembre-1891/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Mon cœur est rempli d'émotion en voyant ces pauvres cheveux qui sans doute n'ont d'autre valeur que le travail délicat et la grâce de leur arrangement, mais qui cependant étaient aimés de celui que le bon Dieu nous a ravi »
- Translation: "My heart is full of emotion seeing these poor hairs. No doubt their only value is the delicate work and the grace of their arrangement, but they were loved by the one God has taken from us."
- Other sources: none
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the fact of the gift. The letter does not describe the object's form.
