### F-OW-195 — The sister who "had the talent of displeasing me in everything"
- Claim: "There is in the community a sister who has the talent of displeasing me in everything; her manners, her words, her character seemed very disagreeable to me." Thérèse did for her what she would do for the person she loved most, prayed for her each time they met, gave her her "most lovable smile", and when the struggle was too hard "fled like a deserter". The sister once asked, pleased: "Would you tell me, Sr Thérèse of the Child Jesus, what attracts you so much to me? Each time you look at me I see you smile." Thérèse said she smiled because she was glad to see her ("I did not add that it was from the spiritual point of view").
- When / where: c. 1895–1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: an unnamed sister (identified by editors as Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin)
- Source: Ms C 13v–14r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « une soeur qui a le talent de me déplaire en toutes choses »
- Translation: "a sister who has a knack for getting on my nerves in everything"
- Other sources: Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin gave testimony at the processes (other stage-2 part). The identification is not in Thérèse's text.
- New to English readers: partial (famous in modern retellings; not in baseline)
- Confidence: high for the text; medium for the identification.

### F-OW-196 — The Christmas trees and "a pearl for your crown"
- Claim: At recreation (without the prioress) the portress rang for someone to open the workmen's gate for the Christmas-crib trees; the subprioress told Thérèse or her neighbour to go as "tierce". Thérèse untied her apron slowly so her neighbour could go first and have the pleasure. The sister replacing the bursar laughed: "Ah, I thought it wouldn't be you gaining a pearl for your crown; you were going too slowly." "Certainly the whole community believed I had acted from nature." It taught her never to judge.
- When / where: c. Dec (year not given), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: the subprioress; an unnamed sister; the acting bursar
- Source: Ms C 13r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « j'avais bien pensé que ce n'était pas vous qui alliez gagner une perle à votre couronne, vous alliez trop lentement... »
- Translation: "I thought as much: you weren't going to earn a pearl for your crown, you were going too slowly!"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-197 — Offering to Merciful Love, 9 Jun 1895
- Claim: On Trinity Sunday, 9 Jun 1895, she thought of souls who offer themselves as victims to God's Justice; she did not feel drawn to it. "O my God, will only your Justice receive victims? Doesn't your Merciful Love need them too?" She offered herself as a victim to Merciful Love, with Pauline's permission. Since then, "I cannot fear purgatory."
- When / where: 9 Jun 1895, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus
- Source: Ms A 84r–84v; see Pri 6 (works.md)
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Votre Amour Miséricordieux n'en a-t-il pas besoin lui aussi?... »
- Translation: "Doesn't your Merciful Love need them too?"
- New to English readers: no (event in baseline); partial (her reasoning)
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-198 — The rosary "costs me more than an instrument of penance"
- Claim: "Alone (I am ashamed to admit it) saying the rosary costs me more than putting on an instrument of penance... I feel I say it so badly! However hard I try to meditate on the mysteries of the rosary, I cannot fix my mind." She cannot bear searching books for beautiful prayers: "it gives me a headache, there are so many!" "I do as children do who cannot read: I simply tell God what I want to tell him." Prayer is "a surge of the heart, a simple look turned towards Heaven". When dry she says one Our Father "very slowly".
- When / where: written June 1897
- People: —
- Source: Ms C 25r–25v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « la récitation du chapelet me coûte plus que de mettre un instrument de pénitence »
- Translation: "saying the rosary costs me more than putting on an instrument of penance"
- Other sources: The rosary confession is not in the 1911 edited text (check: not quoted there; see gaps file).
- New to English readers: yes (if absent from Taylor; see gaps file)
- Confidence: high.

- Claim: Thérèse wrote a note to her cousin Marie, hoping "(trembling)" that Marie was keeping her promises to stay "as quiet as a little child in its Mother's arms". She prayed for everyone at La Musse, who were accepting "the sacrifice of separation".

### F-OW-200 — Léonie leaves the Visitation: the sisters' "agony" and the Uncle's calm
- Claim: The Martin sisters were "plunged in great grief because of our poor Léonie. It was like a true agony." God sent no consolation. Thérèse could only pray Christ's prayers: "My God, my God, why have you forsaken us!" and "Thy will be done". Then God sent "one of His Saints still travelling on earth": seeing her uncle's calm, their anguish disappeared. Her uncle's faith "can be compared to Abraham's". He seemed "already transfigured", blessing the hand that took "his Treasure" (Marie, entering the Carmel).
- When / where: 20 July 1895, Carmel parlour (the uncle's visit "yesterday")
- People: Isidore Guérin; Marie Guérin; Léonie; Mme Guérin
- Source: LT 178 (to Mme Guérin, 20–21 July 1895), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-178-a-mme-guerin-20-21-juillet-1895/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Nous étions plongées dans une bien grande douleur à cause de notre pauvre Léonie ; c'était comme une véritable agonie » ; « je ne pouvais faire une autre prière que celle de Notre Seigneur sur la croix : «Mon Dieu, mon Dieu, pourquoi nous avez-vous abandonnés!» »
- Translation: "We were plunged into very great grief because of our poor Léonie. It was like a real agony." / "I could say no other prayer than Our Lord's on the cross: 'My God, my God, why have you abandoned us!'"
- Other sources: LT 176 (the Le Mans temptation)
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT in 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She writes that she must not tell her uncle he is a saint "because he would not love me any more".

### F-OW-201 — Léonie in the parlour, crying; Marie "a strong woman" who prefers "little angel"
- Claim: The next day Marie Guérin brought Léonie (just out of the Visitation) to the Carmel. "We could not get a word out of her, she was crying so much. At last she looked at us and all went well." Marie had been "a true strong woman". She disliked the compliment, so Thérèse called her "little angel", which she liked better. "She is gay enough to make stones laugh." The nuns served both girls "in earthenware dishes like the Carmelites, which amused them a lot". Marie wanted to give Thérèse "three sous for a stamp" rather than let her write.
- When / where: 21 July 1895, Carmel parlour
- People: Léonie; Marie Guérin; Mme Guérin
- Source: LT 178, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-178-a-mme-guerin-20-21-juillet-1895/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « nous ne pouvions pas lui faire dire une parole tant elle pleurait, enfin elle a fini par nous regarder et tout s'est bien passé » ; « Elle est d'une gaîté à faire rire les pierres » ; « nous les avons servies dans les plats en terre comme les carmélites ce qui les a bien amusées »
- Translation: "We could not get a word out of her, she was crying so hard. In the end she looked at us and everything went well." / "She is cheerful enough to make the stones laugh." / "We served them in earthenware dishes like the Carmelites, which amused them a lot."
- Other sources: LT 180
- New to English readers: yes ("plats en terre": ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She comments that energy is "the most necessary virtue", and that if Marie could give a little to Léonie "it would not do the latter any harm". This is frank about her sister.

### F-OW-203 — "The old dean of the novitiate" and the photographs at recreation
- Claim: Thérèse told Jeanne that the whole novitiate had caught Marie Guérin's gaiety. "The old dean of the novitiate", "despite my seven and a half years of religious life", often lost her gravity before "the charming Imp". "Our Mother" brought Jeanne's and Francis's photographs to recreation and passed them round. When Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie's turn came, she smiled at each, saying "Good morning, my Fifine… Good morning, my Seraph", and all the Carmelites laughed. Her "beautiful voice" was the charm of recreations.
- When / where: 14–17 Oct 1895, Carmel recreation
- People: Jeanne La Néele ("Fifine"); Francis La Néele ("mon Séraphin"); Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin), postulant; Mère Agnès ("Notre Mère")
- Source: LT 180 (to Mme La Néele, 14–15 and 17 Oct 1895), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-180-a-mme-la-neele-14-15-et-17-octobre-1895/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « C'est une grande consolation pour moi, la vieille doyenne du noviciat, de voir tant de gaîté entourer mes derniers jours » ; « «Bonjour, ma Fifine... Bonjour, mon Séraphin». Ces expressions de tendresse ont fait rire toutes les carmélites »
- Translation: "It is a great comfort to me, the old dean of the novitiate, to see so much gaiety around my last days." / "'Good morning, my Fifine… Good morning, my Seraph.' These words of tenderness made all the Carmelites laugh."
- Other sources: 1911 prints only the "centuple" paragraph, dated "Août 1895".
- New to English readers: yes ("vieille doyenne", "Bonjour, ma Fifine": ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: "mes derniers jours" is said in jest, as she was 22. She was still in the novitiate after 7½ years.

### F-OW-204 — Flower patterns, not fish: "I have never liked what is eaten"
- Claim: Jeanne offered a feast present. Thérèse asked for "a flower pattern" (a model for painting) instead of fish. "My Uncle spoils his dear Carmelites, they are sure not to die of hunger… Little Thérèse, who has never liked what is eaten, does like things useful to her community." With patterns one can earn money to buy fish, "a bit like Perrette's story". She would like a branch of wild roses, or periwinkles, buttercups, "or even any other common flower". She added the pearls Jeanne had mentioned. "You see I am converted… I talk like a magpie and I am too bold in my requests."
- When / where: 14–17 Oct 1895
- People: Jeanne La Néele; Isidore Guérin
- Source: LT 180, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-180-a-mme-la-neele-14-15-et-17-octobre-1895/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « La petite Thérèse, qui n'a jamais aimé ce qui se mange, aime bien cependant les choses utiles à sa communauté, elle sait qu'avec des modèles, l'on peut gagner de l'argent pour acheter du poisson. » ; « je parle comme une pie »
- Translation: "Little Thérèse, who has never liked things to eat, does like things useful to her community. She knows that with patterns one can earn money to buy fish." / "I chatter like a magpie."
- Other sources: LT 167 bis (Jeanne's "jolis modèles" in 1894)
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT in 1911)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Her painting work helped the community's income. "Perrette" is La Fontaine's milkmaid who counts her profits before she has them.

### F-OW-206 — First "brother": the seminarian Maurice Bellière (Oct 1895)
- Claim: In 1895, "as a feast-day bouquet" from St Teresa (15 Oct), while she was at the laundry, Mère Agnès took her aside and read a letter from a young seminarian asking for a sister to pray for him as a future missionary; Mère Agnès chose Thérèse. "It was as if musical strings forgotten until then had been touched for the first time." After a thank-you letter, he gave no sign of life until July, except a card in November saying he was entering the barracks.

- New to English readers: partial

### F-OW-207 — Going to the laundry; Francis "found me remedies"
- Claim: Thérèse had to stop writing: "I must go 'to the washing', to listen, while scrubbing the linen, to the dear little Imp, who will no doubt sing that 'This washing must lead us to the Shore without Storm…'". The next day was St Luke's feast, one of Francis's patrons. She would offer Communion for him and ask Jesus to reward him for "the trouble he took to find me remedies".

- Translation: "I have to go 'to the washing', to listen, while scrubbing the linen, to the dear little Imp" / "I will ask Jesus to reward him for the trouble he took to find me remedies"

### F-OW-209 — Why and how she wrote Ms A
- Claim: She wrote Ms A for Mère Agnès, who asked her to; she first thought it would "distract my heart by occupying it with itself". Before writing she knelt before the statue of the Virgin. She writes "without worrying about style" and admits many digressions. She writes "at so many different times, because of my little free time". She titled it "Springtime story of a little white flower written by herself".
- When / where: Jan 1895 – Jan 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus
- Source: Ms A 2r–3v; 56v

- Translation: "The springtime story of a little white flower, written by herself"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

- Claim: "I attach no importance to my dreams... usually I dream of woods, flowers, streams and the sea, and almost always I see pretty little children, I catch butterflies and birds such as I have never seen." She wonders why, thinking of God all day, she does not dream of him more.

### F-OW-211 — Books leave her dry; only the Gospel feeds her
- Claim: At 17–18 St John of the Cross was her only spiritual food; later "all books left me in aridity... If I open a book by a spiritual author (even the most beautiful), I feel my heart tighten". Scripture and the Imitation help; "above all it is the Gospel". "I have never heard him speak, but I feel he is in me"; lights come "rather in the middle of the day's occupations" than at prayer.
- When / where: 1890–1895
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 83r–83v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je sens aussitôt mon coeur se serrer »
- Translation: "I immediately feel my heart tighten"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-212 — Good Friday 1896: the first blood
- Claim: She had kept the Lenten fast "in all its rigour" and felt strong. On the night of Holy Thursday to Good Friday, after watching at the Repository until midnight, she lay down and "felt something like a stream rising, rising, bubbling up to my lips". The lamp was out; she waited for morning to confirm "my happiness", thinking it was blood. At dawn she went to the window and saw she was right. At the chapter of faults she told the prioress, adding she felt no pain and asking for no special treatment; she kept all the Good Friday austerities. The next night it happened again.
- When / where: night of 2–3 Apr 1896, and 3–4 Apr 1896; her cell, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Source: Ms C 4v–5r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je sentis comme un flot qui montait, montait en bouillonnant jusqu'à mes lèvres »
- Translation: "I felt something like a wave rising, rising, bubbling up to my lips"
- New to English readers: partial (the event is in baseline; her description is not)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She reports joy, not fear. She says she felt no pain.

### F-OW-213 — The trial of faith begins (Easter 1896): "a wall that rises to the heavens"
- Claim: During the Easter season 1896 "Jesus made me feel that there really are souls who do not have faith"; her soul was invaded "by the thickest darkness", and the thought of Heaven became "only a subject of combat and torment"; it had not ended by June 1897. She uses a parable of someone born in a land of fog who has heard of a sunny country; the darkness, "borrowing the voice of sinners", mocks her: "You dream of light... Go on, go on, rejoice in death, which will give you not what you hope for but a night deeper still, the night of nothingness." "I do not want to write more; I would be afraid of blaspheming." "It is no longer a veil for me, it is a wall that rises to the heavens and covers the starry sky." "When I sing of the happiness of Heaven... I feel no joy, for I simply sing what I WANT to believe." She says she has made more acts of faith in one year than in her whole life; she turns her back on the enemy "without deigning to look him in the face" and tells Jesus she is ready to shed her blood "to confess that there is a Heaven". She sits "at the table of sinners" and accepts to eat "the bread of sorrow" for them.
- When / where: Easter 1896 (Easter was 5 Apr 1896) to at least June 1897
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague (recipient)
- Source: Ms C 5v–7v; 31r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « ce n'est plus un voile pour moi, c'est un mur qui s'élève jusqu'aux cieux et couvre le firmament étoilé »
- Translation: "it is no longer a veil for me; it is a wall that reaches to the heavens and hides the starry sky"
- Other sources: The 1898/1911 edited text keeps much of this passage but softens it (check wording in HA 1911 ch. IX). Her poems of 1896–1897 express joy; she says they do not show her state.
- New to English readers: partial (baseline names "trial of faith" only)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Essential, "not the pious picture" material. Ms A 80v (1891) already records a first doubt "whether there was a Heaven".

### F-OW-214 — Dream of Venerable Anne of Jesus, 10 May 1896
- Claim: Since Easter "the storm roared" in her soul. At dawn on Sunday 10 May 1896 she dreamed she was in a gallery with the prioress; three Carmelites in mantles and big veils came "from Heaven"; the tallest lifted her veil and covered Thérèse with it; she recognised Venerable Anne of Jesus, founder of Carmel in France, whom she had never prayed to. Her face "produced its own light". Thérèse asked: "Will God leave me long on earth? Will he come for me soon?" "Yes, soon, soon... I promise you." "Does God ask anything more of me than my poor little actions and desires? Is he pleased with me?" "God asks nothing else of you. He is pleased, very pleased!" She woke before she could ask graces for her sisters. "I believed, I felt that there is a Heaven."
- When / where: 10 May 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Venerable Anne de Jésus (Ana de Lobera, 1545–1621)
- Source: Ms B 2r–2v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Oui, bientôt, bientôt... Je vous le promets. »
- Translation: "Yes, soon, soon... I promise you."
- Other sources: See M-OW-09.
- New to English readers: yes (not in baseline)
- Confidence: high as her report of a dream.

### F-OW-215 — Second "brother": Fr Adolphe Roulland (May 1896)
- Claim: At the end of May 1896 the prioress (Marie de Gonzague) called her before the refectory, "the first time you called me like that", and asked her to take on "the spiritual interests of a missionary who is to be ordained and leave soon". ### F-OW-216 — 2 Aug 1896: Mère Agnès nearly sent to Saigon
- Claim: "I will never forget 2 August 1896"; that day "there was serious talk of Mère Agnès de Jésus leaving" (for the Carmel of Saigon). Thérèse would not have moved to stop it, but she felt her sister's "so sensitive, so delicate" soul was not made to live among souls who could not understand her; "Jesus was silent". Months later there was talk of Sr Geneviève (Céline) and Sr Marie de la Trinité going; the prioress forbade her novices to think of leaving now.
- When / where: 2 Aug 1896 and following months, Carmel of Lisieux

- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Jamais je n'oublierai le 2 Août 1896 »
- Translation: "I will never forget 2 August 1896."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-217 — Ms B: "My vocation is love"
- Claim: Writing to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur on the sixth anniversary of her profession (8 Sep 1896), she lists her impossible desires: to be warrior, priest, apostle, doctor, martyr; "the courage of a Crusader, of a Papal Zouave"; a priest, yet like Francis of Assisi refusing priesthood; missionary on five continents "from the creation of the world"; every martyrdom: flayed like Bartholomew, boiled like John, "like Joan of Arc, my dear sister, I would like to murmur your name at the stake". Reading 1 Corinthians 12–13 at prayer she found peace: "I understood that the Church had a Heart, and that this Heart was burning with love... My vocation, at last I have found it, my vocation is love!... In the heart of the Church, my Mother, I will be Love."
- When / where: 8 Sep 1896, retreat, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (recipient)
- Source: Ms B 2v–3v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « ma vocation, enfin je l'ai trouvée, ma vocation, c'est l'amour... »
- Translation: "my vocation, I've found it at last: my vocation is love!"
- New to English readers: no for the key phrase (widely quoted); partial for the list of desires
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-218 — Ms B: throwing flowers; the little bird who falls asleep
- Claim: Since she cannot preach or shed blood, she will "throw flowers": let no small sacrifice, look or word escape, doing the smallest things for love. She pictures herself as "a weak little bird covered only with light down", not an eagle, but with "the eyes and heart of an eagle"; it gets distracted, pecks a seed, chases a worm, wets its feathers in a puddle, then comes back to the Sun and "tells in detail its infidelities". When clouds hide the Sun, its little eyes close and it falls asleep, "still believing it is fixing its dear Star". She asks Jesus to choose "a legion of little victims".
- When / where: 8–17 Sep 1896
- People: —
- Source: Ms B 4r–5v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « elle prend une petite graine à droite et à gauche, court après un petit ver... »
- Translation: "it picks up a little seed here and there, runs after a little worm..."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-219 — Ms B's cover letter: "My consolation is to have none"
- Claim: In the covering letter to Marie (Ms B 1r–1v) she writes: "Do not think I am swimming in consolations; oh no! my consolation is to have none on earth." Jesus teaches her "not through books, for I do not understand what I read". She says her "little doctrine" has no exaggeration: "all is calm and at rest". "Jesus does not ask for great deeds, but only surrender and gratitude."
- When / where: Sep 1896 (the cover letter is dated "Septembre 1896"; Ms B 2r is dated 8 Sep 1896)
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur
- Source: Ms B 1r–1v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Ne croyez pas que je nage dans les consolations, oh non ! ma consolation c'est de n'en pas avoir sur la terre. »
- Translation: "Don't think I'm swimming in consolations; oh no! My consolation is to have none on earth."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-220 — Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin's dream of the black door, and Thérèse's confession
- Claim: On 8 Jan 1897 Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin dreamed of a dark room shut by a heavy black door with a line of bright light beneath; a voice called for "Sister Thérèse of the Child Jesus" and insisted "She must be very beautiful!" She told Thérèse, who replied (as recorded by Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin): "How beautiful! It's not a dream, it's a vision, and you had it for me... I don't believe in eternal life; it seems to me that after this mortal life there is nothing more. I can't express the darkness I'm plunged in... the black door is so much the image of what is happening in me." In the infirmary: "Your dream is my only ray of light."
- When / where: Jan 1897 and summer 1897
- People: Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin (recorder); Mère Agnès (summarised the dream in the obituary circular of Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin)
- Source: Dernières paroles à d'autres soeurs, "Thérèse de St-Augustin" n. 1, same URL
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin; later circular by Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Je ne crois pas à la vie éternelle, il me semble qu'après cette vie mortelle il n'y a plus rien. »
- Translation: "I don't believe in eternal life; it seems to me that after this mortal life there's nothing more."
- Other sources: This sister is identified by modern editors as the one "who had the talent of displeasing me" (Ms C 13v); if so, Thérèse confided her darkest words to her. Treat the identification with care.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium (a later account by one sister; strongest wording of the trial found in this family of sources).

### F-OW-221 — "I had a nature that was not easy"
- Claim: To Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin: "Tell me if you have had struggles." "Oh! have I had them! I had a nature that was not easy; it didn't show, but I felt it; I can assure you I haven't been one single day without suffering, not one." "But people say you had none." "Ah! the judgments of creatures. Because they don't see, they don't believe." About the doctor: "Today he says I'm lost, tomorrow that I'm better... all that doesn't touch my soul."
- When / where: 1897
- People: Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin (recorder)
- Source: Dernières paroles à d'autres soeurs, "Thérèse de St-Augustin" nn. 3–4, same URL
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « J'avais une nature pas commode, cela ne paraissait pas mais moi je le sentais bien »
- Translation: "I had a difficult nature; it didn't show, but I felt it."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium.

### F-OW-222 — Carnet jaune begins (6 Apr 1897); health from 3 April
- Claim: The Carmel editors' note: from 3 Apr 1897 she was seriously ill, with daily fever and indigestion and much coughing; she was treated with vesicatories (blistering plasters, very painful, up to twelve hours). On 6 Apr Mère Agnès began to note her sayings on small pieces of paper, later copied into the "Carnet jaune". First saying: "When we are misunderstood and judged unfavourably, what is the use of defending ourselves?... it is so sweet to say nothing."
- When / where: 3–6 Apr 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: cj-avril-1897 editors' note; CJ 6.4.1, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-avril-1897/
- Source type: scholarship (Carmel editors) for health; eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès, Carnet jaune)
- Rights: unknown (note); public-domain (her words); transcription unknown
- Original words: « c'est si doux de ne rien dire, de se laisser juger n'importe comment ! »
- Translation: "it's so sweet to say nothing, to let yourself be judged any old way!"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-223 — How the "last words" were recorded, and how reliable they are
- Claim: The Carmel site publishes an essay by the historian Claude Langlois. His points (reported here in our words): no sister knew shorthand; Mère Agnès first copied each evening what she had heard, and from 8 July 1897 noted sayings through the day, "turning her long presence into a continuous interview", which Thérèse knew (see CJ 18.8.3). Four versions exist, all after her death: two made for the process (1909: 306 sayings; 1910: 275), the Novissima Verba (1927: 362), and the "Carnet jaune" (714 sayings). The Carnet jaune was written out in 1921–1923 from a "Gros carnet noir" of 1904–1905, which was compiled from loose sheets; the black notebook was destroyed and only one loose sheet survives. So there is no way to check the Carnet jaune against its sources. Langlois (Les dernières paroles de Thérèse de Lisieux, 2000) holds that Mère Agnès, "by her own admission", reworked some sayings into "quasi-slogans", but that most sayings came through unrewritten; the "strategic" sayings (her authority to publish the writings; Thérèse's mission after death) are the ones to treat with most care. Céline (Sr Geneviève) began her own notes on 21 July 1897 at Léonie's request, and wrote down only sayings addressed to her.
- When / where: 1897; copies 1904–1905, 1909, 1910, 1921–1923, 1927; critical edition 1971
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus; Sr Geneviève; Claude Langlois; Jean-François Six (critic, 1973)
- Source: C. Langlois, "Les dernières paroles de Thérèse sont-elles de Thérèse ?", https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/oeuvres-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-de-therese/les-dernieres-paroles-de-therese-sont-elles-de-therese/
- Source type: scholarship
- Rights: unknown (modern essay; facts only)
- Original words: « Le carnet jaune a été rédigé en 1921-1923. »
- Translation: "The Yellow Notebook was written in 1921–1923."
- Other sources: Mère Agnès's own "Remarque importante" at the end of September in the Carnet jaune claims Thérèse told her on 16 Jul 1897 "you alone know all the folds of my little soul", so that "later, because of the authority that would be given me, people could trust entirely what I would say and write about her" (cj-septembre-1897, appendix; signed Sr Agnès de Jésus).
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that this is Langlois's view; the underlying question stays open.
- Notes: Every Carnet jaune card in this dossier is "Thérèse as recorded by Mère Agnès, in a copy made 24–26 years later". The biography must say this once, clearly.

### F-OW-224 — "God will breathe you in like a little drop of dew"
- Claim: Mère Agnès asked Thérèse how she herself would die, showing her fears. Thérèse answered with a tender smile: "God will suck you up like a little drop of dew..."
- When / where: 7 Apr 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 7.4, cj-avril-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown

- Translation: "God will draw you up like a little drop of dew."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-225 — Humiliations from sisters; watching the magpies; "I tell the whole truth"
- Claim: She confided "very painful humiliations" given her by some sisters: "I arrange things, even in the storm, to stay at peace inside... while listening, I must be able to look out of the window and enjoy the sight of the sky, the trees... Just now, during my struggle about Sr X., I watched the fine magpies playing in the meadow." As a child she read a story praising a school mistress who told one girl "you're not wrong" and another "you're right"; she thought it was wrong, and still does: "If I'm not loved, too bad! I tell the whole truth; let no one come to me if they don't want to know it." "Kindness must not degenerate into weakness."
- When / where: 18 Apr 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); "Sr X." (unnamed)
- Source: CJ 18.4.1, 18.4.3–4, cj-avril-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Si je ne suis pas aimée, tant pis ! Moi je dis la vérité tout entière »
- Translation: "If I'm not liked, too bad! I tell the whole truth."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.
- Notes: Her firmness as novice guide; counters the "sweet only" image.

### F-OW-226 — May 1897: relieved of all duties; the secret of Roulland
- Claim: Editors: in May she coughed much, had cough syrup, fever again, vesicatories and sessions of "pointes de feu" (cauterisation); she was relieved of all work, stopped going to choir and stopped guiding novices. "It's not 'death' that will come for me, it's God. Death is not a phantom, a horrible spectre as in pictures." Mère Agnès notes Thérèse had been forbidden by Mère Marie de Gonzague to tell her (Agnès) about Fr Roulland for nearly two years; she even secretly borrowed Agnès's burnisher to paint a parchment picture for him, returning it when Agnès was out. Only three months before her death did the prioress free her to speak.
- When / where: May 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); Mère Marie de Gonzague; Fr Adolphe Roulland
- Source: cj-mai-1897 editors' note; CJ 1.5.1–2 and Mère Agnès's note, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-mai-1897/
- Source type: eyewitness record (Mère Agnès); scholarship (Carmel editors)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription and note unknown
- Original words: « La mort, ce n'est pas un fantôme, un spectre horrible, comme on la représente sur les images. »
- Translation: "Death isn't a ghost, a horrible spectre, as they show it in pictures."
- New to English readers: yes (the secrecy and burnisher)
- Confidence: medium-high.
- Notes: Shows the prioress's control and the tension between Mère Marie de Gonzague and Mère Agnès.

### F-OW-227 — "I cough like a locomotive"
- Claim: "I'm coughing! I'm coughing! It's like a railway engine arriving at the station. I'm arriving at a station too: the station of Heaven, and I'm announcing it!" Also: "Our family won't stay long on earth... We were all born crowned." She had sung her own poem "Ma Joie" while dressing.
- When / where: 7 May 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 7.5.1–3, cj-mai-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Ça fait comme la locomotive d'un chemin de fer quand elle arrive à la gare. »
- Translation: "It's like a railway engine pulling into the station."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-228 — "If I'm not surprised enough in Heaven, I'll pretend"
- Claim: "I have such a high idea of Heaven that sometimes I wonder how God will manage to surprise me when I die... If I'm not surprised enough, I'll pretend to be, to please God. There'll be no danger of letting him see my disappointment." God says he will reward "according to works": "I have no works! ...Well! he'll reward me according to his own works." She would like to go to Hanoi "to suffer much... to be all alone... As for being useful there, the thought doesn't even cross my mind." "It's all the same to me to live or die."
- When / where: 15 May 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 15.5.1–2, 15.5.6–7, cj-mai-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « si je ne suis pas assez surprise, je ferai semblant de l'être, pour faire plaisir au bon Dieu »
- Translation: "if I'm not surprised enough, I'll pretend I am, to please God"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-229 — "If I hadn't said anything, no one would think me ill"
- Claim: "I asked God to follow community life until my death, but he doesn't want it! I could go to all the offices, I'm sure, I wouldn't die one minute sooner. It sometimes seems to me that if I had said nothing, no one would find me ill." She asked not to be stopped from saying her "little" Offices of the Dead. "I always need work ready; that way I'm never preoccupied and never waste time." On 19 May: "Nothing gives me 'little' joys like 'little' troubles."
- When / where: 18–19 May 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 18.5.2–4; 19.5, cj-mai-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Il me semble parfois que si je n'avais rien dit, on ne me trouverait pas malade. »
- Translation: "Sometimes I think if I hadn't said anything, no one would think I was ill."
- New to English readers: partial (in Novissima Verba)
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-230 — Her photograph: "the envelope"
- Claim: Shown her photograph: "Yes, but... that's the envelope; when will we see the letter? Oh, how I'd like to see the letter!" On 30 May, when Mère Agnès was hurt not to have been told of the Good Friday 1896 haemoptysis, Thérèse wrote her a note: she had hidden "a little corner of the envelope", never "a single line of the letter"; "it was on Good Friday that Jesus began to tear the envelope of your little letter a little".
- When / where: 20 and 30 May 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)

### F-OW-231 — Why she preferred Théophane Vénard to Aloysius Gonzaga
- Claim: "Théophane Vénard pleases me even more than St Aloysius Gonzaga, because St Aloysius's life is extraordinary and his is quite ordinary. And he speaks himself, whereas for the saint it's another who tells the story and makes him speak." "Vénard loved his family very much, and so do I... I don't understand saints who don't love their family." "St Aloysius was serious even at recreation, but Théophane Vénard was always cheerful." She asked that no wreaths be bought at her death, but the money used "to buy back little Black children". She links her relapse to a novena to Vénard (Nov 1896) made to know whether she should go to Tonkin: "precisely during the novena I began coughing again... He is calling me."
- When / where: 21–27 May 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); Théophane Vénard
- Source: CJ 21/26.5.1, 21/26.5.3; 27.5.10, cj-mai-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Je ne comprends pas les saints qui n'aiment pas leur famille... »
- Translation: "I don't understand saints who don't love their families."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-232 — Expensive remedies offered for sick missionaries; "I'm a coward who wants to deserve the compliments"
- Claim: "I'm convinced remedies are useless to cure me; but I've made an arrangement with God so that poor sick missionaries, who have neither time nor means to look after themselves, benefit from them." "They've told me so often that I have courage, and it's so little true, that I said: well, I mustn't make everyone a liar! And I set about acquiring this courage... like a warrior who, hearing himself praised for bravery, knowing very well he's only a coward, would end by being ashamed of the compliments and want to deserve them." "If I didn't have this trial of soul... I think I would die of joy at the thought of leaving earth soon."
- When / where: 21–26 May 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 21/26.5.5–6, 21/26.5.10, cj-mai-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « tout en sachant très bien qu'il n'est qu'un lâche »
- Translation: "knowing full well he's only a coward"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-233 — The lottery ticket; "they want me!"
- Claim: "I'm like someone with a lottery ticket... I have a ticket, it's my illness, and I can keep good hope!" A little neighbour at Les Buissonnets, aged three, called by other children, said: "Mama! they want me! let me go, please..."; "It seems to me the little angels are calling me today... I don't hear them, but I feel them." She wanted an obituary circular: "it's so sweet to know each other a little, to know a bit whom we'll live with eternally." "I always see the good side of things... If I have only pure suffering... I make it my joy... like Papa's trials, which make me more glorious than a queen."
- When / where: 27 May 1897 (Ascension)
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 27.5.1, 27.5.6, 27.5.8–9, cj-mai-1897

### F-OW-234 — Ms C begun (June 1897), for Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Claim: Ms C opens "June 1897" addressed to Mère Marie de Gonzague, who asked her to finish "singing the mercies of the Lord". "Many sisters think you have spoiled me... it is not so." She thanks her for "not having spared me": the little flower needed "the water of humiliation". Since a year and a half Jesus makes the flower grow by "the sun" of the prioress's smile. "I act with you like a child because you do not act with me as a prioress but as a mother." She writes for obedience, "not to make a literary work", and says the prioress may burn it before her eyes without reading it.
- When / where: June–July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Source: Ms C 1r–2r; 6r; 32v–33r

- Rights: public-domain

### F-OW-235 — The little way: "an elevator"
- Claim: "Compared to the saints, there is between them and me the same difference as between a mountain whose summit is lost in the sky and the obscure grain of sand trodden underfoot." Instead of being discouraged, she decided God would not inspire impossible desires; "to grow bigger is impossible; I must bear with myself as I am". She wanted "a little way, very straight, very short, a completely new little way. We are in a century of inventions... in the houses of the rich an elevator replaces the stairs." "The elevator that must lift me to Heaven is your arms, O Jesus!... I must stay little, and become so more and more."
- When / where: June 1897 (reflecting on a discovery of c. 1894–1895)
- People: —
- Source: Ms C 2v–3r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « l'ascenseur qui doit m'élever jusqu'au Ciel, ce sont vos bras, ô Jésus ! »
- Translation: "the lift that must raise me up to Heaven is your arms, O Jesus!"
- New to English readers: no (the elevator image is in the baseline)
- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-236 — Wish to go to the Carmel of Hanoi "if the Virgin cures me"
- Claim: She has always thought that if Jesus did not take her soon, she would be sent like Noah's dove "to infidel shores". The prioress told her she had the special vocation for foreign Carmels and only her health prevented it. "If the Blessed Virgin cures me" she still desires to leave for a foreign land: "here I am loved, by you and all the sisters... That is why I dream of a monastery where I would be unknown, where I would suffer poverty, lack of affection, in short the exile of the heart." She admits: "I know my incapacity... having no knowledge of earthly things."
- When / where: June 1897
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Source: Ms C 9r–10v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain

- Translation: "I dream of a monastery where I'd be unknown, where I'd have to suffer poverty, lack of affection, in a word the exile of the heart."
- Other sources: The Hanoi Carmel is named in her letters of 1896–1897 (see letters cards), not here.

### F-OW-237 — 4 June 1897: farewell to her three sisters; "don't be surprised if I don't appear"
- Claim: Editors: Ms C begun 4 Jun 1897 after Mère Agnès discovered the 1896 haemoptysis; early June: side pain, vomiting, anguish, loss of appetite; 6 Jun "the famous snail syrup" and a milk diet; improvement mid-June after a community novena to Notre-Dame des Victoires (5–13 Jun); fever and side pain back 25 Jun. On 4 Jun, lying on Céline's straw mattress in her cell, she said goodbye to her sisters: "Don't be surprised if I don't appear to you after my death and you see nothing extraordinary as a sign of my happiness... it's 'my little way' to want to see nothing." "Don't be upset if I suffer a lot and you see in me no sign of happiness at the moment of death. Our Lord died a Victim of Love, and see what his agony was!"
- When / where: 4 Jun 1897, Sr Geneviève's cell
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Sr Geneviève

- Source type: eyewitness record (Mère Agnès); scholarship (Carmel editors)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription and note unknown
- Original words: « Ne vous étonnez pas si je ne vous apparais pas après ma mort »
- Translation: "Don't be surprised if I don't appear to you after I die."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-238 — Writing Ms C in the garden: haymakers and interruptions
- Claim: Writing in the garden in June 1897, she is constantly interrupted: "a good sister passes by with a pitchfork on her shoulder" and chats about "hay, ducks, hens, the doctor's visit"; another haymaker drops flowers on her knees "perhaps thinking to inspire poetic ideas". Tired of opening and closing "this famous notebook", she opens a book (which will not stay open) and says she is copying Psalms for the prioress's feast. A haymaker: "My poor little sister, it must tire you to write like that all day." "Don't worry, I seem to write a lot but really I write almost nothing." "Good!" said she, "but all the same, I'm glad we're haymaking, it always distracts you a little." The infirmarians "do not fear to take two thousand steps where twenty would do".
- When / where: June 1897, garden of the Carmel of Lisieux
- People: unnamed lay sisters (haymakers); the infirmarians
- Source: Ms C 17r–17v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Ma pauvr'ptite soeur, ça doit vous fatiguer d'écrire comme ça toute la journée. »
- Translation: "My poor little sister, it must wear you out, writing like that all day long."
- New to English readers: partial (in Taylor)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She records the lay sisters' country speech. A strong comic scene near her death.

### F-OW-239 — The prioress's care of her illness
- Claim: "Since I have been ill, the care you lavish on me has taught me much about charity. No remedy seems too expensive to you, and if it does not work you tirelessly try something else. When I went to recreation, how careful you were that I be well placed, sheltered from draughts!"
- When / where: 1896–1897
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Source: Ms C 27v

### F-OW-240 — Snail syrup and raw eggs; Fr Youf on her temptations
- Claim: They hid from her that her syrup was snail syrup; she noticed and laughed: "What does it matter to me to take snail syrup, as long as I don't see the horns! Now I eat snails like the little ducks! Yesterday I did like the ostriches, I ate raw eggs!" The chaplain Youf told her about her temptations against faith: "Don't dwell on that, it's very dangerous." "Not very comforting to hear." "Are you resigned to die?" "Ah, Father, I find one needs resignation only to live. To die, it's joy I feel." When Mère Agnès said she would photograph her "to please Our Mother", she quoted her father's Auvergnat story: "Little north wind, stop blowing! It's not for me, it's for my comrade who has no jacket..."
- When / where: 6 Jun 1897
- People: Abbé Youf; Mère Agnès (recorder); Louis Martin (his story)
- Source: CJ 6.6.2, 6.6.5–6, cj-juin-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « pourvu que je ne voie pas les cornes ! »
- Translation: "as long as I don't see the horns!"
- New to English readers: yes (snail syrup, Youf's warning)
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-241 — The white hen and her chicks (7 June 1897)
- Claim: Sitting in the garden by the cemetery she laid her head on Mère Agnès's heart and sang "Me forget you, dear Mother, no, never!" Going down the steps she saw under the medlar tree the little white hen with all her chicks under her wings; she stood looking, eyes full of tears, and could not say why. That evening: "I cried thinking that God chose this comparison to make us believe in his tenderness. All my life that's what he has done for me! He has hidden me entirely under his wings!" It was ten years to the day since her father gave her the little white flower.
- When / where: 7 Jun 1897, garden of the Carmel
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 7.6.1–3, cj-juin-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Il m'a entièrement cachée sous ses ailes !... »
- Translation: "He has hidden me completely under his wings!"
- Other sources: Ms A 50v dates the flower to Pentecost 29 May 1887 (Pentecost 1897 was 6 Jun; the anniversary is liturgical).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-242 — "The Thief"; "a shower of roses" (9 June 1897)
- Claim: "It says in the Gospel that God will come like a thief. He will come and steal me very nicely. Oh, how I'd like to help the Thief!" "My trial?" "Not passed, but it's as if suspended. The nasty snakes aren't hissing in my ears any more." To Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, "How sad we'll be when you leave us!": "Oh no, you'll see, it will be like a shower of roses." "I'm like a little child on the railway waiting for his papa and mama to put him on the train. Alas, they don't come and the train leaves! But there are others..."
- When / where: 9 Jun 1897
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 9.6.1–5, cj-juin-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Oh ! non, vous verrez, ce sera comme une pluie de roses. »
- Translation: "Oh no, you'll see, it'll be like a shower of roses."
- Other sources: See the card on the origin of the shower of roses (Langlois; Marie's deposition on the St Aloysius reading).
- New to English readers: no (the phrase); partial (context)
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-243 — Origin of "a shower of roses" and "spend my heaven doing good"
- Claim: According to Langlois (Carmel site), the two process versions of the sayings date the "shower of roses" to 9 Jun 1897: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur said, "We will be very sad when you die", and Thérèse answered, "Oh no, you'll see, it will be like a shower of roses." In her deposition Marie explained she had read in the refectory a story from the Life of St Aloysius Gonzaga in which a sick man saw roses fall on his bed as a sign of healing; Thérèse then said at recreation: "Me too, after my death I will make roses rain." The other saying, "I want to spend my heaven doing good on earth", was said separately on 17 Jul 1897. The first Histoire d'une âme (1898, ch. XII) printed "After my death, she had told us gracefully, I will make a shower of roses fall"; the phrase vanished from the 1899–1900 editions and returned in 1901, paired with "I want to spend my heaven doing good on earth", which had been written on the wooden cross of her grave. At the process the witnesses cite "spend my heaven" about twenty times and mostly ignore the roses. Céline drew "Thérèse covering her crucifix with roses" in 1912.
- When / where: 9 Jun and 17 Jul 1897; editions 1898–1901; drawing 1912
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Mère Agnès; Sr Geneviève; Mgr de Teil (vice-postulator)
- Source: Langlois essay (URL above), section on the "pluie de roses"
- Source type: scholarship (reporting the process texts and her sisters' depositions)
- Rights: unknown (facts only)
- Original words: « Moi aussi – après ma mort, je ferai pleuvoir des roses »
- Translation: "Me too: after my death I'll make it rain roses."
- Other sources: The Carnet jaune entries for 9 Jun and 17 Jul are in the April–July part of this dossier (F-OW cards from out_cj1).
- New to English readers: yes (the origin in the Aloysius reading and the textual history)
- Confidence: medium-high; secondhand via a modern historian quoting the process.
- Notes: The famous saying is recorded by her sisters, not written by her.

### F-OW-244 — "Writing my little life is like fishing with a line"
- Claim: "To write my 'little' life I don't rack my brains; it's like fishing with a line: I write what comes to the end." She threw flowers to the statue of St Joseph "to please him. I don't want to give in order to receive." (15 Jun) "I'm happy, I don't offend God at all during my illness. Just now I was writing on charity and I was often disturbed; so I tried not to lose patience, to practise what I was writing." (25 Jun) During a sermon: "I played truant... I consider my notebook as my little homework."
- When / where: 11–25 Jun 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 11.6.1–2; 15.6.5; 25.6.2, cj-juin-1897

- Other sources: Ms C 17r–17v (interruptions).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-245 — Why she expected to die in 1897: the priesthood she could not have received
- Claim: A sister (in Céline's "Varia 4") recorded that early in 1897, "well before she was ill", Thérèse expected to die that year; in June she gave the reason: "God is going to take me at an age when I would not have had time to be a priest if I could have been a priest; it's at this June ordination that I would have received holy Orders. Well, so that I regret nothing, God lets me be ill, so I could not have gone, and I would die before exercising my ministry."
- When / where: June 1897
- People: unnamed recorder (Varia in Céline's collection)

- New to English readers: yes

### F-OW-246 — "No one believes I'm ill"; shy with her uncle
- Claim: "My soul is exiled, Heaven is closed to me, and on the earth's side it's trial too... I can see people don't believe I'm ill, but God allows that." (12 Jun) "People don't think I'm as ill as I am. It's harder then to be deprived of Communion, of the Office." (30 Jun) After a parlour with her uncle, where as usual she said almost nothing: "How shy I was with my uncle! Coming back I scolded a novice a lot, I didn't recognise myself. What contrasts there are in my character! My shyness comes from an extreme embarrassment I feel when people pay attention to me." She preferred saints "who fear nothing, like St Cecilia" to St Simeon Stylites.
- When / where: 12–30 Jun 1897
- People: Isidore Guérin; a novice; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 12.6.1; 29.6.3; 30.6.1–2, cj-juin-1897

- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown

- Translation: "What contrasts there are in my character!"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high.

- Claim: Ms C ends on folio 37r with "par la confiance et l'amour..." ("by confidence and love"): "even if I had on my conscience all the sins that can be committed, I would go, my heart broken with repentance, and throw myself into Jesus' arms". It is the last page of the autobiographical manuscripts.

### F-OW-248 — A capacity for suffering, a small one for joy
- Claim: Recorded in Céline's "Varia 3": "I had a very great capacity for suffering and a very small one for enjoying; I couldn't bear joy. Joy, for example, took away all my appetite, while on days I had much sorrow I ate like four. It was the opposite of everyone else." Also: "How sorry I'd be to have read all those books!" in front of a bookcase: "I'd have broken my head, I'd have lost precious time I could have used simply to love God." And: "I'm counting on not staying idle in Heaven; my desire is to work for the Church and souls" (from a letter).

- New to English readers: yes

### F-OW-249 — Last visit to the Blessed Sacrament; walking along the wall
- Claim: On 2 Jul 1897 she went for the last time to the Blessed Sacrament in the oratory, "at the end of her strength". Mère Agnès recalls that that morning, when the community went to the oratory, "no one thought to support her. She walked very slowly along the wall. I didn't dare offer her my arm."
- When / where: 2 Jul 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 2.7, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/

### F-OW-250 — The doctor who regretted not doing an autopsy; "maternalised milk"
- Claim: A friend of the Carmel had died of a tumour; Dr de Cornière said in front of Thérèse, "What a pity I couldn't do the autopsy!" Thérèse: "Ah! that's how people are indifferent to each other on earth! Would he say that about a mother or a sister?" When the doctor prescribed "lait maternisé" (condensed milk from the chemist) she cried hot tears for various reasons. "I need food for my soul; read me a saint's life... not to distract me, but for examples of humility." "Even the saints abandon me! I asked St Anthony at Matins to find our lost handkerchief. He didn't bother! But I told him I loved him all the same."
- When / where: 3 Jul 1897
- People: Dr de Cornière; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 3.7.1, 3.7.4, 3.7.6, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « C'est comme cela qu'on est indifférent les uns pour les autres sur la terre ! »
- Translation: "That's how indifferent people are to one another on earth!"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high.
- Notes: Editors' July note: haemoptysis almost daily 6 Jul–5 Aug; 7 Jul suffocation, high fever; "gentle remedies": ice, pancreatin, mustard poultices, cupping; right lung "wound"; 8 Jul moved to the infirmary; stomach, head, heart pain, diarrhoea; lost unless a miracle; 30 Jul extreme unction and viaticum from Canon Maupas.

### F-OW-251 — On Fr Pichon and Fr Alexis; "dying of love is not dying in transports"
- Claim: "I'm very grateful to Fr Alexis; he did me much good. Fr Pichon treated me too much like a child; yet he did me good too by telling me I had not committed mortal sin." "Our Lord died on the Cross in anguish, and yet that's the most beautiful death of love... Dying of love is not dying in transports. I confess frankly, it seems to me that's what I'm experiencing."
- When / where: 4 Jul 1897
- People: Fr Alexis Prou OFM; Fr Almire Pichon SJ; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 4.7.2, 4.7.4, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Le P. Pichon me traitait trop comme une enfant »
- Translation: "Father Pichon treated me too much like a child."
- New to English readers: yes (the verdict on Pichon)
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-252 — The wound of love after her Offering (June 1895), told on 7 July 1897
- Claim: Asked to tell again what happened after her Offering to Love ("I told you the same day, but you paid no attention"): "I was beginning my Way of the Cross, and suddenly I was seized by such a violent love for God that I can only explain it by saying it was as if I'd been plunged entirely into fire. Oh, what fire and what sweetness at the same time! I was burning with love and felt that one minute, one second more, I could not have borne this ardour without dying... I experienced it only once and only for an instant, then fell back into my usual dryness." "Since 14 I had assaults of love too... but it was not a real flame."
- When / where: 7 Jul 1897 (event: a few days after 9 Jun 1895)
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 7.7.2, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « c'était comme si on m'avait plongée tout entière dans le feu »
- Translation: "it was as if I had been plunged entirely into fire"
- Other sources: See M-OW list. Mère Agnès admits she "seemed to attach no importance" to it in 1895.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-253 — "Not my style": she refuses to put on edifying words for the doctor
- Claim: Mère Agnès asked her to say a few edifying, amiable words to Dr de Cornière. "Ah, little Mother, that's not my little style... Let M. de Cornière think what he likes. I love only simplicity; I have a horror of 'pretence'." Of the "gentle remedies": "I suffer, but not enough to scream." Then, slyly: "Yet He sent us trials 'to scream'... and we didn't 'scream' all the same" (of their father's illness). "I'm like a poor 'little grey wolf' who wants to go back to his forest" (her father's nickname for her, "mon petit loup gris").
- When / where: 7 Jul 1897
- People: Dr de Cornière; Mère Agnès (recorder); Louis Martin (recalled)
- Source: CJ 7.7.4–7, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Je n'aime que la simplicité, j'ai horreur de la «feintise». »
- Translation: "I love only simplicity; I loathe pretending."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-254 — 8 July 1897: carried to the infirmary; eau de Cologne; "a Gennin box, not a beer"
- Claim: So ill that extreme unction was discussed, she was carried down to the infirmary: "I think I'm dreaming!... At last they're not mad" (Youf and de Cornière). Examining her senses before the sacrament, for smell she recalled: "on my last journey from Alençon to Lisieux I used a bottle of eau de Cologne Mme Tifenne had given me, and with pleasure." Looking at her thin hands: "It's getting to be a skeleton already, that's what 'suits' me." "I'd rather be reduced to dust than preserved like St Catherine of Bologna." "I'd like to be put in a little Gennin box, not in the 'bière'" (pun: bière = coffin/beer; Gennin sent artificial flowers in long boxes). She warned that many young priests would ask for Carmelite "sisters" and that such correspondence could be "false money to buy souls".
- When / where: 8 Jul 1897
- People: Abbé Youf; Dr de Cornière; Mme Tifenne (Alençon friend); Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 8.7.1–2, 8.7.6, 8.7.8, 8.7.16–17, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Je voudrais être mise dans une petite boîte à Gennin, pas dans la bière. »
- Translation: "I'd like to be put in a little Gennin box, not in a 'bier'." (pun on bière, coffin/beer)
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-255 — "Eat 'dates' as much as you like"; the wounded mouse
- Claim: Told she would perhaps die on 16 Jul or 6 Aug: "Eat as many 'dattes' [dates/fruit] as you like; I don't want any more... I've been caught out too often by dates." She wanted the Guérins to "have a party" at La Musse, not be sad. When a mouse was caught in the infirmary she made up a whole story, asking for the wounded mouse to be laid beside her and examined by the doctor, to make the sisters laugh. "Holy Poverty! How funny, a saint who won't go to Heaven!"
- When / where: 9 Jul 1897
- People: the Guérin family; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 9.7.1, 9.7.5, 9.7.7, 9.7.9, cj-juillet-1897

- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown

- Translation: "I've been fooled by dates too often."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-256 — "I have lived so little"; if she had committed every crime
- Claim: "Alas, how little I have lived! Life always seemed very short to me. My childhood days, it seems they were yesterday." "People might think it's because I haven't sinned that I have such great confidence in God. Tell them clearly, Mother, that if I had committed every possible crime I would still have the same confidence." She recited verses of "La jeune poitrinaire" ("my days are condemned... when you see the dead leaves fall... pray to God for me"). She recalled a grace in "the grotto of St Mary Magdalene" in July 1889: for a week "it was as if a veil were thrown over all earthly things... I did things as if not doing them; it was as if I'd been lent a body" (while in charge of the refectory).
- When / where: 11 Jul 1897 (recalling July 1889)
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 11.7.2, 11.7.5–7, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « si j'avais commis tous les crimes possibles, j'aurais toujours la même confiance »
- Translation: "if I had committed every crime there is, I would have the same confidence"
- Other sources: Ms C 36v–37r ends with the same thought.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-257 — Her jobs in the Carmel, in her own list (told 13 July 1897)
- Claim: On entering: the linen room with the subprioress Sr Marie des Anges, plus sweeping the stairs and dormitory; asking for mortifications in the refectory cost her; she pulled weeds at 4:30, which displeased the prioress. After clothing (Jan 1889): the refectory until 18 (sweeping, putting out water and beer). Forty Hours 1891: sacristy with Sr Saint-Stanislas. From June 1892: two months without office (painted the angels of the oratory; tierce for the bursar). Then the turn (portress) with Sr Saint-Raphaël, keeping painting, until the elections of 1896, when she asked to help Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph in the linen room. People thought her slow and not devoted; Mère Agnès scolded her for a refectory tablecloth left unmended, "and I was wrong, it was time she lacked"; Thérèse cried without excusing herself. She hated spiders but cleaned the cobwebs from the dark hole of St Alexis under the stairs. Also (12 Jul): a violent inner struggle "with thoughts against authority" when told to prepare a night-light for Mère Marie de Gonzague's family, lodging unexpectedly with the turn sisters; she pretended it was for the Virgin and Child and made it spotless.
- When / where: 1888–1896, told 12–13 Jul 1897
- People: Sr Marie des Anges; Sr Saint-Stanislas; Sr Saint-Raphaël; Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 12.7.1; 13.7.18, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « il me venait de telles pensées contre l'autorité »
- Translation: "such thoughts against authority came to me"
- New to English readers: yes (her own job list and the night-light struggle)
- Confidence: medium-high.
- Notes: Central for daily life in Carmel.

### F-OW-258 — Céline's notes: "I will come and fetch you" and "Mademoiselle Lili"
- Claim: Sr Geneviève recorded that on 12 Jul 1897 Thérèse said Céline would feel her departure most, "because as soon as she has trouble she comes to me and she'll have no one... but God will give her strength... and then, I'll come back!" "I'll come and fetch you as soon as possible and I'll get Papa in on it, you know he was always in a hurry..." In June, pointing her finger at the Child Jesus as if giving a lesson: "My little Jesus, if you take me, you'll have to take Mademoiselle Lili too. That's my condition... no middle way, take it or leave it!" (M. Toto and Mlle Lili were names from a children's story; she was M. Toto). When Céline said she would go mad losing her: "If you go mad, bobonne, the 'Bon Sauveur' will come and get you!" (the asylum where their father had been).
- When / where: June–July 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Geneviève (recorder); Louis Martin (recalled)
- Source: Dernières paroles à Céline, 12 Juillet and Juillet nn. 2–3, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/oeuvres-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-a-celine/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Sr Geneviève)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Si vous êtes folle, bobonne, le "Bon Sauveur" viendra vous chercher !.. »
- Translation: "If you go mad, nursie, the 'Bon Sauveur' will come and fetch you!"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium (Céline's notes, reworked later; Langlois quotes them as authentic in substance).
- Notes: A joke about her father's asylum: shows how the family spoke of it between themselves.

### F-OW-259 — "The Thief": Jesus who steals the grape
- Claim: Recorded in Varia 3 (12 Jul 1897): "God has gardens he entrusts to owners, and usually he never picks fruit without their permission; but God wants a little bunch of grapes so much that the owner won't give him that he's going to steal it." Note: the prioress could not bring herself to give Thérèse "permission" to die; from then on Thérèse called Jesus "the Thief". Marie du Sacré-Cœur recorded (8–9 Jul): "The 'Thief' is far away, he's gone to steal other children!" "On 9 June I saw the Thief." "They've put me in a 'bed of misfortune', a bed that makes you miss the train" (Mère Geneviève had received extreme unction three times in the same bed). After the doctor found her better: "The 'Thief' has gone again!"
- When / where: 8–12 Jul 1897
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague (the "owner"); Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (recorder)
- Source: Dernières paroles à Céline, Varia 3 n. 13; Dernières Paroles à Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 8–9 juillet, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/oeuvres-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-marie-du-sacre-coeur/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (unnamed sister; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « on m'a mise «dans un lit de malheur», dans un lit qui fait manquer le train »
- Translation: "they've put me in an 'unlucky bed', a bed that makes you miss the train"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-260 — Plans after death: baptising children, helping priests; "no roast larks"
- Claim: "Don't think that when I'm in Heaven I'll drop roast larks into your beaks... That's not what I had or wanted. You may have great trials, but I'll send you lights to value and love them." "Don't imagine I feel a lively joy at dying, like the joy I used to feel going for a month to Trouville or Alençon... Only one expectation makes my heart beat: the love I'll receive and that I'll be able to give. And I think of all the good I'd like to do after my death: have little children baptised, help priests, missionaries, the whole Church... but first comfort my little sisters." "God will have to do all my wishes in Heaven, because I never did my own will on earth." "Since my childhood God gave me the deep feeling that I would die young." Instead of wreaths: "buy three little heathen babies [to baptise]: a little Marie-Louis-Martin, a little Marie-Théophane, a little girl Marie-Cécile... and a little Marie-Thérèse."
- When / where: 13 Jul 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 13.7.2, 13.7.11, 13.7.13, 13.7.16–17, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Ne croyez pas que lorsque je serai au Ciel je vous ferai tomber des alouettes rôties dans le bec... »
- Translation: "Don't think that when I'm in Heaven I'll drop roast larks into your mouths."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium (Langlois: sayings about her mission after death are the ones most open to reworking).
- Notes: The famous "I want to spend my heaven doing good on earth" is dated 17 Jul (not yet carded; see HANDOFF).

### F-OW-261 — Plans for Heaven: "to help the missionaries"
- Claim: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur recorded (13 Jul 1897): "If you knew what plans I'm making, what things I'll do when I'm in Heaven... I'll begin my mission..." "What plans?" "Plans to come back with my little sisters, and go over there to help the missionaries, and then stop the little heathens from dying before being baptised." (12 Jul) "If you had to begin your life again, how would you do it?" "I'd do as I did." (16 Jul) She would choose to die at 80 with less glory if it gave God more pleasure: "I work for his sole pleasure, for his whims."
- When / where: 12–16 Jul 1897
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (recorder)
- Source: Dernières Paroles à Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 12, 13 and 16 juillet, same URL
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Je commencerai ma mission... »
- Translation: "I'll begin my mission..."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-262 — The half-dead lamp that lit all the candles
- Claim: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie had no match to light the candles for a procession; she lit her candle from the little lamp before the relics, almost out, and from hers all the community's candles were lit. "It's the same with the Communion of Saints... How many times have I thought I might owe all the graces I've received to the prayers of a soul who asked God for me and whom I'll know only in Heaven." "In Heaven one won't meet indifferent looks."
- When / where: 15 Jul 1897
- People: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 15.7.5, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Au Ciel on ne rencontrera pas de regards indifférents »
- Translation: "In Heaven you won't meet any indifferent looks."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high. Mère Agnès notes: "the conversation was too long, I could not take everything, nor word for word."

### F-OW-263 — A saying scratched out by Mère Agnès (16 July 1897)
- Claim: With "the air of a child with a nice trick in mind": "I'd like to give you a proof of love no one has ever given you..." The next words were scratched out by Mère Agnès ("Mère Agnès a gratté la suite !", Carmel editors' note). On the same day Thérèse said "You alone know all the folds of my little soul...", which Mère Agnès later used to claim authority over what would be written about her.
- When / where: 16 Jul 1897
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 16.7.4–5, cj-juillet-1897; "Remarque importante" at the end of cj-septembre-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record (Mère Agnès); scholarship (Carmel editors' note on the erasure)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription and note unknown
- Original words: « Je voudrais vous donner un témoignage d'amour que personne ne vous ait jamais donné... »
- Translation: "I'd like to give you a sign of love that no one has ever given you."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that the text was erased; its content is unknown.
- Notes: Direct evidence of Mère Agnès editing her own record.

### F-OW-264 — "I'll be on your tail": to cousin Marie Guérin
- Claim: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (her cousin Marie Guérin) recorded (18 Jul 1897): "Oh, when I'm in Heaven I'll do many things, great things... And also, when I'm up there, it's I who'll follow you closely!..." When Marie said she might frighten her: "Does your guardian angel frighten you? He follows you all the time; well, I'll follow you the same way, and closely too! I won't let anything pass!" (2 Aug) "I find no natural pleasure in being loved, pampered, but a very great one in being humiliated." (Sep) To Marie, who promised to become a saint once Thérèse was gone: "Don't wait for that... Believe me, never wait till tomorrow to start becoming a saint."
- When / where: Jul–Sep 1897
- People: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (recorder)
- Source: Dernières paroles à d'autres soeurs, "Marie de l'Eucharistie", https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/oeuvres-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-a-dautres-soeurs/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « c'est moi qui vous filerai de près !... »
- Translation: "it's me who'll be right on your heels!"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium.

### F-OW-265 — "Love, to be loved, and to come back to earth"
- Claim: Céline, writing to her aunt Mme Guérin on 22 Jul 1897: while she read to Thérèse by the window about the happiness of Heaven, Thérèse interrupted: "That's not what draws me..." "What then?" "Oh! it's Love! To love, to be loved, and to come back to earth." Also "In Heaven you'll sit next to me!" (21 Jul) and she would fasten Céline's "azure wings" herself, "because you'd put them on too low or too high!"
- When / where: 21–22 Jul 1897
- People: Sr Geneviève (recorder); Mme Céline Guérin (letter recipient)
- Source: Dernières paroles à Céline, Juillet n. 4 and 21 Juillet, same URL
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Sr Geneviève)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Oh! c'est l'Amour ! Aimer, être aimée et revenir sur la terre. »
- Translation: "Oh, it's Love! To love, to be loved, and to come back to earth."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high (dated letter of 22 Jul 1897).

### F-OW-266 — "I won't write any more now!" and the warning about the manuscript
- Claim: On 1 Aug 1897 during Matins, about "the manuscript of her life", Thérèse told Mère Agnès: "After my death you must speak to no one about my manuscript before it is published; you must speak of it only to Our Mother. If you do otherwise, the devil will set you more than one trap to spoil God's work... a very important work!" She also said: "I won't write any more now!"
- When / where: 1 Aug 1897, infirmary, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (recorder); Mère Marie de Gonzague ("Our Mother")
- Source: CJ 1.8.2 and 1.8.3, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-aout-1897/
