### F-OW-329 — 29 Sep: "I'll never know how to die!"
- Claim: From the morning she seemed in agony, with a painful rattle; the community said the prayers of the Manual around her bed for about an hour. At noon to the prioress: "Mother, is this the agony?... How am I to die? I'll never know how to die!..." Read the prayers for the dying, at the mention of demons she made a childlike threatening gesture and said, smiling, "Oh! Oh!" After the doctor: "Is it today, Mother?" "Yes, my little girl." An insect got into her sleeve: "Leave it, it doesn't matter... I know these little creatures." "Oh Mother, how the nerves hurt!" Late at night, hands joined: "Yes, my God, yes my God, I accept everything!" "Not atrocious, Mother, but much, much... just what I can bear." She asked to be alone at night; the prioress refused; Marie and Céline shared the night watch.
- When / where: 29 Sep 1897, infirmary
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès (recorder); Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Sr Geneviève; Sr Marie de la Trinité (sent away gently)
- Source: CJ 29.9.1–11, cj-septembre-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès, Carnet jaune)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Comment fais-je faire pour mourir ? Jamais je ne vais savoir mourir !... »
- Translation: "How am I going to die? I'll never know how to die!"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high.

### F-OW-330 — 30 Sep 1897, day: "pure agony without any consolation"
- Claim: In the morning, during Mass, alone with Mère Agnès, looking at the statue: "Oh! I prayed to her with such fervour! But it is pure agony, without any mixture of consolation." In the afternoon she could sit up: "See what strength I have today! No, I'm not going to die! I have months more, perhaps years!" Asked if she would accept it: "I'd have to..." then, falling back: "I accept it!" Mère Agnès wrote down exclamations "impossible to render the tone of": "I don't believe in death for me any more... I believe only in suffering... Well, so much the better!" "If this is the agony, what is death?!" "Oh Mother, I assure you the chalice is full to the brim!" "But God won't abandon me... He has never abandoned me." Given a picture of Our Lady of Mount Carmel: "Mother, present me quickly to the Blessed Virgin; I'm a baby who can't go on!... Prepare me to die well." Told her humility was her preparation: "Yes, it seems to me I have never sought anything but the truth; yes, I have understood humility of heart... It seems to me I am humble." "All I wrote about my desires for suffering, oh! it's true all the same!... And I don't repent of having given myself to Love." "I would never have believed it was possible to suffer so much! never! never! I can only explain it by the burning desires I have had to save souls."
- When / where: 30 Sep 1897, infirmary, from morning to about 5 p.m.
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Source: CJ 30.9, cj-septembre-1897; also Sr Geneviève's "Dernières paroles de notre chère petite Thérèse, 30 Septembre", dernieres-paroles-a-celine
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès; Sr Geneviève)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Si c'est ça l'agonie, qu'est-ce que c'est que la mort ? !... »
- Translation: "If this is dying, what is death?!"
- Other sources: Céline's list gives "the vase is full to the brim" (« le vase est plein jusqu'au bord ») where Mère Agnès has « le calice ». Céline also records: "I can't go on... and yet I must last... I am... I am reduced..." (« Je suis... je suis réduite... »), absent from Mère Agnès's version. Novissima Verba (English) has "the chalice is full to overflowing".
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high (two recorders agree in substance).

### F-OW-331 — 30 Sep 1897, 3 p.m.: arms on her sisters' shoulders "like a cross"
- Claim: Sr Geneviève recorded that in the afternoon, with only Mère Agnès and herself present, Thérèse, trembling, called them to help her; suffering in all her muscles, she put one arm on Mère Agnès's shoulder and the other on Céline's, and stayed "with her arms in a cross". "At that very moment 3 o'clock struck", and they thought of Jesus on the cross.
- When / where: 30 Sep 1897, about 3 p.m., infirmary
- People: Sr Geneviève (recorder); Mère Agnès
- Source: Dernières paroles à Céline, 30 Septembre, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/oeuvres-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-a-celine/
- Source type: eyewitness record (Sr Geneviève)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « elle resta ainsi les bras en Croix. A ce moment-là, même, 3 heures sonnèrent »
- Translation: "she stayed like that with her arms out in a cross. At that very moment the clock struck three."
- New to English readers: yes (not in Mère Agnès's Carnet jaune entry; check Novissima Verba)
- Confidence: medium (Céline's later written account).

### F-OW-332 — 30 Sep 1897, about 5–7 p.m.: the last agony
- Claim: About 5 p.m. her face changed; Mère Agnès understood it was the last agony. The community came in; she greeted them with a smile, holding and constantly looking at her crucifix. For more than two hours "a terrible rattle tore her chest"; her face was congested, hands purple, feet icy; she trembled in every limb; heavy sweat ran down her face; she gave small involuntary cries to breathe. Robins and other birds sang loudly outside the window for a long time; Mère Agnès prayed they would stop. Céline put a piece of ice to her lips; she smiled at her "as a supreme farewell". At the 6 o'clock Angelus she looked long at the statue of the Virgin. Around 5 p.m. Mère Marie de Gonzague knocked down the relics of Vénard and Anne of Jesus pinned to the curtain; they were picked up and she caressed them.
- When / where: 30 Sep 1897, 5–7 p.m.
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); Sr Geneviève; Mère Marie de Gonzague; the community
- Source: CJ 30.9 and its appendix (Paroles retrouvées), cj-septembre-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Pendant plus de deux heures, un râle terrible déchira sa poitrine. »
- Translation: "For more than two hours a terrible rattle tore at her chest."
- Other sources: Céline says Thérèse had promised her last look to the prioress "if God leaves me free", but in fact looked at Céline "with a prophetic insistence" a few minutes before death, then lowered her eyes to seek the prioress (Dernières paroles à Céline, 30 Sep).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for the main facts (several witnesses); the "last look" is Céline's interpretation.

### F-OW-333 — Her last words, 30 Sep 1897, a little after 7 p.m.
- Claim: After the prioress sent the community away (7 p.m. and some minutes), Thérèse sighed: "Mother! Isn't this the agony yet?... Am I not going to die?..." — "Yes, my poor little one, it's the agony, but God perhaps wants to prolong it a few hours." She answered with courage: "Well!... come on!... Come on!... Oh! I would not want to suffer less long..." And looking at her crucifix: "Oh! I love him!... My God... I love you....." Then she fell gently back, head leaning to the right. Mère Agnès's text gives « Oh ! je l'aime !...... Mon Dieu... je vous aime..... ». Sr Geneviève's list gives « Eh bien, allons ! allons ! oh ! je ne voudrais pas moins souffrir !.... Oh ! je l'aime.... « Mon Dieu... je... vous aime ! » ».
- When / where: 30 Sep 1897, a little after 7 p.m., infirmary, Carmel of Lisieux (the Carmel editors give 7:20 p.m.)
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès (recorder); Sr Geneviève (recorder)
- Source: CJ 30.9 (cj-septembre-1897); Dernières paroles à Céline, "Dernières Paroles de notre chère petite Thérèse, 30 Septembre, Le Soir"; Carmel editors' health note (19h20)
- Source type: eyewitness record (Mère Agnès; Sr Geneviève); scholarship (Carmel editors) for the time
- Rights: public-domain; transcription and note: unknown
- Original words: « Oh ! je l'aime !...... Mon Dieu... je vous aime..... »
- Translation: "Oh! I love him!... My God... I love you..."
- Other sources: Baseline: "My God, I love you!" The two recorders agree; Céline's version marks a pause: "My God... I... love you!"
- New to English readers: no (the words); partial (the preceding exchange and "I would not want to suffer less long")
- Confidence: high (two independent recorders in the community).

### F-OW-334 — After death: the "ecstasy", the smile, the body "no more than 12 or 13"
- Claim: The prioress rang the infirmary bell to call back the community, saying "Open all the doors." The sisters knelt and, per Mère Agnès, saw "the ecstasy": her face regained its lily colour, her eyes were fixed upward "shining with peace and joy"; she made "certain beautiful movements of the head, as if Someone had wounded her divinely with an arrow of love". Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie brought a candle close: no movement of the eyelids. It lasted "about the space of a Creed", and she breathed her last. Afterwards she kept "a heavenly smile"; she held the crucifix so tightly it had to be pulled from her hands. Marie, Agnès and Sr Aimée de Jésus laid her out and noted she looked no more than 12 or 13. Her limbs stayed supple until burial on Monday 4 Oct 1897.
- When / where: 30 Sep – 4 Oct 1897
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Sr Aimée de Jésus; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 30.9, cj-septembre-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Cette extase dura à peu près l'espace d'un Credo, et elle rendit le dernier soupir. »
- Translation: "This ecstasy lasted about as long as it takes to say a Creed, and then she breathed her last."
- Other sources: Novissima Verba (English) reports the "twelve or thirteen" detail and "Open all the doors". See M-OW list.
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium (interpretive language: "ecstasy"; recorded 1904–1923 in its final form).

## Appended block A — letters LT 1–109 (1877 – July 1890), F-OW-335 onward. Cards are in date order inside this block. The main list above is in date order for the rest of the corpus.

### F-OW-335 — Her first letters: Pauline held her on her lap and guided her hand
- Claim: At age four Thérèse "wrote" her first letters with help. Pauline held her on her lap because she could not yet hold a pen-holder. In the next letter she says Marie will no longer guide her hand and she cannot write alone. In LT 1 she calls herself "a little imp who always laughs" and says she spends the day teasing her poor little sisters.
- When / where: 4 April 1877 and 12–17 April 1877, Alençon (the family still lived there; her mother died in August 1877).
- People: Louise Magdelaine (recipient, a girl she did not know); Pauline Martin (sister); Marie Martin (sister); "ma soeur Marie Aloysia" and "ma soeur Louise de Gonzague" of the Visitation; Jeanne and Marie Guérin (cousins).
- Source: LT 1 (to Louise Magdelaine, 4 Apr 1877), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-1-a-louise-magdelaine-4-avril-1877/ ; LT 2 (to Jeanne and Marie Guérin, 12–17 Apr 1877), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-2-a-jeanne-et-marie-guerin-12-17-avril-1877/
- Source type: own-writing (dictated / hand guided)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « elle me tient sur ses genoux car je ne sais pas seulement tenir un porte-plume, elle veut que je vous dise que je suis une petite paresseuse, mais ce n'est pas vrai car je travaille toute la journée à faire des malices à mes pauvres petites soeurs enfin je suis un petit lutin qui rit toujours » ; « Marie ne veut plus me conduire la main et je ne sais pas écrire toute seule »
- Translation: "She is holding me on her knees because I don't even know how to hold a pen-holder. She wants me to tell you I am a little lazybones, but that is not true, because I work all day long at playing tricks on my poor little sisters. So I am a little imp who always laughs." / "Marie will not guide my hand any more and I do not know how to write by myself."
- Other sources: Ms A describes her as a lively, stubborn small child (baseline does not cover this letter).
- New to English readers: yes — "petit lutin qui rit toujours" is ABSENT from the 1911 edition; not in baseline.
- Confidence: high — exact text; note that the words were probably Pauline's pen with the child's dictation.
- Notes: The letter itself says Pauline told her what to say ("elle veut que je vous dise"). So this is a child's voice shaped by an older sister. She adds that she "knows no other people" than the two Visitation nuns.

### F-OW-336 — Age five: a bead ring from a shop lady at her aunt's
- Claim: While staying with her aunt Guérin in Lisieux, Thérèse went to buy grey stockings with her aunt. The shop lady gave her beads and she made herself a ring. Her cousin Marie was in the country, and she played alone with her aunt.
- When / where: 26 June 1878, Lisieux (Guérin home).
- People: Pauline Martin (recipient); Céline Fournet Guérin, "ma tante"; Marie Guérin (cousin).
- Source: LT 5 (to Pauline, 26 Jun 1878), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-5-a-pauline-26-juin-1878/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « J'ai été acheter des bas gris avec ma tante et la dame m'a donné des perles. Je me suis fait une bague avec. »
- Translation: "I went to buy grey stockings with my aunt and the lady gave me some beads. I made myself a ring with them."
- New to English readers: yes — small childhood detail, not in baseline or 1911.
- Confidence: high — exact text. The spelling here is clean, so an adult may have corrected it (not stated).
- Notes: None.

### F-OW-337 — Age seven: her own spelling, and she knows it is bad
- Claim: In a letter to Pauline written at her aunt's house, Thérèse admits she makes many mistakes and is "hardly skilful". The letter keeps her child spelling ("técrire", "baucoup", "conais", "gerre abile", "segon cienc", "himages"). She reports four good-marks (bons-points) the first day and five the second. While Marie does the accounts, she amuses herself hanging up pretty pictures her aunt gave her.
- When / where: 1 December 1880, Lisieux, at the Guérin house ("nous sommes chez ma Tante").
- People: Pauline (recipient); Marie (sister, doing the accounts); Mme Guérin (aunt); Louis Martin ("Papa").
- Source: LT 6 (to Pauline, 1 Dec 1880), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-6-a-pauline-1er-decembre-1880/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je fais baucoup de fautes, mais tu conais bien ta petite Thérèse et tu sait bien que Je ne suis gerre abile. [...] J'ai eu quatre bons-points le prmier Jours et le segon cienc. »
- Translation: "I make lots of mistakes, but you know your little Thérèse well, and you know I am not very clever at it. [...] I got four good-marks the first day and the second five."
- New to English readers: yes — the misspelled original is not in the 1911 edition or baseline.
- Confidence: high — the Carmel transcription keeps her spelling.
- Notes: Good evidence for her real childhood writing level at nearly eight. Compare the tidy spelling of LT 5 (1878), which may have been corrected by an adult.

### F-OW-338 — Age eight: roses thrown at St Domitia, and "show my letter to no one"
- Claim: Thérèse writes to Pauline, who is on holiday at Houlgate. She asks if Pauline rode a donkey. She thanks Pauline for giving her a holiday from lessons while Pauline is away. On St Domitia's day her aunt put a pink sash on her and she threw roses to St Domitia. She ends: "Show my letter to no one."
- When / where: about 4 July 1881; Thérèse in Lisieux, Pauline at Houlgate.
- People: Pauline (recipient, her teacher at home); Mme Guérin (aunt); Marie; Marie-Thérèse and "la petite Marguerite" (people with Pauline, not identified in the letter).
- Source: LT 7 (to Pauline, 4 Jul 1881), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-7-a-pauline-4-juillet-1881/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je te remercie bien de me donner congé pendant que Tu est à houlgat. [...] Si Tu savais le Jour de Sainte Domitia ma Tante ma mis une sinture rose et j'ai Jeté des roses à Sainte domitia. Ne montre ma lettre à personne. »
- Translation: "Thank you for giving me a holiday while you are at Houlgate. [...] If you only knew: on St Domitia's day my aunt put a pink sash on me and I threw roses to St Domitia. Don't show my letter to anyone."
- New to English readers: yes — absent from 1911; not in baseline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Shows that Pauline was her teacher ("congé" = time off from lessons). Spelling still childish ("sinture", "houlgat").

### F-OW-339 — Age nine: her "confession" to Mère Marie de Gonzague — she talks back
- Claim: Writing to the Carmel prioress, Thérèse confesses that lately she always answers back when Marie tells her to do something. She repeats a family saying: when Pauline was small and made excuses to her aunt at Le Mans, the aunt said "As many holes, as many pegs". Thérèse says she is "even worse". She wants to put a pretty flower in each "hole" and offer it to the Child Jesus to prepare for First Communion. She signs "Thérésita".
- When / where: November–December 1882 (date uncertain in the source), Lisieux. Pauline had entered the Carmel in October 1882.
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress of the Lisieux Carmel); Marie Martin (sister, in charge of her at home); Pauline; "ma tante du Mans" (Sr Marie-Dosithée, Visitation nun of Le Mans — the name is not in the letter).
- Source: LT 9 (to Mère Marie de Gonzague, Nov–Dec 1882), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-9-a-mere-marie-de-gonzague-nov-dec-1882/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Depuis quelque temps je répons toujours quand Marie me dit de faire quelquechose [...] elle lui disait : Autant de trous autant de chevilles, mais moi c'est bien pire encore. »
- Translation: "For some time now I always answer back when Marie tells me to do something. [...] she used to tell her: 'As many holes, as many pegs.' But with me it is even worse."
- New to English readers: yes — "autant de chevilles" ABSENT from 1911; not in baseline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: A child's admitted fault, in her own words. "Autant de trous autant de chevilles" means: for every fault, an excuse ready. The identification of the Le Mans aunt is from general family history, not from this letter.

### F-OW-340 — Preparing for First Communion: the little book of "practices" and the rosary of practices
- Claim: Pauline sent Thérèse a little handmade book for her First Communion preparation. Thérèse loved its picture of a dove giving its heart to the Child Jesus. She tries each day to do as many "practices" (small acts of virtue) as she can and says the short prayers "that make the scent of roses". She thanks Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin for a "chapelet de pratique" (a counting rosary for good acts) and for embroidering the cover of her book. She had not written earlier because she thought letters were not allowed in Lent.
- When / where: 1–6 March 1884, Lisieux.
- People: Pauline (Sr Agnès de Jésus, Carmelite); Marie (showed her the book); Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin (Carmelite of Lisieux); Mère Marie de Gonzague; Léonie and Céline.
- Source: LT 11 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 1–6 Mar 1884), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-11-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-1-6-mars-1884/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Tous les jours je tâche de faire le plus de pratiques que je peux [...] Remercie bien pour moi ma Sr Thérèse de St Augustin de son joli petit chapelet de pratique, et de m'avoir brodé la belle couverture de mon livre. »
- Translation: "Every day I try to do as many practices as I can [...] Please thank Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin for me for her pretty little rosary of practices, and for embroidering the lovely cover of my book."
- Other sources: Ms A mentions the book and the counting of sacrifices (partly in baseline as "preparation for First Communion").
- New to English readers: partial — the book is described in Ms A (Taylor 1912); the letter's details (embroidered cover, the nun's name) are not in baseline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin appears here as a kind helper in 1884. The letter says nothing about any later relationship.

### F-OW-341 — First and second Communion keepsakes
- Claim: For her First Communion (8 May 1884) Thérèse gave small written keepsakes to Marie and Céline. On 21 May 1885 she gave Marie a keepsake "of the second Communion of your little daughter". She calls herself Marie's "little daughter".
- When / where: 8 May 1884 and 21 May 1885, Lisieux.
- People: Marie Martin (godmother and "mother" after Zélie's death); Céline; Marie Guérin (undated keepsake LT 14).
- Source: LT 12 (to Marie, 8 May 1884), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-12-a-marie-8-mai-1884/ ; LT 13 (to Céline, 8 May 1884), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-13-a-celine-8-mai-1884/ ; LT 17 (to Marie, 21 May 1885), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-17-a-marie-21-mai-1885/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « A ma chère petite Marie souvenir de la première communion de / Ta petite fille Thérèse »
- Translation: "To my dear little Marie, a keepsake of the first communion of your little daughter Thérèse."
- New to English readers: partial — First Communion is baseline; the keepsakes are not.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: These are very short dedications (probably written on holy pictures; the source does not say). LT 17 confirms the date of the "second Communion", 21 May 1885.

### F-OW-342 — May 1885: constant headaches before the second Communion retreat
- Claim: Her aunt asked for news of her health. Thérèse writes that she is better than on Sunday, but "I still have a very bad headache". She will enter retreat on Sunday evening; the Communion stays fixed on the 21st. She signs "enf[ant] des Sts Anges" (a member of the Holy Angels association at school).
- When / where: 10–17 May 1885, Lisieux.
- People: Mme Céline Guérin (aunt); Jeanne and Marie Guérin (Marie "finishing her cure").
- Source: LT 16 (to Mme Guérin, fragments, 10–17 May 1885), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-16-a-mme-guerin-fragments-10-17-mai-1885/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je vais mieux que dimanche, cependant j'ai toujours très mal à la tête. »
- Translation: "I am better than on Sunday, but I still have a very bad headache."
- Other sources: Ms A says her scruples began in the retreat before this second Communion (1885).
- New to English readers: yes — the headache report is not in baseline or 1911.
- Confidence: high for the words; the letter is fragmentary.
- Notes: A health detail between the 1883 illness and the 1885–86 scruples. The letter does not give a cause.

### F-OW-343 — The "little Queen" and her "Papa-King": Pauline's verses copied for her father's feast
- Claim: Her father was away on a journey on his feast day (St Louis, 25 August). Pauline had written verses for Thérèse to recite to him. Since she could not recite them, Thérèse copied them into her letter: "Le Souhaits d'une petite Reine pour la fête de son Papa-Roi" (sixteen quatrains). She signs "Ta Reine".
- When / where: 25 August 1885, Lisieux; Louis Martin travelling.
- People: Louis Martin (father); Pauline (author of the verses).
- Source: LT 18 (to M. Martin, 25 Aug 1885), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-18-a-m-martin-25-aout-1885/
- Source type: own-writing (letter) enclosing verses by Pauline
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Mais je n'ai point d'aile qui brille / Je ne suis point un Séraphin / Je suis une petite fille / Qu'on tient encore par la main. »
- Translation: "But I have no shining wing, I am not a Seraph, I am a little girl who is still held by the hand."
- Other sources: 1911 (Pauline's biographical chapter) says her father called her "sa petite reine... de France et de Navarre".
- New to English readers: yes — the poem and "Papa-Roi" are ABSENT from 1911; the "little queen" nickname is partial.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: The verses are Pauline's, not Thérèse's. Do not quote them as Thérèse's poetry. The letter shows the King/Queen game was in place by 1885.

### F-OW-344 — Private lessons with Madame Papinot, and a white dress for the procession
- Claim: After leaving the Benedictine school, Thérèse had lessons with Madame Papinot. The lessons "go very well" and have been increased, so she had no time to write. Tomorrow she will be "in white for the procession"; Marie tried her clothes on her. She apologizes for writing without a draft ("brouillon").
- When / where: Saturday 26 June 1886, "Aux Buissonnets", Lisieux.
- People: Marie Guérin (cousin); Madame Papinot (private teacher); Marie Martin; Céline; Pauline.
- Source: LT 19 (to Marie Guérin, 26 Jun 1886), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-19-a-marie-guerin-26-juin-1886/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « pour les leçons elles marchent toujours très bien, elles sont augmentées depuis quelques temps [...] demain je serai en blanc pour la procession Marie m'a esséyé mes affaires »
- Translation: "The lessons are still going very well; they have been increased for some time now [...] tomorrow I will be in white for the procession; Marie tried my things on me."
- New to English readers: yes — "Papinot" ABSENT from 1911; not in baseline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She normally made a draft before writing a letter ("je n'ai pas eu le temps de faire un brouillon").

### F-OW-345 — The swing and the rings: Marie fears she will become hunchbacked
- Claim: Marie was afraid Thérèse would become hunchbacked, so she asked Papa to hang rings and a swing. Thérèse prefers the swing; her hands are all red from the rings. The day before she spent the afternoon at Madame Maudelonde's with Céline and Hélène. Madame Papinot gave her a day off for the feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel so she could attend the sermon.
- When / where: Thursday 15 July 1886, Les Buissonnets.
- People: Marie Guérin (recipient); Marie Martin; Louis Martin; Mme Maudelonde (a relative of the Guérins); Céline Maudelonde and Hélène Maudelonde (the letter says "Céline et Hélène"); Mme Papinot.
- Source: LT 20 (to Marie Guérin, 15 Jul 1886), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-20-a-marie-guerin-15-juillet-1886/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je viens de me balancer ; Marie a peur que je devienne bossue et elle a demendé à Papa de pendre les anneaux et la balançoire ; les anneaux me plaisent moins que la balançoire, j'ai les mains toutes rouges d'y avoir été. »
- Translation: "I have just been on the swing. Marie is afraid I will become hunchbacked, and she asked Papa to hang up the rings and the swing. I like the rings less than the swing; my hands are all red from them."
- Other sources: 1911 Ms A mentions a swing in an earlier childhood scene (a different episode).
- New to English readers: yes — this episode is not in 1911 or baseline.
- Confidence: high for facts; medium for identifying "Céline et Hélène" as the Maudelonde girls (the letter only gives first names).
- Notes: A concrete body/health detail at age 13: the family worried about her posture.

### F-OW-346 — Waiting for Marie's return: candles to the Virgin, a letter from "Monsieur Pichon", and the maid Félicité
- Claim: Marie was travelling with their father. The family worried that Marie would not know to meet "le Père" at Dover on his return. Céline sent letters to Dover and Calais poste restante. Thérèse says the Virgin "had a candle every day" and she prayed hard. A telegram arrived and relieved them. A letter from "Monsieur Pichon" to Papa was opened on Pauline's advice. Thérèse begs Marie not to bring her any souvenir. In a postscript: "Félicité ... has been in a charming mood since you left."
- When / where: Saturday 2 October 1886, 6 p.m., feast of the Holy Angels, Lisieux.
- People: Marie Martin (godmother, "Marraine"); Louis Martin; "le Père" (not named; a priest returning via Dover); "Monsieur Pichon"; Céline; Pauline (in the Carmel); Léonie; Félicité (household servant); the Guérins.
- Source: LT 21 (to Marie, 2 Oct 1886), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-21-a-marie-2-octobre-1886/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Tous les jours la Ste Vierge a eu un cierge et je l'ai tant priée et suppliée [...] Je t'en conjure ne me rapporte rien cela me fera vraiment de la peine. [...] Félicité te dit bien des choses elle est d'une humeur charmante depuis que tu est partie »
- Translation: "Every day the Blessed Virgin had a candle, and I prayed and begged her so much [...] I beg you, don't bring me anything back; it would really hurt me. [...] Félicité sends her regards; she has been in a charming mood since you left."
- New to English readers: yes — not in 1911 or baseline.
- Confidence: medium — the facts of the letter are clear, but who "le Père" is and the purpose of the trip are not stated.
- Notes: The letter does not say who "le Père" is (possibly Fr Pichon himself, but "Monsieur Pichon" writes separately, so it may be a different person). Marie entered the Carmel on 15 October 1886 (baseline); this letter is two weeks before. The line about Félicité's mood since Marie left may be a joke about Marie's strictness; the letter does not explain it.

### F-OW-347 — An April Fool to Céline
- Claim: On the eve of 1 April, Thérèse wrote Céline a two-line mock-solemn rhyme about her "Diadème" passing to Céline's head, then "Poisson d'Avril!" and the promise of a comb from the April fish.
- When / where: 31 March 1887, Les Buissonnets.
- People: Céline.
- Source: LT 22 (to Céline, 31 Mar 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-22-a-celine-31-mars-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je garde mon Diadème jusqu'à Demain matin / Mais après sur ta tête passera mon Destin / Poisson d'Avril !... / Demain tu auras un peigne que le poisson d'avril te donnera. »
- Translation: "I keep my Diadem until tomorrow morning, but after that my Destiny will pass onto your head. April Fool! Tomorrow you will get a comb that the April fish will give you."
- New to English readers: yes.
- Confidence: high for text; low for what the "diadème" refers to (not explained).
- Notes: Playfulness three months after the Christmas 1886 "conversion".

### F-OW-348 — "Louploup": teasing her sick cousin
- Claim: Her cousin Marie Guérin had a toothache. Thérèse writes a playful note: she asks herself the questions and answers them "to my advantage". She had just come back from visiting the Carmel, where Marie and Pauline will pray for Marie Guérin. She calls her cousin "mon petit Louploup" and says the Louploup must deserve its name and eat like a real wolf. She notes her letter is crooked because she has not held a pen for so long.
- When / where: Monday 27 June 1887, Les Buissonnets; the Guérins at Trouville.
- People: Marie Guérin; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur and Sr Agnès (in the Carmel).
- Source: LT 23 (to Marie Guérin, 27 Jun 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-23-a-marie-guerin-27-juin-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Il faut que mon petit Louploup mérite son nom, qu'il mange comme un vrai Louploup. » ; « Je te quitte en t'embrassant non pas sur les deux joues, je craindrais de te faire mal aux dents, mais sur ton joli petit front. »
- Translation: "My little Louploup must deserve its name and eat like a real wolf." / "I leave you with a kiss, not on both cheeks, for fear of hurting your teeth, but on your pretty little forehead."
- New to English readers: yes — "Louploup" ABSENT from 1911.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Marie Guérin apparently ate little; the letter implies it but does not state it.

### F-OW-349 — "My sermons on death", "my eyes that fascinate you", and the silkworms
- Claim: To her cousin Jeanne, Thérèse drew a sailing boat herself because "the artist Darel" was not there. She jokes that Jeanne must be glad no longer to hear "my sermons on death", no longer to see "my eyes that fascinate you", and no longer to be pushed on the way to the Misses Pigeon. Eight of her silkworms have died; four remain. She blames Céline, who "gave them so much care" that they died "of grief or of sudden apoplexy". "The tall Englishwoman" has left. Papa will leave on Wednesday for a short stay in Alençon, so the aunt's invitation cannot be accepted.
- When / where: 27 June 1887, Les Buissonnets; Guérins at Trouville.
- People: Jeanne Guérin (cousin); "l'artiste Darel" (not identified); "la grande Anglaise" (not identified); "les demoiselles Pigeon" (not identified); Céline; Louis Martin; Mme Guérin.
- Source: LT 24 (to Jeanne Guérin, 27 Jun 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-24-a-jeanne-guerin-27-juin-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je pense que tu es bien contente de ne plus entendre mes sermons sur la mort, de ne plus voir mes yeux qui te fascinent et de ne plus être poussée en allant chez les demoiselles Pigeon... / J'ai à vous annoncer la mort de huit de mes chers vers à soie [...] Céline leur a prodigué tant de soins qu'elle est arrivée à me les faire mourir presque tous de chagrin ou d'apoplexie foudroyante »
- Translation: "I think you are very glad no longer to hear my sermons on death, no longer to see my eyes that fascinate you, and no longer to be pushed on the way to the Misses Pigeon... I must announce the death of eight of my dear silkworms [...] Céline gave them so much care that she managed to make almost all of them die of grief or sudden apoplexy."
- New to English readers: yes — "vers à soie" and "sermons sur la mort" ABSENT from 1911.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Shows her humour and self-mockery at 14, after the 1886 conversion. She mocks her own habit of preaching about death to her cousin.

### F-OW-350 — Léonie's last week at home before the Visitation; Céline's dead birds
- Claim: Léonie had two days left at the Buissonnets before entering the Visitation. Thérèse feels sorrow mixed with joy: she believes Léonie will be happy only there and will find at the Visitation "all that she lacks in the world". Céline is "in mourning" for her two cordon-bleu birds (finches); the male followed his mate the next morning, and their bodies are at the taxidermist. She teases Marie Guérin for her swollen cheeks: "why did you take your face to the sculptor again?"
- When / where: 14 July 1887, Les Buissonnets; Guérins at Trouville.
- People: Marie Guérin; Léonie Martin; Céline; Mme Guérin (ill); Jeanne Guérin; Louis Martin (Marie Guérin's godfather: "sa chère filleule").
- Source: LT 25 (to Marie Guérin, 14 Jul 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-25-a-marie-guerin-14-juillet-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je suis heureuse de voir enfin ma chère Léonie dans son centre ; oui je crois que là seulement elle sera heureuse, à la Visitation elle trouvera tout ce qui lui manque dans le monde. / Céline est en deuil de ses deux petits cordons bleus [...] maintenant ses dépouilles mortelles sont chez l'empailleur. »
- Translation: "I am happy to see my dear Léonie at last in her element; yes, I believe only there will she be happy; at the Visitation she will find everything she lacks in the world. / Céline is in mourning for her two little cordon-bleus [...] now their mortal remains are at the taxidermist's."
- New to English readers: yes.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Léonie left the Visitation later (not covered in these letters until LT 29, when she is still there). Thérèse's hope "only there will she be happy" is a judgement about Léonie's difficulties at home; the letter does not name them.

### F-OW-351 — "I let my pen run like a little mad thing"
- Claim: Marie Guérin fell ill again on holiday. Thérèse scolds her playfully ("little wretch, as soon as you could have some fun, you hurry to be ill"). She pictured her as a "châtelaine" running in the park, but she lives as a patient in a gilded room that is only "a beautiful cage" for a bird. She admits she put "the plough before the oxen" in a sentence and asks Marie to follow the little crosses she added. She says her pen ran "like a little mad thing".
- When / where: 18 August 1887, Les Buissonnets.
- People: Marie Guérin; Isidore Guérin ("Mon oncle"); Mme Guérin; Jeanne Guérin.
- Source: LT 26 (to Marie Guérin, 18 Aug 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-26-a-marie-guerin-18-aout-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Tu es bien heureuse que je sois loin de toi, sans cela bien certainement tu aurais eu affaire à moi... [...] j'ai laissé courir ma plume comme une petite folle »
- Translation: "You are lucky I am far from you; otherwise you would certainly have had to deal with me... [...] I let my pen run like a little mad thing."
- New to English readers: yes.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Her cousin's poor health recurs through 1887–1890 (toothache, swollen cheeks, sore foot, influenza, and a "glande" before the Rome trip in LT 62).

### F-OW-352 — Her uncle's first answer: "a public scandal" — the world is the only obstacle
- Claim: Thérèse finally spoke to her uncle Isidore Guérin about entering Carmel. He was kind, gave her "a little very affectionate sermon", said he was very sure of her vocation, and that the only obstacle was "the world": it would be "a real public scandal" to see a child enter Carmel; she would be "the only one in all France". By the rules of human prudence she should not expect to enter before 17 or 18. If God wills it, He can show it. She did not mention any date. She quotes St Teresa: "God alone suffices."
- When / where: Saturday 8 October 1887, Lisieux (uncle's house). Written the same day.
- People: Isidore Guérin (uncle, pharmacist, her guardian figure after her mother's death); Pauline (Sr Agnès, recipient, "ma confidente"); Marie (Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, in retreat); Mère Marie de Gonzague.
- Source: LT 27 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 8 Oct 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-27-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-8-octobre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il m'a dit qu'il était très sûr de ma vocation, que ce n'était pas cela qui l'empêcherait de me laisser partir, il n'y a que le monde, je crois, qui est un obstacle. Ce serait un véritable scandale public que de voir entrer une enfant au Carmel, je serais la seule sur toute la France, etc... [...] il ne faut pas que je croie entrer avant dix-sept ou dix-huit ans »
- Translation: "He told me he was very sure of my vocation, that this was not what would stop him letting me go; I think only the world is an obstacle. It would be a real public scandal to see a child enter the Carmel; I would be the only one in all France, etc. [...] I must not think of entering before seventeen or eighteen."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911) tells the same talk but harsher: the uncle said it was against human prudence, that he would oppose it with all his power, and that only a miracle would change his mind. See the conflict card below.
- New to English readers: yes — "scandale public" ABSENT from 1911; the letter's gentler version is not in baseline.
- Confidence: high — contemporary letter written the same day.
- Notes: Type of conflict: family/social prudence. The uncle's stated reason is public opinion ("le monde"), not doubt about her vocation.

### F-OW-353 — Conflict: the uncle's refusal — letter of the same day vs Story of a Soul
- Claim: The two accounts of her uncle's first answer differ in tone. In LT 27 (written that day) he is "very good", "affectionate", "very sure" of her vocation, and names only "the world" and her age (17–18). In Ms A (written 1895, as printed 1911) he declares he will put "all possible opposition" in the way and will not change his mind "unless there is a miracle".
- When / where: 8 Oct 1887 (event); letter same day; Ms A 1895; 1911 print.
- People: Isidore Guérin; Thérèse; Pauline.
- Source: LT 27 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 8 Oct 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-27-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-8-octobre-1887/ ; compared with Histoire d'une âme 1911, chap. V (Gutenberg #36708).
- Source type: own-writing (both), different dates
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: LT 27: « Mon oncle a été très bon [...] il m'a fait un petit sermon très AFFECTUEUX » ; 1911: « Il ajouta qu'il allait y mettre de son côté toute l'opposition possible, et qu'à moins d'un miracle, il ne changerait pas d'avis. »
- Translation: LT 27: "My uncle was very kind [...] he gave me a little, very AFFECTIONATE sermon." / 1911: "He added that for his part he would put up all possible opposition, and that unless there was a miracle he would not change his mind."
- New to English readers: yes — the letter's version is not in English baseline; Ms A's version is in Taylor.
- Confidence: medium — the letter tells Pauline she reports only the obstacles and that he said "many other very kind things"; she may have softened the scene for Pauline, or Ms A may have sharpened it. Both are her own words.
- Notes: LT 28 (23 Oct) confirms the uncle agreed "yesterday" (Saturday 22 Oct), which matches Ms A's "the fourth day, a Saturday".

### F-OW-354 — First letter to Fr Pichon: "I am not like my sisters"
- Claim: Thérèse asks Fr Almire Pichon SJ, director of her sisters, to take "the last one" as well. She says she cannot express herself well in a letter, unlike her sisters. She announces that "Papa is willing for me to enter at Christmas" and that her uncle, who found her too young, told her "yesterday" that he wanted to do God's will. Her sisters at the Carmel told her she could write to him "quite simply". She signs asking him to bless "your second little Lamb".
- When / where: 23 October 1887, Lisieux.
- People: Fr Almire Pichon SJ (Jesuit, director of Marie and Pauline); Louis Martin; Isidore Guérin; her sisters in the Carmel.
- Source: LT 28 (to Fr Pichon, 23 Oct 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-28-au-p-pichon-23-octobre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je voudrais pouvoir me faire connaître à vous mais je ne suis pas comme mes sœurs, je ne sais pas bien dire dans une lettre tout ce que je ressens. [...] Papa veut bien que j'entre à Noël. [...] Bénissez votre second petit Agneau »
- Translation: "I would like to make myself known to you, but I am not like my sisters; I don't know how to say well in a letter all that I feel. [...] Papa is willing for me to enter at Christmas. [...] Bless your second little Lamb."
- Other sources: Fr Pichon is in the baseline by name only.
- New to English readers: yes — ABSENT from 1911 ("second petit Agneau", "entre à Noël").
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Fixes the dates: father's consent before 23 Oct; uncle's consent 22 Oct 1887; target entry Christmas 1887. "Second little lamb": the letter does not say who the first was (perhaps Pauline, whom she elsewhere calls "l'agneau").

### F-OW-355 — To Léonie at the Visitation: kissing the Child Jesus's foot for her
- Claim: Léonie, then at the Visitation, had asked Thérèse to pray to Blessed Margaret Mary that she might become "a holy Visitandine"; Thérèse says she does so every day. Léonie had warned her to look after her pretty little statue of the Child Jesus; it is unharmed, and Thérèse "kissed his little foot" for Léonie. They are "so busy now" that Céline cannot write.
- When / where: 23–30 October 1887 (date uncertain in source), Lisieux; Léonie at the Visitation of Caen (the city is not named in the letter).
- People: Léonie Martin; Céline.
- Source: LT 29 (to Léonie, 23–30 Oct 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-29-a-leonie-23-30-octobre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il n'est pas abîmé, il est aussi frais que lorsque tu l'as quitté, j'ai embrassé son petit pied pour toi, sa petite main semblait te bénir de loin. »
- Translation: "It is not damaged; it is as fresh as when you left it. I kissed his little foot for you; his little hand seemed to bless you from afar."
- New to English readers: yes.
- Confidence: high; the letter is unfinished in the source (breaks off "mais").
- Notes: "Si pressées maintenant" — likely the preparations for the Rome pilgrimage (leaving early November); the letter does not say so.

### F-OW-356 — Paris, Hôtel de Mulhouse: exhausted, afraid of the carriages
- Claim: On the way to Rome, Thérèse writes from Paris that she is "extremely tired; everything is spinning around me". Paris has fine things "but all that is not happiness". Like her godmother Marie, she is always afraid of being run over; carriages surround her at every moment. She was very happy at Notre-Dame-des-Victoires and asked a grace for her cousin Jeanne at the Sacred Heart of Montmartre. Céline did not want her to write the day before.
- When / where: 6 November 1887, Paris, Hôtel de Mulhouse.
- People: Sr Agnès and Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (recipients); Céline; Léonie; Jeanne Guérin; the Guérins; Mère Marie de Gonzague.
- Source: LT 30 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus and Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 6 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-30-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-et-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur-6-novembre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je suis extrêmement fatiguée ; tout tourne autour de moi. Demain nous ne serons plus en France. [...] Je suis un peu comme ma chère Marraine, j'ai toujours peur d'être écrasée, à chaque instant je me trouve cernée par les voitures. »
- Translation: "I am extremely tired; everything is spinning around me. Tomorrow we will no longer be in France. [...] I am a bit like my dear Godmother; I am always afraid of being run over; at every moment I find myself hemmed in by carriages."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911) mentions Notre-Dame-des-Victoires and the Montmartre consecration.
- New to English readers: yes — "peur d'être écrasée" and "Hôtel de Mulhouse" ABSENT from 1911.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: "Demain nous ne serons plus en France" does not match Ms A's route exactly (they left Paris 7 Nov and crossed Switzerland); it is her expectation at the time.

### F-OW-357 — Venice: "How small I felt before those gigantic mountains"; "Signorella"
- Claim: From Venice, Thérèse tells her cousin that crossing the Swiss mountains she felt small and that "one prays so well there; one feels God is there". That afternoon they visited Venice's monuments by gondola. The hotel staff call her "Signorella"; it is the only Italian word she understands. Céline makes her hurry and will not let her stay up late. She says the beauties of Italy cannot make her forget "my dear little Lisieux".
- When / where: Thursday 10 November 1887 (evening), Venice. A covering note (LT 31 B, Monday 14 Nov) says Céline forgot to post it for four days.
- People: Marie Guérin (recipient); Mme Guérin; Isidore Guérin; Jeanne Guérin; Céline; Louis Martin; Maria and Marcelline (named in a P.S.; Marcelline appears to be a Guérin servant).
- Source: LT 31 / 31 A (to Marie Guérin, 10 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-31-lt-31-a-a-marie-guerin-10-novembre-1887/ ; LT 31 B (to Marie Guérin, 14 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-31-b-a-marie-guerin-14-novembre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Comme je me semblais petite devant ces montagnes gigantesques ! [...] Les gens de l'hôtel m'appellent Signorella mais je ne comprends pas autre chose que ce mot qui veut dire petite demoiselle. »
- Translation: "How small I felt before those gigantic mountains! [...] The hotel people call me Signorella, but I understand no other word; it means little miss."
- Other sources: Ms A has a long passage on the Swiss mountains (Taylor).
- New to English readers: yes for "Signorella" (ABSENT from 1911); partial for the mountains.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She also writes "(Tu sais, je n'ai pas oublié ce que tu as fait pour moi un Dimanche)" — a private kindness from Marie Guérin, not explained.

### F-OW-358 — Pauline tells her to speak to the Pope; she had already thought of it
- Claim: A letter from Pauline (received at the "Hôtel de Milan") told Thérèse to speak to the Pope. She does not know how she will do it. She has heard the Holy Father passes in front of the faithful but does not think he stops. Still, she is "quite resolved to speak to him", because she had thought of it before Pauline wrote, but waited for God to make it known. The same day she tells Pauline: "since Monseigneur does not want it, it is the last means left".
- When / where: 14 November 1887, in Italy (Rome pilgrimage; the audience was on 20 Nov).
- People: Mme Guérin (recipient of LT 32, whose feast was 19 Nov); Pauline (Sr Agnès, author of the instructions); Mgr Flavien Hugonin, bishop of Bayeux ("Monseigneur").
- Source: LT 32 (to Mme Guérin, 14 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-32-a-mme-guerin-14-novembre-1887/ ; LT 34 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 14 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-34-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-14-novembre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: LT 32: « Je ferai ce que Pauline me dit dans sa lettre (Hôtel de Milan), je ne sais comment je m'y prendrai pour parler au Pape. [...] avant que Pauline m'ait écrit, j'y pensais » ; LT 34: « puisque Monseigneur ne veut pas, c'est le dernier moyen qui me reste de parler au Pape »
- Translation: "I will do what Pauline tells me in her letter (Hôtel de Milan); I don't know how I will manage to speak to the Pope. [...] before Pauline wrote to me, I was already thinking of it." / "Since Monseigneur does not want it, it is the last means left to me: speaking to the Pope."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911) reports Pauline's letter and the audience; the letters add that the bishop's refusal was known by 14 Nov.
- New to English readers: partial — the audience is baseline; these planning details are not.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Opposition: Mgr Hugonin (bishop) had not agreed by 14 Nov. The letters show Pauline directing the plan from inside the Carmel.

### F-OW-359 — "The little ball of the Child Jesus" — first use of the image
- Claim: On 14 November 1887 Thérèse signs a letter "Le petit jouet de Jésus" and asks that the Child Jesus prepare everything "so that his little ball has only to roll where he wants". On 20 November, after the audience, she writes: "I am the little Ball of the Child Jesus; if he wants to break his toy, he is quite free."
- When / where: Rome pilgrimage, 14 and 20 November 1887.
- People: Pauline (Sr Agnès).
- Source: LT 34 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 14 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-34-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-14-novembre-1887/ ; LT 36 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 20 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-36-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-20-novembre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il faut que ce soit le Petit Jésus qui prépare tout pour que sa petite balle n'ait plus qu'à rouler où il le veut » ; « je suis la petite Balle de l'Enfant Jésus ; s'il veut briser son jouet il est bien libre »
- Translation: "It must be the Little Jesus who prepares everything, so that his little ball has only to roll where he wants." / "I am the little Ball of the Child Jesus; if he wants to break his toy, he is quite free."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911) explains she had offered herself as a little ball before the trip.
- New to English readers: partial — the image is in Ms A (Taylor); the letter dates are new.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: The image returns in the clothing retreat (LT 74, 76, 79; January 1889).

### F-OW-360 — Relics of St Agnes: refused by the monks
- Claim: In Rome Thérèse asked some monks whether she could have relics of St Agnes; it was impossible.
- When / where: before 14 November 1887, Rome.
- People: Marie (Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, recipient); unnamed monks.
- Source: LT 33 (to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 14 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-33-a-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur-14-novembre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown

- Translation: "I asked at some monks' house whether I could have relics of St Agnes; it is impossible."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911, Taylor) confirms that "men refused" her a relic at the church of St Agnes, and says a small piece of red marble then fell at her feet from an old mosaic, which she took as St Agnes's own souvenir for Pauline. The letter of 14 Nov does not mention the marble.
