She says neither one breeds melancholy, but they must not say useless words, because "after each useful sentence there always comes a little amusing refrain that must be kept for recreation". She also recalls, as things that will be rewarded in Heaven, taking "the potion on reception days", going to Matins "in spite of our sad faces", "chasing rabbits and gathering oats".
- When / where: 16 Jul 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin); Mme Guérin; Thérèse
- Source: LT 192 (to Mme Guérin, 16 Jul 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-192-a-mme-guerin-16-juillet-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Nous sommes toujours ensemble à l'emploi et nous nous entendons très bien. » ; « d'avoir chassé les lapins et cueilli de l'avoine »
- Translation: "We are always together at the work and we get on very well." ; "having chased rabbits and picked oats."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the words. The letter gives no explanation of "rabbits and oats" or "the potion on reception days", and we do not guess.
- Notes: Marie Guérin entered the Carmel in 1895 (see earlier cards). The "emploi" that both worked in is not named in this letter. LT 193 (30 Jul 1896) says the Sichuan map hangs on "the wall of the emploi where I work".

### F-OW-450 — Roulland's photograph in the cell, and the map of Sichuan on the workroom wall
- Claim: On 30 Jul 1896 Thérèse tells Roulland that she may now call him "brother", since Jesus has joined them "by the bonds of the apostolate". The prioress had "introduced you into the enclosure": she received his letters "yesterday" and allowed Thérèse to keep his photograph, "a very special privilege, since a Carmelite has not even the portraits of her nearest relatives". She has pinned the map of "Su-tchuen" on the wall of the workroom where she works. His holy card is kept in the Gospels "which never leave me". When she put it in at random, it fell on "Whoever has left everything to follow me will receive a hundredfold in this world and eternal life". She copies from Isaiah for him in her meditation that evening. She asks him for the main dates of his life so that she can thank God for his graces.
- When / where: 30 Jul 1896, Carmel of Lisieux (letter); Roulland's departure for China was that summer (dates not in this letter)
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland (recipient); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress, "Notre Bonne Mère")
- Source: LT 193 (to P. Roulland, 30 Jul 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-193-au-p-roulland-30-juillet-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « une carmélite n'a pas même les portraits de ses parents les plus proches » ; « j'ai fixé la carte du Su-tchuen sur le mur de l'emploi où je travaille »
- Translation: "A Carmelite does not even have the portraits of her closest relatives." ; "I have fixed the map of Su-tchuen on the wall of the workroom where I work."
- New to English readers: yes ("Su-tchuen", "mon Frère" opening and "d'aller vous visiter au Su-tchuen" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The permission to keep a photograph was exceptional, and the letter says so. The "map" detail is a concrete, checkable detail for a scene. The letter does not state where the Gospel image was placed apart from "on my heart, in the Gospels".

### F-OW-451 — "If I go soon to Heaven I will ask Jesus to visit you in Sichuan"
- Claim: At the end of LT 193, Thérèse writes: "If I go soon to Heaven, I will ask Jesus for permission to go and visit you in Su-tchuen, and we shall continue our apostolate together." She asks that Jesus never let her enjoy consolations while he suffers, and even that her "brother always have the consolations and I the trials". She adds: "Perhaps that is selfish?... But no, since my only weapon is love and suffering, and your sword is that of the word and apostolic labours." She also says that "death itself will make our union closer".
- When / where: 30 Jul 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland (recipient)
- Source: LT 193 (to P. Roulland, 30 Jul 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-193-au-p-roulland-30-juillet-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Si je vais bientôt dans le Ciel, je demanderai à Jésus la permission d'aller vous visiter au Su-tchuen et nous continuerons ensemble notre apostolat. »
- Translation: "If I go soon to Heaven, I will ask Jesus for permission to visit you in Su-tchuen, and we shall continue our apostolate together."
- Other sources: Later sayings of 1897 ("I will spend my heaven doing good on earth") repeat the idea; see the Carnet jaune cards.
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is the earliest dated form we have in her writing of the "from Heaven" promise, one year before the Carnet jaune saying (17 Jul 1897) and about 3 months after the first blood-spitting. The letter does not refer to her illness. The "Su-tchuen" mission is Roulland's; he went to Sichuan (outside knowledge; the letter names it).
- Related item: LT 193 bis (20 Aug 1896) is a holy card for Roulland, text: "O Divine Blood of Jesus, water our Mission, make the elect spring up" (« O sang Divin de Jésus ! arrosez notre Mission, Faites germer les Élus »), signed "Th. de l'Enf. Jésus rel. carm." The Carmel site links a page on the discovery of the image.

### F-OW-452 — To a novice: "the little Child" who throws flowers, and "the most painful martyrdom is ours"
- Claim: Around 8–17 Sep 1896 she writes to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph (two fragments). She is "delighted with the little Child" and says the child's vocation is not to evangelise one mission but "all the missions", "by loving, by sleeping, by THROWING FLOWERS to Jesus when he slumbers". Jesus will throw the flowers on every shore and save souls with them. "A missionary and warrior child, what a wonder!" In the second fragment: "The most painful, the most LOVING martyrdom is ours, since Jesus alone sees it." It will never be revealed on earth, but when the Lamb opens the book of life the court of Heaven will hear the names of poor children "who never did brilliant deeds" proclaimed with those of the missionaries and martyrs. She ends: "I continue to nurse the very sick toques (bonnets)."
- When / where: 8–17 (?) Sep 1896 (both fragments), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph (recipient, an older nun of the community; not identified further in the letter)
- Source: LT 194 and LT 195 (to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph, 8–17 (?) Sep 1896, fragments), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-194-a-soeur-marie-de-saint-joseph-8-17-septembre-1896-fragment/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-195-a-soeur-marie-de-saint-joseph-8-17-septembre-1896-fragments/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « C'est en aimant, en dormant, en JETANT des FLEURS à Jésus lorsqu'il sommeille » ; « Le martyre le plus douloureux, le plus AMOUREUX est le nôtre puisque Jésus seul le voit. » ; « Je continue à soigner les toques très malades. »
- Translation: "By loving, by sleeping, by THROWING FLOWERS to Jesus when he is asleep." ; "The most painful, the most LOVING martyrdom is ours, because Jesus alone sees it." ; "I keep on nursing the very sick bonnets."
- New to English readers: yes (all three quoted phrases ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; medium for the dating (the source gives "(?)")
- Notes: "Le petit Enfant" is not explained in the fragment: it is probably an image or gift linked to her Ms B "little child" (Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph drew or gave it), but this is a guess and no source in this file says so. The "toques" line shows a task: she repairs the community's head-coverings (source does not explain further). It is the first line of the letters of Sept 1896 with the "flower-throwing" image that becomes the "shower of roses".

### F-OW-453 — LT 196 is the same text as the opening of Ms B (no new card needed)
- Claim: The Carmel site prints Ms B 1r–2v also as LT 196 (to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, "13 (?) Sept 1896"). The wording matches Ms B 1r in _work/msB.f.txt (it opens "Ô ma Sœur chérie! vous me demandez de vous donner un souvenir de ma retraite, retraite qui peut-être sera la dernière..." and ends with "ma petite doctrine", "En écrivant, c'est à Jésus que je parle"). The facts are carded under Ms B 1r–1v (see the card on the covering letter to Marie: "my consolation is to have none on earth").
- When / where: about 13 Sep 1896 (source marks "(?)"), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (recipient)
- Source: LT 196, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-196-a-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur-13-septembre-1896/ ; Ms B 1r–2v
- Source type: own-writing

- Translation: "Do not think I swim in consolations. Oh no! My consolation is to have none on earth."
- New to English readers: partial (Ms B is in Taylor 1912, but chk.py finds this opening ABSENT from the 1911 French edition, so the letter form of the text is not in the old English book)
- Confidence: high

### F-OW-454 — To Marie, 17 Sep 1896: "confidence and nothing but confidence", after Marie read the "little bird" pages
- Claim: Marie (Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur) asked whether she could love God as Thérèse does. Thérèse answers that she is "not embarrassed" to reply, and that if Marie had understood "the story of my little bird" (the Ms B pages) she would not ask. She says her desires for martyrdom "are nothing"; they do not give her "the unlimited confidence I feel in my heart". Spiritual riches "make one unjust" when one rests in them. Such desires are "a consolation Jesus sometimes grants to weak souls like mine", and when he does not give it "it is a grace of privilege". What pleases God "is to see me love my littleness and my poverty; it is the blind hope I have in his mercy. That is my only treasure." She tells Marie that if she wants to feel joy and attraction for suffering, she is looking for her own consolation. "The more one is weak, without desires or virtues, the more one is fit for the workings of this consuming and transforming Love." She ends: "It is confidence and nothing but confidence that must lead us to Love." She adds that fear leads to Justice, not to the Justice Jesus will have "for those who love him". A clock struck nine and she had to stop.
- When / where: 17 Sep 1896, Carmel of Lisieux (evening; "9 o'clock strikes")
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (Marie Martin, sister and godmother, recipient)
- Source: LT 197 (to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 17 Sep 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-197-a-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur-17-septembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « ce qui lui plaît c'est de me voir aimer ma petitesse et ma pauvreté, c'est l'espérance aveugle que j'ai en sa miséricorde... Voilà mon seul trésor. » ; « C'est la confiance et rien que la confiance qui doit nous conduire à l'Amour... »
- Translation: "What pleases him is to see me love my littleness and my poverty; it is the blind hope I have in his mercy. That is my only treasure." ; "It is confidence and nothing but confidence that must lead us to Love."
- Other sources: The 1911 edition prints this as "Lettre VIe" (see the edit card below); Ms B (8 Sep 1896) is the source of "my little bird".
- New to English readers: no (the main passages are in 1911, so in Taylor 1912); partial for the original wording
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is the best-known letter of her mature "way". The source shows the occasion: it answers Marie's question and cites the Ms B image. "Si nous allions ensemble au martyre" is her plain admission that Marie would have more merit than she at martyrdom.

### F-OW-455 — Edit: 1911 rewrites the 17 Sep 1896 letter to Marie
- Claim: The 1911 edition prints LT 197 as "Lettre VIe" with changes. (1) "ces paroles du Père" becomes "d'un saint religieux". (2) The Carmel text has "a dit le psalmiste" for "Le véritable pauvre d'esprit, où le trouver? il faut le chercher bien loin"; the 1911 text has "dit l'auteur de l'Imitation". (3) The sentence "Le bon Dieu ne vous donnerait pas le désir d'être possédée..." is moved to before "Puisque nous voyons la voie". (4) "9 heures sonnent, je suis obligée de vous quitter" and the closing lines are cut. (5) A new ending about the baptism promise and "l'ange qui m'a conduite" is added from another letter. (6) "Ma Soeur chérie" becomes "Ma sœur bien-aimée"; "ma Soeur chérie... MARRAINE" becomes "Marraine chérie".
- When / where: text of 17 Sep 1896; edition 1911
- People: Pauline (editor); Marie (recipient)
- Source: LT 197 (Carmel site) compared with Histoire d'une âme 1911 (D1a-histoire-dune-ame-gutenberg-36708.txt, "Lettre VIe", 17 Sep 1896)
- Source type: own-writing / edition
- Rights: public-domain (her words and the 1911 text); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « comme si ... » is not needed; see the differences above
- Translation: ours; see the Claim
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 translated the edited text)
- Confidence: high (both texts read)
- Notes: The word "psalmiste" in the manuscript is a slip or a different attribution (the phrase is not Psalm text; in 1911 it is credited to the Imitation). The editors' change is a correction, not a softening. The Carmel text does not give the source. "Père" in the original is not identified.

### F-OW-456 — First letter to Abbé Maurice Bellière: she writes for the sick prioress
- Claim: Thérèse writes her first letter to the seminarian Abbé Maurice Bellière because "our Reverend Mother", ill, gave her "the mission of answering your letter". She says Bellière's July letter "had greatly distressed me"; she blamed her own lack of fervour for the "combats" he suffered and did not stop asking the "Queen of Apostles" for help. She was consoled to receive, as a feast-day bouquet, news that her prayers were heard. Now the storm has passed she thanks God, quoting "Blessed is the man who has suffered temptation" and "He who has not been tempted, what does he know?". She asks that he become "not only a good missionary but a saint on fire with love of God and souls". She says: "A Carmelite who was not an apostle would depart from the aim of her vocation", and that St Teresa wished to give "a thousand lives to save one soul". She asks his prayers for the cure of the prioress. She signs "your unworthy little sister".
- When / where: 21 Oct 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Barthélemy Bellière (seminarian and later White Father, recipient); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress, ill, the "Révérende Mère"); St Teresa of Avila
- Source: LT 198 (to l'abbé Maurice Bellière, 21 Oct 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-198-a-labbe-maurice-belliere-21-octobre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Notre Révérende Mère étant malade, m'a confié la mission de répondre à votre lettre » ; « une carmélite qui ne serait pas apôtre s'éloignerait du but de sa vocation »
- Translation: "Our Reverend Mother, being ill, has entrusted me with the mission of answering your letter." ; "A Carmelite who was not an apostle would move away from the aim of her vocation."
- New to English readers: yes ("Notre Révérende Mère étant malade", "Reine des Apôtres" and "mille vies pour sauver une seule âme" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the text. "White Father" and Bellière's first name are outside the letter.
- Notes: The letter shows how the relationship began: the prioress assigned her, and she replied on behalf of the prioress. The "July letter" of Bellière is not in this file. The letter does not say what temptations he faced. The tone is formal ("Monsieur l'Abbé") and she does not yet use "brother".

### F-OW-457 — Two notes to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph: the "little soldier" and "Messire Satanas"
- Claim: In LT 199 (20–30 Oct 1896) she scolds the recipient with a soldier's story: she is not surprised by her "p.f." fights, but that the "little soldier" gives up his weapons "to the first corporal" and even chases the corporal "up the barracks stairs" so that he takes the last piece of armour. A strong sunbeam then burns the disarmed soldier and gives him fever. The punishment is to shut himself "in the prison of love" and sleep, but before that he must play "the musical instrument of penance" that evening. "Above all no staying awake! Tomorrow we will wear our arms out together." In LT 200 (late Oct 1896): "the little child is a brave one who deserves gold epaulettes", but must not lower himself to fight with "little stones": "His weapon must be Charity." The child "mocks Messire Satan" and "sleeps always on the Heart of the Great General". Near that Heart "one learns valour, and above all confidence".
- When / where: 20–30 (?) Oct 1896 and late Oct (?) 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph (recipient); Thérèse
- Source: LT 199 and LT 200 (to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-199-a-soeur-marie-de-saint-joseph-20-30-octobre-1896/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-200-a-soeur-marie-de-saint-joseph-fin-octobre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Son arme doit être «La Charité». » ; « le petit enfant se moque de Messire Satanas, et ... il sommeille toujours sur le Coeur du Grand Général »
- Translation: "His weapon must be 'Charity'." ; "The little child laughs at Sir Satan and always sleeps on the Heart of the Great General."
- New to English readers: yes (all ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; low for the identity of "p.f." (the letter uses the initials and does not spell them out) and of the "fight". We do not know what the recipient did.
- Notes: The sister's difficulties are not described. The letters use a private code (military images, "p.f.", "the little child"). They show how Thérèse spoke to a sister (probably a younger or troubled sister in her care) with jokes and firmness. The letters do not say she was a novice.

### F-OW-458 — Same day, 8 Sep 1890: Roulland's vocation "saved" and her profession
- Claim: In her long letter to Père Roulland (1 Nov 1896), Thérèse tells "a secret" she found on the sheet of "memorable dates" he sent her: on 8 Sep 1890 his missionary vocation was "saved by Mary, Queen of apostles and martyrs" (the letter gives no details). On that same day a little Carmelite became "the spouse of the King of Heaven"; her sole aim was to save souls, "above all the souls of apostles". She had asked Jesus for "an apostolic soul", since she could not be a priest and wanted a priest to receive in her place the same graces and aspirations. She asks: "Do you not think, as I do, that our union, confirmed on the day of your priestly ordination, began on 8 September?" She had thought she would meet "the apostle, the brother I had asked of Jesus" only in Heaven. She says that when the prioress proposed that she become his "auxiliary" she hesitated, thinking another sister would serve him better, and accepted only because Jesus looks at "good will", not works. "I did not know that for 6 years I had a brother who was preparing to become a missionary." She also mentions the book "L'Âme d'un Missionnaire" (life of Père Nempon), which he sent, and says Roulland asked in a letter from Marseille that she pray he would not be made a seminary director or sent back to France.
- When / where: 1 Nov 1896, Carmel of Lisieux (letter to a priest travelling to China)
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland; Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress, "notre bonne Mère"); Père Nempon (subject of the book)
- Source: LT 201 (to P. Roulland, 1 Nov 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-201-au-p-roulland-1-novembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Le 8 Septembre 1890 votre vocation de missionnaire était sauvée par Marie, la Reine des apôtres et des martyrs ; en ce même jour une petite carmélite devenait l'épouse du Roi des Cieux. » ; « Je ne savais pas que depuis 6 ans j'avais un frère qui se préparait à devenir Missionnaire »
- Translation: "On 8 September 1890 your vocation as a missionary was saved by Mary, Queen of apostles and martyrs. On that same day a little Carmelite became the bride of the King of Heaven." ; "I did not know that for six years I had a brother who was preparing to become a missionary."
- New to English readers: yes ("Le 8 Septembre 1890 votre vocation" and "L'âme d'un Missionnaire" ABSENT from the 1911 edition; the letter is not in Taylor 1912 as far as local files show)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The letter shows how she read a coincidence of dates as a sign. It says nothing about what threatened Roulland's vocation in 1890. Compare LT 189, 193 and later LT 221, 226, 254.

### F-OW-459 — Her own account to Roulland of Christmas 1886 ("night of my conversion") and of Rome
- Claim: Roulland guessed she would thank God at Christmas for "the day of graces". She answers that she did not receive the grace of the religious vocation that day: "Our Lord, wanting my first glance for himself alone, deigned to ask for my heart from the cradle." Christmas night 1886 "was decisive for my vocation", but "to name it more clearly I must call it the night of my conversion". Jesus "who made himself a child for love of me" took her "out of the swaddling clothes and imperfections of childhood" and changed her so much that she "no longer recognised herself". Without that change she "would have had to stay in the world for many years". She quotes St Teresa: "I want you to be women in nothing, but in everything to equal strong men", and says Teresa would not have known her as her child unless the Lord had clothed her with his strength. She promises to pray for a girl who meets obstacles to her vocation and hopes she will not have to go "as far as Rome", adding "Perhaps you do not know your sister had the audacity to speak to the Pope?". She says her obstacle was her youth: "our good Father Superior formally refused to receive me before 21", saying a child of 15 could not know what she was undertaking. She says he was prudent, and that God wanted her to take the fortress of Carmel "at the point of the sword".
- When / where: 1 Nov 1896, Carmel of Lisieux; events of Dec 1886, Nov 1887, 1888
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland; Canon Delatroëtte (the "Père Supérieur"; the letter does not name him); Leo XIII; St Teresa of Avila; Louis Martin (father)
- Source: LT 201 (to P. Roulland, 1 Nov 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-201-au-p-roulland-1-novembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Ce n'est point ce jour-là que j'ai reçu la grâce de la vocation religieuse. Notre Seigneur voulant pour Lui seul mon premier regard, daigna me demander mon coeur dès le berceau » ; « pour la nommer plus clairement je dois l'appeler : la nuit de ma conversion » ; « Sans doute vous ignorez que votre soeur a eu l'audace de parler au Pape ?... »
- Translation: "It was not on that day that I received the grace of the religious vocation. Our Lord, wanting my first glance for himself alone, asked for my heart from the cradle." ; "To name it more clearly I must call it the night of my conversion." ; "Perhaps you do not know that your sister had the audacity to speak to the Pope?"
- Other sources: Ms A (1895) gives the same "conversion" of Christmas 1886 (baseline). This letter is 1896, a year later.
- New to English readers: partial (the events are in the baseline; the wording to Roulland and the phrase "the audacity to speak to the Pope", the vocation-from-the-cradle sentence and the Teresa quotation are ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high. "Canon Delatroëtte" is our identification (see LT 39–40 cards); the letter says only "notre bon Père Supérieur".
- Notes: She uses the word "conversion" for Christmas 1886 (the letter describes it as leaving "the swaddling clothes and imperfections of childhood", not as a change from sin). Compare her contemporary letter LT 36 (audience). The letter also says her father offered to take her to Jerusalem if she delayed entry by two or three months; she refused, "one day in the house of the Lord is better than a thousand elsewhere" (see next card).

### F-OW-460 — Her father offered Jerusalem if she waited two or three months; she chose Carmel
- Claim: In LT 201 Thérèse tells Roulland that she wanted no journey "for personal pleasure". When her "incomparable father" proposed to take her to Jerusalem if she would delay her entry by two or three months, she did not hesitate, despite "the natural attraction which drew me to visit the places sanctified by the life of the Saviour". She chose "the rest in the shadow" of the one she desired. She writes that "one single day passed in the house of the Lord is worth more than a thousand elsewhere".
- When / where: told 1 Nov 1896; the offer was made in 1887–88 (the letter gives no date)
- People: Louis Martin (father); Roulland (recipient)
- Source: LT 201 (to P. Roulland, 1 Nov 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-201-au-p-roulland-1-novembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « lorsque mon incomparable père me proposa de me conduire à Jérusalem si je voulais retarder mon entrée de deux ou trois mois »
- Translation: "When my incomparable father proposed to take me to Jerusalem if I would delay my entry by two or three months."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911 edition) says her father "proposed again a journey to Jerusalem" but not the "two or three months" condition (chk.py: "conduire à Jérusalem" ABSENT from 1911).
- New to English readers: yes for the "two or three months" condition; partial for the Jerusalem offer
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This shows her father's offer as a trade-off. It adds a fact about the timeline: the entry could have been delayed to spring or summer 1888 if she had accepted.

### F-OW-461 — "The hair of a future martyr": her "sin of envy" and Moses and Joshua
- Claim: Thérèse tells Roulland she committed "a sin of envy" when she read that his hair would be cut and replaced by a Chinese queue. She wanted not the queue but "a small lock of the hair that had become useless", to keep as a relic "when you are in Heaven, the palm of martyrdom in hand". She jokes that she asks "long in advance" because she knows she, "who is recognised as your sister only by Jesus", will be forgotten in the distribution of his relics. She hopes he will "pay" for this "little recreation" by a gift of "the hair of a future martyr". She writes that he has put on Chinese dress and hopes for the "incomparable favour" of martyrdom after "many years of apostolic labour": "sang pour sang". She sets out their roles: "Like Joshua you fight in the plain; I am your little Moses", with heart lifted to Heaven. She says she will keep all his anniversaries, especially 3 July, the day he first received Communion and, in 1896, said his first Mass at the Carmel where she "received Jesus from your hand". She will send her angel on 25 Dec to place her intentions by the host he consecrates at his dawn Mass; the nuns will sing Christmas Matins then.
- When / where: 1 Nov 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland; Thérèse
- Source: LT 201 (to P. Roulland, 1 Nov 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-201-au-p-roulland-1-novembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « j'ai commis un péché d'envie en lisant que vos cheveux allaient être coupés » ; « Comme Josué vous combattez dans la plaine, moi je suis votre petit Moïse »
- Translation: "I committed a sin of envy when I read that your hair was going to be cut off." ; "Like Joshua you fight in the plain; I am your little Moses."
- New to English readers: yes (both phrases and "Les cheveux d'un futur Martyr" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: A rare joke about relics in her own hand. She predicts he will be a martyr, which did not happen (outside knowledge: Roulland lived to 1934). The letter states that his first Mass at the Carmel was on 3 July 1896 only by implication ("assisted at your first Mass at the Carmel"); the date of the Mass is the same as his first Communion anniversary in her reading.

### F-OW-462 — To her aunt: "a poor cicada" who is "more mischievous every day" after nearly nine years in Carmel
- Claim: To Mme Guérin, on her aunt's feast (16 Nov 1896), Thérèse jokes that she cannot find the words for her heart. She mocks Boileau's line ("what is well conceived is clearly said") and cites "the profound Father Faber" for the idea that full hearts shut themselves in. She says the letters of her "four amiable sisters" will contradict her, but she loves her aunt as much. She asks her aunt to pray that she grow "in wisdom like the Divine Child Jesus", "which I do not; ask our dear little Marie de l'Eucharistie, she will tell you I do not lie; day by day I become more mischievous, and yet I have been nearly nine years in the house of the Lord". She adds that she should be "advanced in the ways of perfection", but is "still at the bottom of the ladder", not discouraged, and "as cheerful as the cicada"; she sings always, hoping to share the riches of her sisters, "much more generous than the ant". She will sing her "finest tune" on her aunt's feast day and her aunt's mother's ("Bonne-Maman"). She asks Francis, Jeanne and Léonie (the La Néele cousins and Léonie) to embrace her aunt.
- When / where: 16 Nov 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mme Guérin (aunt); M. Guérin (uncle); Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin); Francis and Jeanne La Néele; Léonie; Father Frederick Faber (author; the letter says "le profond Père Faber")
- Source: LT 202 (to Mme Guérin, 16 Nov 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-202-a-mme-guerin-16-novembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « de jour en jour, je deviens plus maligne, et cependant il y a bientôt neuf ans que je suis dans la maison du Seigneur » ; « je suis aussi gaie que la cigale, comme elle je chante toujours »
- Translation: "Day by day I become more mischievous, and yet it will soon be nine years that I have been in the house of the Lord." ; "I am as cheerful as the cicada; like her I always sing."
- New to English readers: yes ("cigale" and "Père Faber" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She mentions Boileau without naming him ("le poète"); our identification is from outside the letter. Father Faber (English spiritual writer) appears here as a known author for her, and this is an addition to the list of her readings. "Four sisters" in Carmel: Pauline, Marie, Céline and herself, with cousin Marie Guérin as the fifth. The letter is a good picture of her playful tone in a month when her illness was quiet.

### F-OW-463 — December 1896: "Jesus wants us two cherubs, not two foundresses", and a flight to Saigon
- Claim: To Sr Geneviève (Céline), a note dated "December (?) 1896" comforts "poor D." because "Mr T." "was caught in the trap". When he has wings, no snares will catch him or her. Together, with "small white wings", they will fly "high and far" and "even shake our little silver wings as far as Saigon". "That is all we can do best for him, because Jesus wants us as two cherubs and not as two foundresses, at this moment for certain; if he changes his mind we will change ours too, that's all."
- When / where: December (?) 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Céline (Sr Geneviève, recipient); "Mr T." and "D." (the letter uses initials; not decoded)
- Source: LT 207 (to Sr Geneviève, Dec (?) 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-207-a-soeur-genevieve-decembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « c'est Jésus qui nous veut deux chérubins et non deux fondatrices, en ce moment c'est certain, s'Il change d'idée nous en changerons aussi voilà tout !... »
- Translation: "It is Jesus who wants us two cherubs and not two foundresses. At this moment that is certain. If he changes his mind, we will change ours, and that is all."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911 edition) says that two of her sisters were asked for by the Saigon Carmel; see the earlier F-OW cards on Saigon (Mère Agnès nearly sent, 2 Aug 1896) and the wish to go to Hanoi (Carnet jaune).
- New to English readers: yes ("Saïgon" here and "deux fondatrices" ABSENT from the 1911 letters)
- Confidence: medium. The letter does not say who "Mr T." is, nor what the trap was. Its reference to Saigon and to "two foundresses" is clear.
- Notes: Read with the Carmel's plan to send sisters to Saigon/Hanoi in 1896: this note shows Céline and Thérèse were at least talked of as possible "foundresses" and that Thérèse took the refusal calmly. The letter says nothing about her health.

### F-OW-464 — Two notes to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph: "close your eyes", the "little A." who shows Heaven from the boat
- Claim: LT 205 (Dec 1896): "How ugly to spend one's time fretting instead of falling asleep on the Heart of Jesus!" If night frightens the little child and he complains that he cannot see "the One who carries him", let him "close his eyes", make the sacrifice asked of him, and wait for sleep. "Is it too much to ask of the little child to close his eyes, not to fight against the chimeras of the night?" He should believe that Jesus carries him, and drop "the sterile fear of being unfaithful (a fear that does not suit a child)". It is signed "(An ambassador.)". LT 206: "The little A. has no wish to jump from the boat" (the "nacelle"), "but he is there to show Heaven to the little Child". She hopes to see the child give up "consolations too childish and unworthy of a missionary and a warrior". "I love my p.E... very much, and Jesus loves him even more."
- When / where: December (?) 1896 (both), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph (recipient)
- Source: LT 205 and LT 206 (to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-205-a-soeur-marie-de-saint-joseph-decembre-1896/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-206-a-soeur-marie-de-saint-joseph-decembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « qu'il ferme les yeux, qu'il fasse volontairement le sacrifice qui lui est demandé » ; « la crainte stérile d'être infidèle (crainte qui ne convient pas à un enfant) »
- Translation: "Let him close his eyes and willingly make the sacrifice he is asked for." ; "The sterile fear of being unfaithful (a fear that does not suit a child)."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; the "little child" and "little A." are private code names for the recipient's state; the letters do not decode them.
- Notes: She gave this advice to another sister while, by her own later dating in Ms C, her own trial of faith had already begun (Easter 1896). The letters do not say what the recipient feared.

### F-OW-465 — "Mr Toto" notes to Céline: the foot-warmer, the clapper, and being woken for the Hours
- Claim: Four tiny notes to Céline, winter 1896–97, use a male code name. LT 208 asks Sr Geneviève to spare "poor Mr" his foot-warmer (chaufferette) tomorrow, and to see that he is woken for the Hours: "the waker" is used to seeing "the young lady come to curry-comb Mr each morning to draw him gently from his dreams." She tells the "poor young lady, obliged to carry little pots left and right" that one day Jesus "will come and go to serve" her. LT 209: "Do not forget to wake Mr T. tomorrow, poor Miss L., humbled by everyone, but loved by Jesus and Mr T." LT 210: "Would you look tomorrow morning whether Mr Toto heard the clapper (matraque)?" LT 214 (3 Jan 1897 (?)): "Monsieu Toto souète une bonne fête a madmoisel Lili" (childish spelling) on a small chromo card. Sr Geneviève's note says the card was "offered to me by a baby, in a chromo in high relief, with a little flower".
- When / where: winter 1896–97 (LT 208–210), 3 Jan 1897 (?) (LT 214), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Céline (Sr Geneviève, "Miss L./Lili", recipient); Thérèse (apparently "Mr T./Toto"; the notes do not decode the name)
- Source: LT 208, 209, 210, 214 (to Sr Geneviève), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-208-a-soeur-genevieve-decembre-1896/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-209-a-soeur-genevieve-hiver-1896/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-210-a-soeur-genevieve-hiver-1896/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-214-a-soeur-genevieve-3-janvier-1897/
- Source type: own-writing (LT 214: with a note by Sr Geneviève)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « veiller à ce qu'il soit réveillé pour les Heures » ; « si Mr Toto a entendu la matraque ?... »
- Translation: "See that he is woken for the Hours." ; "Did Mr Toto hear the clapper?"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium. The words are clear; the identification of "Mr Toto" with Thérèse and of "the waker" with Céline is our reading and the notes do not state it.
- Notes: The "matraque" is a wooden clapper used as a signal in the convent (outside knowledge; the note does not explain). These notes show the sisters' domestic code and Thérèse's need for a foot-warmer in winter 1896–97, which fits the reports of her cold, but the notes do not say she was ill.

### F-OW-466 — To Mère Agnès in Dec 1896: "the donkey of little Jesus" and "the little trade of the shop"
- Claim: In LT 203 (4 Dec 1896) she tells Mère Agnès: "you do me more good than all the books in the world!" In LT 204 (18 Dec 1896) she writes that the Virgin is so pleased "to have a little donkey and a little servant" that she has them run left and right for her pleasure, so it is not strange that "little Mother" sometimes falls. When Jesus is grown and no longer needs to learn "the little trade of the shop", he will prepare a place for her in his kingdom "not of this world", and then "he will come and go to serve her". Many a "little nose" that Mère Agnès pulled will have to lift itself to look at "her who had no other ambition than to be the donkey of little Jesus".
- When / where: 4 Dec 1896 and 18 Dec 1896, Carmel of Lisieux (Advent)
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (Pauline, recipient, then a nun of the community; prioress 1893–96)
- Source: LT 203 and LT 204 (to Mère Agnès de Jésus), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-203-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-4-decembre-1896/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-204-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-18-decembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Vous me faites plus de bien que tous les livres du monde !... » ; « celle qui n'eut d'autre ambition que d'être l'âne du petit Jésus »
- Translation: "You do me more good than all the books in the world!" ; "She who had no ambition other than to be the little donkey of little Jesus."
- New to English readers: yes ("petit âne" and "métier de la boutique" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; low for who is the "little donkey and little servant" (LT 204 speaks of Mère Agnès, not of Thérèse).
- Notes: These are two very short notes in a Christmas-time series. The "little donkey" image is one Thérèse used in her Christmas 1896 writings (see the later cards for LT 208–212 and her 1896 Christmas poem).

### F-OW-467 — Christmas 1896: a letter "from the Virgin" to Céline "who has no shelter"
- Claim: For Christmas 1896 Thérèse writes to Céline in the voice of the Virgin Mary; the envelope reads "Sent by the Holy Virgin to my dear child with no shelter in a foreign land". Mary says Céline gives joy to her heart and her Jesus's, but "you do not know, you do not see, and your soul is in sadness". She says she knows the price of "anguish of heart" and recalls St Joseph returning sadly "without having found an inn". She asks Céline to bear "in peace the trial of not pleasing yourself" and to give Mary "a sweet shelter". Jesus loves her the more she is poor and "prefers to see you stumble at night on the stones of the road than walk in full day on a road covered with flowers that could slow your steps". It closes: "I am your Mother, your dear Mamma. (Mary, Queen of the little angels.)"
- When / where: 24 Dec 1896 (Christmas 1896), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Céline (Sr Geneviève, recipient); Mary and St Joseph in the voice
- Source: LT 211 (to Sr Geneviève, 24 Dec 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-211-a-soeur-genevieve-24-decembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Il aime mieux te voir heurter dans la nuit les pierres du chemin que marcher en plein jour sur une route émaillée de fleurs qui pourraient retarder ta marche. »
- Translation: "He prefers to see you strike the stones of the road in the night than walk in full daylight on a road covered with flowers that could slow you down."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The "no shelter" phrase shows Céline's inner sadness in Dec 1896; the letter does not say what caused it (compare earlier letters where Céline "sans asile"). The letter is written as Christmas play in the community's habit of giving each other such notes.

### F-OW-468 — Christmas 1896: "the little Jesus" plays skittles and then spins a top (to Marie de la Trinité)
- Claim: On Christmas night 1896 Thérèse writes to her novice Marie de la Trinité in the voice of the Child Jesus ("Your little Brother Jesus"). He says all year she "amused me a lot playing skittles", and the angels were surprised; a cherub asked whether the harp's melody was not more pleasing than her laugh "when you knock down a skittle with the ball of your love". He answered that her smile was sweeter, because "you could only play and smile by suffering, by forgetting yourself". Now he wants to change games and play "the top" (toupie), and she will be his top. He gives one as a model: "it is not pretty", whoever does not know how to use it will kick it away, "but a child will jump for joy". "To make the top spin, one needs whip-lashes." He asks her to let her sisters "do you this service" and be grateful to those who are most diligent "in not letting you slow down". When he has played enough he will take her "up there" to play "without suffering".
- When / where: 24 Dec 1896 (Christmas night 1896), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de la Trinité (recipient); the Child Jesus (voice)
- Source: LT 212 (to Sr Marie de la Trinité, 24 Dec 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-212-a-soeur-marie-de-la-trinite-24-decembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il faut des coups de fouet... Eh bien ! laisse tes soeurs te rendre ce service »
- Translation: "To make the top spin, you need the whip... So let your sisters do you this service."
- Other sources: The "ball" is her image of 1887 (see the "little ball of the Child Jesus" card). This is the top image in a letter of the same Christmas.
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The note shows how she framed correction among the novices. The letter does not say what the sisters' "whip-lashes" were.

### F-OW-469 — Second letter to Bellière: Advent rule, his "sacrifice", and "I am not an angel"
- Claim: On 26 Dec 1896 Thérèse answers Bellière's letter and explains her delay. The Carmel rule forbids writing or receiving letters in Advent, but the prioress allowed her, as an exception, to read his letter "because you needed to be supported by prayer". She says Jesus sometimes lets us feel "the bitterness of the chalice" and quotes Gethsemane. She says: "the greatest honour God can do a soul is not to give it much but to ask much of it" (she attributes it to "a Saint"). His part is "beautiful": Jesus wants him to begin his mission by suffering. She says "the martyrdom of the heart is no less fruitful than the shedding of blood". She writes that "we have only the one day of this life to save souls", the next is eternity. She pictures the pain of "those you are about to leave" (his mother and family), and Mary at the foot of the cross. The prioress is still ill, "a little better these last days", and sends him a holy card of St Francis of Assisi "who will teach you how to find joy in the trials and combats of life". She asks his prayers, because she is "not an angel as you seem to think, but a poor little Carmelite very imperfect".
- When / where: 26 Dec 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient; preparing to leave for the missions, from the letter's wording); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress, ill); St Francis of Assisi (holy card)
- Source: LT 213 (to l'abbé Bellière, 26 Dec 1896), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-213-a-labbe-belliere-26-decembre-1896/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « la règle du Carmel ne permet pas d'écrire ni de recevoir des lettres pendant le temps de l'avent » ; « le martyre du coeur n'est pas moins fécond que l'effusion du sang » ; « moi qui ne suis pas un ange comme vous paraissez le croire »
- Translation: "The Carmel rule does not allow writing or receiving letters during Advent." ; "The martyrdom of the heart is no less fruitful than the shedding of blood." ; "I am not an angel, as you seem to think."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The letter is the first proof in her own hand of the Advent letter ban. The sacrifice Bellière faced is not spelled out in her letter (it seems to be leaving his mother; she says "those you are about to leave"). It says nothing of Bellière's military service (one letter says "l'année militaire" affected another seminarian, see LT 201 post-script).

### F-OW-470 — 9 Jan 1897: tears of Mère Agnès and "if there is a Heaven, it is for me"
- Claim: To Mère Agnès (9 Jan 1897): "if you knew how touched I am to see how much you love me!" She hopes to go "up there" soon. Since "If there is a Heaven, it is for me", she will be rich and "will have all the treasures of the good God", and will repay Mère Agnès a hundredfold. It hurts her to "always receive without ever giving". She was sorry to see Mère Agnès's tears but glad of "the good effect they produced", which "was magic". She says she blames nobody "when my little Mother is looked at sideways", since the sisters are "only instruments set at an angle by Jesus himself" so that Mère Agnès's way "resembles the one he chose for himself" when his face was hidden. Mère Agnès is "not an object of contempt, but few recognise her since Jesus hid his face". She compares her to the swallow that leads her companions to their new homeland.
- When / where: 9 Jan 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (Pauline, recipient); the community
- Source: LT 216 (to Mère Agnès de Jésus, 9 Jan 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-216-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-9-janvier-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Puisque «S'il y a un Ciel, il est pour moi», je serai riche » ; « les soeurs ne sont que des instruments posés de travers par Jésus lui-même »
- Translation: "Since 'if there is a Heaven, it is for me', I shall be rich." ; "The sisters are only instruments set crooked by Jesus himself."
- New to English readers: yes ("S'il y a un Ciel, il est pour moi" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words. The cause of Mère Agnès's tears is not given: the letter refers to an event ("the tears", "the good effect") without describing it.
- Notes: The quotation marks around "S'il y a un Ciel..." show that she quotes someone (the source does not say whom).

### F-OW-471 — January 1897: "the little trade of the shop", and the Wandering Jew's song about death
- Claim: LT 217 (Jan (?) 1897, to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph): "Charming, the little verses". It is ugly "to go begging at others' doors when one's purse is full"; sleeping, being gentle and cheerful is "the little trade of the shop", which "must never close, not even on Sundays and feasts", "the days Jesus keeps to try our souls". She tells the sister to sing "like a finch"; she herself "like a poor little sparrow groans in my corner, singing like the Wandering Jew: Death can do nothing to me, I see it well!" She asks about "the famous tablecloth" (the nappe): is it still in question?
- When / where: January (?) 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph (recipient)
- Source: LT 217 (to Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph, Jan (?) 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-217-a-soeur-marie-de-saint-joseph-janvier-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « moi comme un pauvre petit passereau je gémis dans mon coin, chantant comme le Juif errant : La mort ne me peut rien, je m'en aperçois bien !... »
- Translation: "I, like a poor little sparrow, groan in my corner, singing like the Wandering Jew: Death cannot touch me, I see that well!"
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words; the reference is to a folk song (outside knowledge: "Le Juif errant" song).
- Notes: This shows her joking about death in January 1897, three months before Easter 1897 and the worsening of her illness. The sparrow "groaning in my corner" is a self-picture in the winter of her trial.

### F-OW-472 — To Frère Siméon (Rome), 27 Jan 1897: "my course here below will not be long"
- Claim: Thérèse joins Céline in thanking "Monsieur le Directeur", Frère Siméon, for "the precious favour you obtained for our Carmel" (the letter does not say what). She is sad to hear his health has been shaken. She hopes he will stay in exile "so that the immense weight of your merits may make up for other souls who will appear before God with empty hands". She writes: "I believe my course here below will not be long. When I appear before my beloved Spouse I will have only my desires to offer him." If he goes first, she hopes he will meet her and present his works for her. She recalls he obtained the blessing of Leo XIII for them "so many times". She asks only the grace "to love Jesus and to make him loved". She promises: "I do not wait for Heaven to make this prayer."
- When / where: 27 Jan 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Frère Siméon (Christian Brother, Rome; "Monsieur le Directeur"); Céline (Sr Geneviève); Leo XIII
- Source: LT 218 (to Frère Siméon, 27 Jan 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-218-au-frere-simeon-27-janvier-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je crois que ma course ici-bas ne sera pas longue... lorsque je paraîtrai devant mon Epoux Bien-Aimé je n'aurai que mes désirs à Lui présenter »
- Translation: "I believe my course here below will not be long. When I appear before my beloved Spouse, I shall have only my desires to offer him."
- Other sources: The "empty hands" idea is in her Act of Oblation (June 1895). Ms A (1911) says her father visited "the venerable Brother Siméon" a few days after 20 Nov 1887.
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 letters)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: "I believe my course will not be long" (Jan 1897) is one of her earliest dated statements that she expected to die soon after Good Friday 1896. The favour obtained is not named in this letter.

### F-OW-473 — 22 Feb 1897: Mère Agnès's "broken nose", a joke about noses
- Claim: In a note to Mère Agnès (22 Feb 1897) Thérèse thanks her and jokes: "Your nose has been broken, yes, but it IS long! You will always have enough left, whereas mine, if I had it broken, there would be none left." "How happy we are to know how to laugh at everything!"
- When / where: 22 Feb 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (Pauline)
- Source: LT 219 (to Mère Agnès de Jésus, 22 Feb 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-219-a-mere-agnes-de-jesus-22-fevrier-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « votre nez a été cassé, oui mais !! il EST LONG !... Il vous en restera toujours assez, au lieu que le mien si je le faisais casser, il ne m'en resterait plus !... »
- Translation: "Your nose has been broken, yes, but it IS long! You will always have enough left, while mine, if it were broken, there would be nothing left."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium. The note does not say whether the "broken nose" is literal or a figure (for instance a snub from the community).
- Notes: A very short sisterly joke. It shows how the two sisters used physical jokes in a hard period. It does not describe an event.

## Appended block D — letters LT 220–228 (Feb – May 1897), continued numbering

### F-OW-474 — Feb 1897 to Bellière: "my job as sacristan", a poem copy, and a godmother's choice of names
- Claim: On Wednesday evening 24 Feb 1897 ("before entering the silence of holy Lent") Thérèse adds a note to the prioress's letter to Abbé Bellière. She says he will get more of her poems, at the prioress's order, only in some weeks: "I have few free moments, even on Sunday, because of my job as sacristan (sacristine)". She says the poems reveal "not what I am, but what I would like and ought to be"; she looked "at the depth more than the form" and did not always keep the rules of verse; her aim was to put down "the feelings of the Carmelite" to answer her sisters' wishes. She accepts to be godmother of "the first child you will have the joy of baptising" and chooses protectors for her future godchild: the Virgin, St Joseph and St Maurice, "patron of my dear little brother". She gives him a short prayer to say daily for her: "Merciful Father, in the name of our sweet Jesus, of the Virgin Mary and the saints, I ask you to set my sister on fire with your Spirit of Love and to grant her the grace to make you much loved." She asks him for "the memorable dates of your life" to thank God with him. She notes he is engaged to the Lamb and will become his "spouse" at ordination to the subdiaconate.
- When / where: 24 Feb 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress); St Maurice (Bellière's patron)

### F-OW-475 — "If I go to Heaven soon": no more grilles, "my soul will fly with you to the far missions"
- Claim: In the same letter to Bellière she writes that he must not regret having "a sister who seems to want to go and enjoy eternal rest and leave you to work alone". She says the only thing she wants is God's will, and "if in Heaven I could no longer work for his glory, I would prefer exile to the homeland". "I do not know the future. Yet if Jesus makes my forebodings come true, I promise to stay your little sister Up There. Our union, far from being broken, will become closer. Then there will be no more enclosure, no more grilles, and my soul will be able to fly with you to the distant missions. Our roles will stay the same: to you the apostolic arms, to me prayer and love." She jokes that he will live longer, so "it is not permitted to you to sing as I do: 'I hope my exile will be short!...'" but he must keep his promise to pray for her all his life.
- When / where: 24 Feb 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient)
- Source: LT 220 (to l'abbé Bellière, 24 Feb 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-220-a-labbe-belliere-24-fevrier-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « si dans le Ciel je ne pouvais plus travailler pour sa gloire, j'aimerais mieux l'exil que la patrie » ; « alors il n'y aura plus de clôture, plus de grilles et mon âme pourra voler avec vous dans les lointaines missions »
- Translation: "If in Heaven I could no longer work for his glory, I would rather have exile than the homeland." ; "Then there will be no more enclosure, no more grilles, and my soul will be able to fly with you to the distant missions."
- Other sources: Same idea in LT 193 (30 Jul 1896 to Roulland) and in the Carnet jaune (17 Jul 1897, "I want to spend my heaven doing good on earth").
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 letters)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is a dated statement seven weeks before Easter 1897 that she expects to die soon ("mes pressentiments"). It says she would prefer to stay on earth if she could not work from Heaven, which is close to the Carnet jaune saying. The letter does not mention her illness.

### F-OW-476 — The lobster that "was possessed": a Carmel kitchen story sent to Roulland
- Claim: In LT 221 she tells Roulland that "Carmel, like Sichuan, is a country foreign to the world, where one loses the most basic customs". A charitable person sent a small lobster "well tied in a hamper" (bourriche). It had long been since such a marvel was seen in the monastery. The sister cook remembered that it must be put in water to cook. She did so "groaning at having to exercise such cruelty on an innocent creature". The lobster "seemed asleep", but at the first heat "its gentleness changed to fury" and it jumped into the middle of the kitchen because the cook had not put a lid on the pot. The cook, armed with tongs, chased it. She went "in tears" to the prioress, declaring that the lobster "was possessed by the devil". The prioress laughed, went to the kitchen, took the lobster ("who had made no vow of obedience, made some resistance"), put it back in its "prison" and shut the door "that is, the lid". That evening at recreation "the whole community laughed to tears", and next day each sister tasted a mouthful. Thérèse says the story "will serve us more than once as a feast, not in the refectory but at recreation".
- When / where: told 19 Mar 1897; the event is "recently" (early 1897), Carmel of Lisieux kitchen
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress); the sister cook (unnamed); the community
- Source: LT 221 (to P. Roulland, 19 Mar 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-221-au-p-roulland-19-mars-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « le homard est endiablé » ; « n'ayant pas fait voeu d'obéissance fit quelque résistance »
- Translation: "The lobster is possessed." ; "Having made no vow of obedience, it put up some resistance."
- New to English readers: yes ("homard" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: A funny scene that shows Carmel diet (a lobster was a rarity), the cook's fear, the prioress's humour and community recreation. The sister cook is not named. The story is an answer to Roulland's own funny story about his cook breaking a pot.

### F-OW-477 — 19 Mar 1897: she would go to Hanoi if Jesus did not "come for her"; the prioress says "the scabbard would be thrown into the sea"
- Claim: Thérèse tells Roulland she has read the life of Théophane Vénard and was "interested and touched more than I can say"; she composed some verses (PN 47), and the next-to-last verse says that "with happiness I would leave for Tonkin if God called me there". She says it is not a dream: "if Jesus does not come soon to fetch me for the Carmel of Heaven, I will one day leave for the Carmel of Hanoi", which the Saigon Carmel founded recently. She says vocations there are rare and superiors often do not let go the sisters. The prioress in her youth had been prevented by her superior from going to help the Saigon Carmel. She reports that at the end of the year thieves entered the Hanoi monastery and took the prioress's crucifix from beside her pillow and the community's small money, and that the French Carmels collected to build a wall. The prioress believes in her vocation but not that it can be realised: "the scabbard must be as strong as the sword", and "the scabbard would be thrown into the sea before reaching Tonkin". She calls the body "frère l'âne, as St Francis called it". She expects that "Jesus will treat me like a little lazy one" and asks Roulland not to pray to free her from purgatory but to pray that she can "still make him loved" after death.
- When / where: 19 Mar 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland (recipient); Théophane Vénard; Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress); the Carmels of Saigon and Hanoi; St Francis of Assisi; St Teresa of Avila
- Source: LT 221 (to P. Roulland, 19 Mar 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-221-au-p-roulland-19-mars-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je partirai un jour pour celui d'Hanoï » ; « il faudrait pour cela que le fourreau soit aussi solide que l'épée » ; « ce misérable frère l'âne, comme l'appelait saint François d'Assise »
- Translation: "I will leave one day for the Carmel of Hanoi." ; "For that the scabbard would have to be as strong as the sword." ; "That miserable brother donkey, as St Francis of Assisi called him."
- Other sources: The Carnet jaune records the Hanoi wish "if the Virgin cures me" (see the earlier F-OW card). Ms A (1911) says two of her sisters were asked for by Saigon. The Hanoi foundation is dated from the Saigon Carmel in the letter.
- New to English readers: yes ("frère l'âne", "Fleuve Bleu" and the thieves story ABSENT from the 1911 edition; the Hanoi wish itself is in the 1911 edition in Ms C form)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The letter shows the health problem in concrete words ("the scabbard"), written 2 weeks before her first fever note (LT 222 bis). It says the prioress's view; we do not have the prioress's own words. The letter gives the Hanoi burglary as news from Tonkin; it gives no date except "the end of last year".

### F-OW-478 — "Whatever it costs to stay in purgatory until the end of the world": Teresa's saying, and "Céline and Thérèse" for Chinese girls
- Claim: In LT 221 she asks Roulland not to pray that she be spared purgatory, and quotes St Teresa: "What does it matter to me to stay in purgatory until the end of the world if by my prayers I save one soul!" She says she wants to "save souls and forget myself for them", even after death. She tells him to choose "Mary" over "Thérèse" for a girl he baptises, since Chinese women do not want two protectors, so the Virgin wins. Later, when he baptises many, she asks him to name two little sisters "Céline and Thérèse", "the names we bore in the world". She says Céline, nearly four years older, "came to join me after closing the eyes of our good father", and does not know the "intimate relations" Thérèse has with him. She says that Céline told her lately her wish that "through you Céline and Thérèse go and live again in China". She says the Carmel of Lisieux calls him "the missionary of our Mother".
- When / where: 19 Mar 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland; Céline (Sr Geneviève); St Teresa of Avila
- Source: LT 221 (to P. Roulland, 19 Mar 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-221-au-p-roulland-19-mars-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Que m'importe à moi de rester jusqu'à la fin du monde en purgatoire si par mes prières je sauve une seule âme! » ; « elle me disait dernièrement son désir que, par vous, Céline et Thérèse aillent revivre en Chine »
- Translation: "What does it matter to me to stay in purgatory until the end of the world if by my prayers I save one single soul!" ; "She told me lately her wish that through you Céline and Thérèse go and live again in China."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Céline did not know the full extent of the Roulland link (she knew only that Thérèse and Céline speak of "our Mother's missionary"). The letter names the prioress as the one who gave Roulland. The request to name Chinese girls "Céline and Thérèse" is a sweet, checkable detail (whether he did is not recorded here).

### F-OW-481 — Easter 1897: "Dear little brother", Colombière and Margaret Mary, and the rule of secrecy
- Claim: On Easter Sunday 1897 ("Alleluia") Thérèse tells Bellière her pen "or rather my heart refuses to call you 'Monsieur l'Abbé' any more". The prioress has allowed her to use the name she uses when she speaks of him to Jesus: "dear little brother". She says Jesus united their souls to work for sinners as he united Père de la Colombière and Margaret Mary, and copies a passage from Margaret Mary's Life in which Jesus shows her his Heart as a furnace and two other hearts uniting with it. Jesus tells Margaret Mary they should be "like brother and sister, equally sharing spiritual goods", and "the infinite riches of my Heart will make up for everything". She adds that he is not yet a Colombière, "but I do not doubt that one day you will be a true apostle", and that she does not compare herself to Margaret Mary. She says she sent him her poems and that "Vivre d'Amour" arrived twice by mistake. She asks whether their relations stay secret: "Nobody except your director must know the union Jesus has formed between our souls."
- When / where: 25 Apr 1897 (Easter Sunday), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière; Mère Marie de Gonzague (gave permission); Claude de la Colombière; Margaret Mary Alacoque
- Source: LT 224 (to l'abbé Bellière, 25 Apr 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-224-a-labbe-belliere-25-avril-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words and the quoted Life); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Ma plume ou plutôt mon coeur se refuse à vous appeler désormais «monsieur l'Abbé» » ; « Personne excepté votre Directeur ne doit connaître l'union que Jésus a formée entre nos âmes »
- Translation: "My pen, or rather my heart, refuses to call you 'Monsieur l'Abbé' from now on." ; "Nobody except your director may know the union that Jesus has formed between our souls."
- New to English readers: yes ("Colombière" ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The letter calls the poem "Vivre d'Amour" one she had already sent, and "Mes Armes" (PN 48, 25 Mar 1897) a poem he smiled at. It says "the prioress said I could" for the first use of "petit frère". The letter's postscript on secrecy explains why the two were not known to the whole Carmel.

### F-OW-482 — Joan of Arc in her own words: "a sweeter, stronger voice" that called her "to make the King of Heaven loved"
- Claim: Answering Bellière, who smiled at her poem "Mes Armes", Thérèse says she dreamed in childhood of fighting on battlefields. When she began to learn the history of France, "the account of the exploits of Joan of Arc enchanted me; I felt in my heart the desire and the courage to imitate her; it seemed to me that the Lord destined me too for great things." She was not mistaken. But instead of heavenly voices calling her to battle, "I heard in the depth of my soul a sweeter, stronger voice, that of the Spouse of virgins, who called me to other exploits, to more glorious conquests, and in the solitude of Carmel I understood that my mission was not to have a mortal king crowned but to make the King of Heaven loved, to subject to him the kingdom of hearts."
- When / where: 25 Apr 1897 (about her childhood), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Joan of Arc; Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient)
- Source: LT 224 (to l'abbé Bellière, 25 Apr 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-224-a-labbe-belliere-25-avril-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « j'ai, dans mon enfance, rêvé de combattre sur les champs de bataille... le récit des exploits de Jeanne d'Arc me ravissait » ; « ma mission n'était pas de faire couronner un roi mortel mais de faire aimer le Roi du Ciel, de lui soumettre le royaume des coeurs »
- Translation: "In my childhood I dreamed of fighting on battlefields... the story of Joan of Arc's exploits delighted me." ; "My mission was not to have a mortal king crowned but to make the King of Heaven loved, to bring the kingdom of hearts under him."
- Other sources: Her plays "Jeanne d'Arc accomplissant sa mission" (RP 1, 21 Jan 1894) and "Jeanne d'Arc martyre" (RP 3, 1895), where she played Joan; the 1911 Ms C says she will not fight "on battlefields" but by prayer.
- New to English readers: yes ("faire couronner un roi mortel" and "aimer le Roi du Ciel" ABSENT from the 1911 letters)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is her own summary, given a few months before her death, of what Joan meant to her: a childhood model whose call she reinterpreted. The letter says she learned Joan's story when she started French history, but gives no year.

### F-OW-483 — "It is not humility": she says God "has done great things in me" and hopes her exile will be short
- Claim: In LT 224 Thérèse tells Bellière that one thing in his letter hurt her: "you do not know me as I really am." She asks him to believe that God gave him for a sister "not a great soul but a very small and very imperfect one". Then she adds: "Do not think it is humility that keeps me from recognising God's gifts; I know he has done great things in me, and I sing it every day with joy." Whoever has been forgiven more must love more, so she tries "to make my life an act of love" and no longer worries about being a small soul; "on the contrary, I rejoice in it". So she hopes "my exile will be short", but "not because I am ready; I feel I never will be, unless the Lord himself transforms me. He can do it in an instant; after all the graces he has given me I still await that one from his infinite mercy." Bellière asked her to ask for martyrdom for him; she says she has often asked it for herself but is not worthy, and "since the Lord seems to want to give me only the martyrdom of love, I hope he will allow me, through you, to gather the other palm". She adds that she is sending "the memorable dates of my life" so they can be united on those days.
- When / where: 25 Apr 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Abbé Maurice Bellière (recipient)
- Source: LT 224 (to l'abbé Bellière, 25 Apr 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-224-a-labbe-belliere-25-avril-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Ne croyez pas que ce soit l'humilité qui m'empêche de reconnaître les dons du bon Dieu, je sais qu'Il a fait en moi de grandes choses et je le chante chaque jour avec bonheur. » ; « Puisque le Seigneur semble ne vouloir m'accorder que le martyre de l'amour »
- Translation: "Do not think it is humility that prevents me from recognising the gifts of the good God; I know he has done great things in me, and I sing it every day with joy." ; "Since the Lord seems to want to grant me only the martyrdom of love."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: This is her own answer to being called "a great soul" and links the "Magnificat" line ("he has done great things in me") with the "little way". She uses "mon exil sera court" twice in these letters (LT 220, 224).

### F-OW-484 — Letter to Sr Anne du Sacré-Cœur of the Saigon Carmel
- Claim: On 2 May 1897 (the feast of the Good Shepherd), Thérèse writes to a former sister of the Lisieux Carmel who is now at Saigon. The prioress said she could write "if it gives you pleasure". She says: "the ardent sun of Saigon is nothing compared to the fire that burns in your soul." She asks Sr Anne to ask Jesus that she may love and make him loved "not with an ordinary love but like the saints who did mad things for him". She asks that she always do his will: "I am ready to cross the world... and I am also ready to die!" She sends respects to Sr Anne's prioress, who "does not know me, but I often hear our good Mother speak of her". The "silence" would end soon, so she stops.
- When / where: 2 May 1897 (Feast of the Good Shepherd), Carmel of Lisieux (she says she uses "the licence of the Good Shepherd", a feast-day permission to talk and write)
- People: Sr Anne du Sacré-Cœur (recipient, nun of the Carmel of Saigon, formerly of Lisieux); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress); the prioress of Saigon (unnamed)
- Source: LT 225 (to Sr Anne du Sacré-Cœur, 2 May 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-225-a-soeur-anne-du-sacre-coeur-2-mai-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown

- Translation: "The burning sun of Saigon is nothing compared to the fire that burns in your soul." ; "I am ready to cross the world... and I am also ready to die!"

- Notes: The letter shows the prioress allowing a letter to an overseas Carmel just after the Hanoi wish (LT 221) and that Thérèse used the feast-day "licence" to write. It does not mention Sr Anne's health or her mission.

### F-OW-485 — 9 May 1897 to Roulland: "Fr Mazel" and "the small zero next to the one"
- Claim: Thérèse has received "with emotion" relics that Roulland sent, and says his letter "is almost a farewell-until-Heaven letter". She quotes Théophane Vénard: "Every missionary's life is fruitful in crosses" and "True happiness is to suffer; to live we must die." He wrote: "I am still a little child who cannot talk." She says Père Mazel, "ordained priest the same day as you", could not speak either, yet "has already gathered the palm" (the letter implies he had died; she says she heard the name for the first time). She had felt drawn to invoke him. She tells Roulland that his blessing of the mortal remains of "the little soul" in his first act as a priest in China "must have smiled at you". She writes that "what consoles me is to think that at your side I can serve some purpose: the zero has no value by itself, but placed next to the one it becomes powerful, provided it puts itself on the right side, after and not before!" She asks his blessing "for the little zero that God has placed near you". She thanks him for remembering her parents at Mass; she says her parents "had so wished for a missionary son", that before her birth they hoped their wish would be fulfilled, and that through her a missionary is now their son.
- When / where: 9 May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland; Père Mazel (ordained priest the same day as Roulland); Théophane Vénard; Louis and Zélie Martin (parents)
- Source: LT 226 (to P. Roulland, 9 May 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-226-au-p-roulland-9-mai-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « le zéro par lui-même n'a pas de valeur, mais placé près de l'unité il devient puissant, pourvu toutefois qu'il se mette du bon côté, après et non pas avant !... » ; « Ils avaient tant désiré un fils missionnaire !... »
- Translation: "The zero by itself has no value, but placed next to the one it becomes powerful, provided it puts itself on the right side, after and not before!" ; "They had so wanted a missionary son!"
- Other sources: The parents' wish for a missionary son is in Zélie Martin's letters (not read here). The 1911 edition prints most of LT 226 as "Lettre VIe (1897)" without the zero image and the parents' paragraph.
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 has the main letter in the 1911 form; "zéro", "sœur d'un conquérant" and the parents' paragraph are ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high for the words. The letter does not say how Père Mazel died.
- Notes: The "missionary son" paragraph gives a rare statement, in her own hand, that her parents wished for a missionary son and hoped for one before her birth: "On m'a raconté" (someone told her). It gives no source for the story.

### F-OW-486 — "My way is all confidence and love": justice, mercy, and closing the learned book (to Roulland)
- Claim: In LT 226 she tells Roulland she does not understand why he doubts entering Heaven at once if the unbelievers killed him. She says the justice that frightens many souls "is the subject of my joy and confidence": to be just is not only to punish but to "recognise right intentions and reward virtue". She hopes as much from God's justice as from his mercy, and quotes Psalm 102 ("he knows our frailty, he remembers that we are dust"). She reasons that missionaries are "martyrs by desire and will" and that none should go to purgatory; if any trace of weakness remains, the Virgin will obtain for them "a perfect act of love". Then: "This is what I think of God's justice; my way is all confidence and love; I do not understand souls who are afraid of so tender a Friend." She writes that when she reads treatises on perfection "surrounded by a crowd of illusions", "my poor mind tires quickly, I close the learned book that breaks my head and dries my heart, and I take the Holy Scripture. Then all seems luminous... perfection seems easy: it is enough to recognise one's nothingness and abandon oneself like a child in God's arms". She adds she is glad there are "several dwellings" in God's kingdom, for she could not enter the one whose way she cannot understand.
- When / where: 9 May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Père Adolphe Roulland (recipient)
- Source: LT 226 (to P. Roulland, 9 May 1897), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-226-au-p-roulland-9-mai-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « ma voie est toute de confiance et d'amour, je ne comprends pas les âmes qui ont peur d'un si tendre Ami » ; « je ferme le savant livre qui me casse la tête et me dessèche le coeur et je prends l'Ecriture Sainte »
- Translation: "My way is all confidence and love; I do not understand souls that are afraid of so tender a Friend." ; "I close the learned book that breaks my head and dries my heart, and I take Holy Scripture."
