### F-OW-557 — 30 July 1897: the flies she would not kill, and "a glass that looks like currant liqueur": the image of her life
- Claim: The flies tormented her but she would not kill them: "I always spare them. Yet they alone gave me trouble in my illness. I have only them as enemies and since the good God recommended to forgive enemies, I am glad to find this small occasion." When a sister wanted to kill one: "What are you going to do to it?" – "Kill it." – "Oh! no, please." She showed a glass holding a very bad medicine that looked like a delicious currant liqueur: "This little glass is the image of my life. Yesterday Sr Thérèse of St Augustine said to me: 'I hope you are drinking good liqueur!' I answered: 'O my Sr Thérèse of St Aug., it is the worst I drink!' Well, my little Mother, that is what appeared to the eyes of creatures. It always seemed to them that I drank exquisite liqueurs and it was bitterness. I say bitterness, but no! for my life was not bitter, because I knew how to make my joy and sweetness of all bitterness."
- When / where: 30 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin; the sister who wanted to kill the fly
- Source: CJ 30.7.7, 9, 15, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Ce petit verre là c'est l'image de ma vie. » ; « Il leur a toujours semblé que je buvais des liqueurs exquises et c'était de l'amertume. Je dis, de l'amertume, mais non ! car ma vie n'a pas été amère, parce que j'ai su faire ma joie et ma douceur de toute amertume. »
- Translation: "This little glass is the image of my life." ; "It always seemed to them that I drank exquisite liqueurs, and it was bitterness. I say bitterness, but no! for my life was not bitter, because I knew how to make my joy and my sweetness from all bitterness."
- Other sources: Novissima Verba has the flies and the "liqueur/cordial" passage (keyword hits).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The glass is a real object: she had a medicine coloured like currant liqueur. The saying is one of the few where she reflects on how others saw her.

### F-OW-558 — 30 July 1897: the Saigon fan, the fruit-and-saints "fanning", and "always the least, Mamma?"
- Claim: On 30 July she had been given a fan (an "écran") that came from the Carmel of Saigon; she used it to chase the flies. As it was very hot she turned toward the pictures pinned to the bed curtain and fanned them: "I fan the saints in my place; I fan you to do you good and because you are saints too." M. de Cornière had said to give her 5 or 6 spoons of Tisserand water; she asked Sr Geneviève to give only 5, then, turning to Mère Agnès: "Always the least, isn't it, Mamma?" She asked Mère Agnès to make Dr de Cornière a picture with the words "What you have done to the least of mine, you have done to me." She said: "Do not tell M. Ducellier I have only a few days left; I am not yet weak to die, and after that when one lives one is quite 'capot' (fool)."
- When / where: 30 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); Sr Geneviève; Dr de Cornière; Abbé Ducellier (her confessor)
- Source: CJ 30.7.10–13, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « J'évente les saints en place de moi ; je vous évente pour vous faire du bien et parce que vous êtes des saintes aussi. » ; « Toujours le moins, est-ce pas, maman ? »
- Translation: "I fan the saints in my place; I fan you to do you good and because you are saints too." ; "Always the least, isn't it, Mamma?"
- New to English readers: partial (Novissima Verba has the Saigon fan and the picture for Dr de Cornière; "Toujours le moins" not found)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The fan is a concrete link with the Saigon Carmel (see the letters cards on Saigon and Hanoi). The doctor's dose ("5 or 6 spoons of Tisserand water") is a treatment detail. The word "capot" is left untranslated here; its sense is uncertain.

### F-OW-559 — 30 July 1897: extreme unction: "I think of nothing", "like a child just baptised", the swallowed skins
- Claim: On 30 July she said to Mère Agnès: "Prepare me for Extreme Unction." With a smile: "I think of nothing! Pray the good God that I receive it as well as one can receive it." She told what "Our Father" (the priest) said to her before the ceremony: "'You will be like a little child who has just received baptism.' Then he spoke to me only of love. Oh! how touched I was!" She showed her hands respectfully after the anointing. Mère Agnès used to collect the little dry skins of her lips; that day she said: "I swallow my little skins today because I have received Extreme Unction and holy Viaticum." In the afternoon, after a short thanksgiving, several sisters came to talk to her; she said in the evening: "How people came to disturb me after Communion! They looked under my nose... but so as not to be irritated I thought of Our Lord who withdrew into solitude and could not prevent the crowd from following, and he did not send them away. I wanted to imitate him by receiving the sisters well."
- When / where: 30 Jul 1897, infirmary (the ceremony that day)
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); the priest ("Notre Père"; the editors name Canon Maupas); the sisters who visited
- Source: CJ 30.7.16–18, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Vous allez être comme un petit enfant qui vient de recevoir le baptême. » (the priest's words, quoted by her) ; « J'avale aujourd'hui mes petites peaux parce que j'ai reçu l'Extrême Onction et le saint Viatique. »
- Translation: "You will be like a little child who has just received baptism." ; "Today I swallow my little skins because I have received Extreme Unction and holy Viaticum."
- Other sources: Her letter LT 263 (10 Aug 1897) reports the same day: "the doctor astonished... it was time... I had this joy on the 30th". Novissima Verba has the preparation and the baptism words.
- New to English readers: partial (the swallowed skins and the sisters' disturbance are not found in Novissima Verba by keyword)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The notebook gives the priest as "Notre Père" (the Father Superior); the editors' summary names him as Canon Maupas. The "skins" of her lips are Mère Agnès's note that she used to gather them, which shows the relic habit already in July 1897.

### F-OW-560 — 31 July 1897: "Do not name a date; it will always be a feast"; "Puss in Boots"; the straw mattress is ready
- Claim: On 31 July people supposed her death might fall on a feast day such as the Transfiguration (6 Aug) or the Assumption (15 Aug). She said: "Do not speak of a date, it will always be a feast!" She told the story of "the miller and his three sons" and said "I have two boots but no sack yet! That means I am not near dying." (The editors' note: it is the story of "Puss in Boots", not a fable of La Fontaine.) The straw mattress ("paillasse") had been brought down so that her body could be laid out. She saw it through the door and cried with joy: "Ah! There is our mattress! It will be quite ready to put my corpse on... My little nose has always had luck!" She asked "How will baby do to die? What will I die of?"
- When / where: 31 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); the community
- Source: CJ 31.7.1–4, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « Ne parlez pas d'une date, ce sera toujours une fête ! » ; « Ah ! Voilà notre paillasse ! Elle va se trouver toute prête pour mettre mon cadavre. »
- Translation: "Do not speak of a date; it will always be a feast!" ; "Ah! There is our mattress! It will be quite ready to put my corpse on."
- New to English readers: partial for the feast saying (Novissima Verba mentions feast days by keyword); yes for "Puss in Boots" and the mattress joke (no match by keyword)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The mattress detail matters for the story of her death: the straw mattress was prepared in advance on which her body would be laid (compare the card on the exposure of the body after 30 Sep). The Carmel editors correct "La Fontaine" to "Puss in Boots".

### F-OW-561 — 31 July 1897: "I will be a little thief; I will take all that pleases me" and "Alleluia!"
- Claim: On 31 July she said: "Yes I will fly... Many things will disappear from Heaven that I will bring you... I will be a little thief, I will take everything that pleases me..." Looking at the statue of the Virgin and pointing to her little basin: "When it came last night (a big spitting of blood) I thought you were going to take me away!" Mère Agnès, who had fallen asleep watching her: she said: "Peter, James and John!..." (the sleeping apostles). She sang: "The Thief will come / and carry me off / Alleluia!" She said: "I thought I must be very nice and wait for the Thief very kindly." And: "Do not chat with each other, it is quite enough to look at each other." "I tell you I have a long time yet if the Blessed Virgin does not put her hand to it!"

- People: Mère Agnès (recorder); the Virgin Mary (statue)

### F-OW-562 — 31 July 1897: "I found happiness and joy on earth, but only in suffering" — the spark under the ashes
- Claim: On 31 July she said: "I have found happiness and joy on earth, but only in suffering, for I have suffered much here below; this must be made known to souls..." She explained: "Since my first Communion, since I asked Jesus to change for me into bitterness all the consolations of the earth, I had a perpetual desire to suffer. Yet I did not think of making it my joy; that is a grace given me only later. Until then it was like a spark hidden under ashes, and like the flowers of a tree that must become fruits in their time. But seeing my flowers always fall, that is, letting myself go to tears when I suffered, I said to myself with surprise and sadness: but it will never be more than desires!"
- When / where: 31 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 31.7.13, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « J'ai trouvé le bonheur et la joie sur la terre, mais uniquement dans la souffrance, car j'ai beaucoup souffert ici-bas ; il faudra le faire savoir aux âmes... » ; « c'était comme une étincelle cachée sous la cendre, et comme les fleurs d'un arbre qui doivent devenir des fruits en leur temps »
- Translation: "I have found happiness and joy on earth, but only in suffering, for I have suffered much here below; it must be made known to souls..." ; "It was like a spark hidden under the ashes, and like the flowers of a tree that must become fruit in their time."
- New to English readers: yes (keyword check: "found happiness" no match in Novissima Verba)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She asks that this be made known: a dated instruction to the recorder. The saying gives the timeline of her attitude to suffering: desire since first Communion (1884); joy in it a later grace.

### F-OW-563 — 31 July 1897: the doctor says "a month or more" the day after saying "today": "It leaves me in deep calm"; "the good God has deceived everyone"
- Claim: On 31 July she said: "This evening, when you told me that M. de Cornière thought I had a month or more, I could not believe it; it was such a great difference from yesterday when he said they had to administer me that very day! But it left me in deep calm. What does it matter to me to remain a long time on earth! If I suffer much and ever more, I have no fear; the good God will give me strength; he will not abandon me." A sister said "If you live a long time, nobody will understand anything!" She answered: "What does it matter! Everybody can well despise me; that is what I always wanted; I will have it at the end of my life!" And: "Now that the good God has done what he wanted to do, that he has deceived everybody... He will come like a thief at the hour when one no longer thinks of it; that is my little idea."
- When / where: 31 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Dr de Cornière; Mère Agnès (recorder)

Si je souffre beaucoup et toujours davantage, je n'ai point peur, le bon Dieu me donnera la force, il ne m'abandonnera pas. » ; « Tout le monde peut bien me mépriser, c'est toujours ce que j'ai désiré ; je l'aurai à la fin de ma vie ! »
- Translation: "What does it matter to me to stay a long time on earth! If I suffer much and ever more, I am not afraid; the good God will give me strength; he will not abandon me." ; "Everyone may well despise me; it is what I always wanted; I will have it at the end of my life!"

- New to English readers: partial ("month" appears in Novissima Verba by keyword; not compared)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: These lines give the doctor's swing in two days: "administer today" (30 July) and "a month or more" (31 July). Her prediction "if you live a long time nobody will understand" is a remark by a sister.

## Appended block J — poems, plays, prayers, and the Diana Vaughan link (dates checked on the Carmel pages), continued numbering

### F-OW-564 — Her profession note (Pri 2): "let no soul be lost today"
- Claim: The Carmel archive holds a note titled "Billet de profession" (profession note). It has no date on the page; the title places it at her profession, 8 Sep 1890 (other cards). In it she asks Jesus: never to let her lose "the second robe of my Baptism"; to take her "before I commit the slightest voluntary fault"; that she find and seek only him; that "creatures be nothing to me and I nothing to them"; for "peace, and also love, infinite love, with no limit other than you". She asks to die a martyr "of the heart or of the body, or rather both". She asks that she never be a burden to the community, "that no one bother about me, that I be looked at, trodden underfoot, forgotten, like a little grain of sand of yours, Jesus". She asks "that today not one soul be damned and that all the souls of purgatory be saved", and adds: "Jesus, forgive me if I say things that should not be said; I only want to please you and console you."
- When / where: 8 Sep 1890 (date from the title; no date on the page), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Jesus (addressed)
- Source: Pri 2, "Billet de Profession", https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/billet-de-profession/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « que je sois regardée foulée aux pieds oubliée comme un petit grain de sable à toi, Jésus » ; « Jésus fais que je sauve beaucoup d'âmes, qu'aujourd'hui il n'y en ait pas une seule de damnée et que toutes les âmes du purgatoire soient sauvées.... »
- Translation: "That I be looked at, trodden underfoot, forgotten, like a little grain of sand of yours, Jesus." ; "Jesus, let me save many souls; let not one be damned today and let all the souls in purgatory be saved."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition: "seconde robe de mon Baptême")
- Confidence: high for the words; medium for the date (from the title).
- Notes: It shows in one page her aims at 17: hidden life, martyrdom, souls. The "grain of sand" (petit grain de sable) is the image she used in 1889–1890 letters (see LT 95 card).

### F-OW-565 — RP 1, "The Mission of Joan of Arc" (21 Jan 1894): her first play, for Mère Agnès's first feast as prioress
- Claim: The Carmel page dates RP 1 "La Mission de Jeanne d'Arc ou La Bergère de Domrémy écoutant ses Voix" to 21 Jan 1894 and gives the occasion: the first feast of Mère Agnès as prioress (St Agnes's day). The cast list: Joan of Arc, the Archangel St Michael, St Catherine, St Margaret, Catherine d'Arc (Joan's sister), and Germaine, a young shepherdess of Domrémy. It opens with Joan alone in the meadow, guarding her flock, singing while she weaves a garland: "I, Joan the shepherdess, I cherish my flock; my crook is light and I love my spindle." The editors list nine borrowed tunes (for example "Tombé du nid", "Les Rameaux", "Les regrets de Mignon", "La plainte du mousse", and a gloss of St Teresa, "Je me meurs de ne point mourir").
- When / where: 21 Jan 1894, Carmel of Lisieux (community feast)
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (prioress; feast of St Agnes); Thérèse (author; the page does not list the actors)
- Source: RP 1, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/j-m-j-t-la-mission-de-jeanne-darc/
- Source type: own-writing (dates and occasion: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain (her text); Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Moi, Jeanne la bergère / Je chéris mon troupeau / Ma houlette est légère / Et j'aime mon fuseau. »
- Translation: "I, Joan the shepherdess, I cherish my flock; my crook is light, and I love my spindle."
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 gives a few lines of the Joan plays; the full text, cast and tunes are not in the baseline)
- Confidence: high for the date and occasion (Carmel page)
- Notes: The page does not say who acted Joan; the baseline says she played Joan in these plays. The last lines of the play (3 speakers together): "Joan of Arc, hear our vows: save France a second time!" Compare LT 224 (25 Apr 1897) and CJ 20.7.6 for her later views of Joan.

- Claim: The Carmel editors date PN 4 to 8 May 1894 and say it was for Céline (Sr Geneviève, not yet a nun). It was written in the movement for Joan's cause: a bill proposed 8 May as an annual feast of Joan of Arc, and Lisieux and its Carmel were preparing with enthusiasm.

- New to English readers: yes (the occasion)

### F-OW-567 — RP 3, "Joan of Arc fulfilling her mission" (21 Jan 1895): the long play, with 14 tunes
- Claim: The Carmel page dates RP 3 to 21 Jan 1895, the feast of the prioress, Mère Agnès de Jésus. Its subtitle is "The victories, the captivity, the martyrdom and the triumphs in Heaven of the Venerable Joan of France". The cast: Joan of Arc; St Gabriel; St Michael; St Catherine; St Margaret; King Charles VII; La Trémouille, baron of Sully; Regnault de Chartres, archbishop of Reims; Robert le Maçon; Raoul de Gaucourt; the duke of Alençon; Jean d'Aulon; Jean Massieu; Frère Martin l'Advenu (Joan's confessor); and "France". The play opens at Chinon in the king's apartment, "dressed simply like one of his favourites, without royal crown". The editors list fourteen tunes.
- When / where: 21 Jan 1895, Carmel of Lisieux (community feast)
- People: Mère Agnès de Jésus (prioress); Thérèse (author)
- Source: RP 3, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/jeanne-darc-accomplissant-sa-mission/
- Source type: own-writing (date and occasion: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain (her text); Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « La scène se passe à Chinon, dans l'appartement royale de Charles VII, Il est entouré de ses favoris et vêtu simplement comme l'un d'eux, sans couronne royale. »
- Translation: "The scene takes place at Chinon in the royal apartment of Charles VII; he is surrounded by his favourites and dressed simply like one of them, without a royal crown."
- Other sources: The baseline says she played Joan in the Joan plays. The photographs of Thérèse as Joan in prison come from this play (see the card on Taxil, Jan–Apr 1897).
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 quotes parts of the Joan plays)
- Confidence: high for the date and cast (Carmel page)
- Notes: The cast list shows careful historical reading: names of the court, the confessor and the priest Massieu are given. It is the longest of her plays.

### F-OW-568 — PN 17 "Vivre d'amour": composed in three days, written down on 26 Feb 1895, 15 stanzas, tune "Il est à moi"
- Claim: The Carmel editors date PN 17 to 26 Feb 1895 and give the recipient as Thérèse herself. They say she conceived and memorised the poem in the three days before Ash Wednesday 1895 and wrote it down on the Tuesday evening. The page gives its tune: "Il est à moi". It has 15 stanzas; the first ends "En notre Amour", and the last "Vivre d'Amour!!!..." Stanza 14 begins "Mourir d'Amour, c'est un bien doux martyre". Stanza 13: "'Vivre d'Amour, quelle étrange folie!' me dit le monde". Stanza 12 compares her with Mary Magdalene who breaks the jar and perfumes his Face.
- When / where: 26 Feb 1895 (Tuesday before Ash Wednesday), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Thérèse
- Source: PN 17, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/vivre-damour-pn-17/
- Source type: own-writing (date and context: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Mourir d'Amour, c'est un bien doux martyre / Et c'est celui que je voudrais souffrir »
- Translation: "To die of love is a very sweet martyrdom, and it is the one I would like to suffer."
- Other sources: CJ 5.8.7 (in the existing card on "Vivre d'amour"); on 16 Jul 1897 Marie Guérin sang stanza 14 before her Communion (CJ July note; LT 255).
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 has the poem; the composition timing and tune are not in the baseline)
- Confidence: high for the date (26 Feb 1895 given by the editors; Ash Wednesday 1895 was 27 Feb, computed from the date of Easter, 14 Apr 1895)
- Notes: The editors link a 2002 thesis by a priest on the poem (not read). Stanza 13 records the worldly reaction ("What a strange folly!").

### F-OW-569 — The Act of Oblation (Pri 6): signed with her three baptismal names, Trinity Sunday, 9 June 1895
- Claim: The Carmel page of the "Offrande à l'Amour Miséricordieux" gives the two leaves. Title: "Offering of myself as a Victim of Holocaust to the Merciful Love of the Good God." She asks to love and to make loved, to save souls, and, since she feels her powerlessness, that God be "himself my holiness". She offers the merits of Jesus, the saints and the Virgin Mary. She says: "I cannot receive Holy Communion as often as I would like, but, Lord, are you not All-Powerful? Stay in me as in the tabernacle." She thanks God "for having made me pass through the crucible of suffering". She writes: "in the evening of this life I shall appear before you with empty hands... All our justices have stains in your eyes. I want to clothe myself with your own Justice." She offers herself "as a victim of holocaust to your merciful love", asks to become "Martyr of your Love", and renews the offering "at each beat of my heart an infinite number of times". Signature: "Marie, Françoise, Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus et de la Sainte Face, rel. carm. ind.", "Feast of the Most Holy Trinity, 9 June in the year of grace 1895."
- When / where: 9 Jun 1895 (Trinity Sunday), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; the Trinity (addressed)
- Source: Pri 6, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/j-m-j-t/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Je vous demande, ô mon Dieu, d'être vous-même ma Sainteté. » ; « Marie, Françoise, Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus et de la Sainte Face rel. carm. ind. / Fête de la Très Sainte Trinité / Le 9 juin de l'an de grâce 1895. »
- Translation: "I ask you, O my God, to be yourself my holiness." ; "Marie, Françoise, Thérèse of the Child Jesus and of the Holy Face, unworthy Carmelite religious. Feast of the Most Holy Trinity, 9 June in the year of grace 1895."
- Other sources: The baseline gives the Act of Oblation as 9–11 June 1895. Ms A records the account. The page dates the text 9 June.
- New to English readers: no (the Act is well known in English)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The signature gives her full baptismal names as "Marie, Françoise, Thérèse". The "empty hands" line ties LT 218 (27 Jan 1897).

### F-OW-570 — RP 4, "Jesus at Bethany" (29 July 1895): she played Jesus
- Claim: The Carmel page dates RP 4 to 29 Jul 1895, the feast of the lay sisters (St Martha's day). - Translation: "I believe it is the one that served St Thérèse of the Child Jesus to represent Our Lord in the play 'Jesus at Bethany' in 1895."

### F-OW-571 — Christmas night 1895: "may your two lilies never be separated" (Pri 9, Céline and Thérèse)
- Claim: A prayer written together by Céline and Thérèse, "Souvenir of the night of Christmas 1895". It quotes Matthew 18:19–20 ("if two of you agree on earth..."). ### F-OW-572 — RP 7, "The Triumph of Humility" (21 June 1896): the play names Diana Vaughan
- Claim: The Carmel page dates RP 7 to 21 Jun 1896, the feast of the prioress Mère Marie de Gonzague. Scene 1: in the recreation room, three novices are together on a free-talk day: Sr Thérèse of the Child Jesus, "dean of the novitiate", Sr Marie-Madeleine, and Sr Marie du Saint-Esprit, a choir postulant. Thérèse (speaking as herself) says: "Satan (the one deprived of love, as our mother St Teresa called him) is content to betroth the souls that belong to him to some of his demons. This thought touched me [deeply] when I read the account of the extraordinary conversion of Diana Vaughan. What a beautiful [...] now that she has become a new Joan of Arc... My greatest desire would be, when her mission is finished, to [see her] unite with Jesus in our little Carmel." Sr Marie-Madeleine answers that Diana would have "ecstasies and revelations", "for the good God [...] fewer privileges than the devil who called her his dear daughter." The transcription shows gaps and bracketed words around this passage. The play then goes on: the novices wish to hear the voices of Heaven and Hell; Lucifer, Baal-Zeboub and demons appear; St Michael weighs their vows against a scroll marked "Humility", and the demon cries "I am conquered!"; angels sing to the tune "La patrie des hirondelles".
- When / where: 21 Jun 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Thérèse (a character in her own play); Sr Marie-Madeleine; Sr Marie du Saint-Esprit; Diana Vaughan (named; a fictional "convert" invented by Léo Taxil); Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress); St Michael; Lucifer; Baal-Zeboub
- Source: RP 7, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/le-triomphe-de-lhumilite/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her text); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Cette pensée m'a [vivement] touchée lorsque j'ai lu le récit de la conversion extraordinaire de Diana Vaughan. Quelle belle [...] maintenant qu'elle est devenue une nouvelle Jeanne d'Arc.... Mon plus grand désir serait lorsque sa mission sera terminée de [la voir] s'unir à Jésus dans notre petit Carmel. »
- Translation: "This thought touched me [deeply] when I read the account of Diana Vaughan's extraordinary conversion. How beautiful [...] now that she has become a new Joan of Arc... My greatest desire would be, when her mission is finished, [to see her] unite with Jesus in our little Carmel."
- New to English readers: yes (the baseline names neither Diana Vaughan nor this play)
- Confidence: high for the text as transcribed; the reason for the blanks in the transcription is not stated on the page.
- Notes: Diana Vaughan was later shown to be a hoax (Léo Taxil, 19 Apr 1897; see the next card). In June 1896 Thérèse spoke of her as a real convert and a "new Joan of Arc". This is a link to Joan of Arc and a check on any account that claims the Carmel never fell for the fraud.

### F-OW-573 — PN 40 "The Carmel's sacristans" (Nov 1896) and her job as sacristan
- Claim: The Carmel editors date PN 40 to Nov 1896: she wrote it for Sr Marie-Philomène de Jésus, head of the altar-bread workshop, at her request, for all the sacristans. In the sacristy itself were the elderly Sr Marie des Anges and Thérèse. Stanza 1: "Here below our sweet office is to prepare for the altar the bread, the wine of the Sacrifice that gives the earth 'Heaven'." Stanza 5: "We hold a holy envy for the work of our hands, for the little white host that will veil the divine Lamb." Stanza 6: "We are hosts too, that Jesus wants to change into himself." Together with LT 220 (24 Feb 1897: "my job as sacristan") it shows she was in the sacristy in 1896–97.
- When / where: Nov 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie-Philomène de Jésus (requester; altar-bread maker); Sr Marie des Anges; Thérèse
- Source: PN 40, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-40/ ; LT 220
- Source type: own-writing (context: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Nous portons une sainte envie / A l'ouvrage de notre main, / A la petite et blanche hostie / Qui doit voiler l'Agneau divin. » ; « Nous sommes aussi des hosties / Que Jésus veut changer en Lui. »
- Translation: "We hold a holy envy for the work of our hands, for the little white host that must veil the divine Lamb." ; "We too are hosts that Jesus wants to change into himself."
- New to English readers: partial (the poem's title and text were not searched in the 1911 edition; the context is new)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The 1911 edition prints a photograph of 1890 captioned "Sœur Thérèse... préparant les vases sacrés lorsqu'elle était sacristine" (seen by chk.py). This poem shows the office as a religious idea, not a chore.

### F-OW-574 — PN 44 "To my little brothers in Heaven" (28 Dec 1896): the four dead siblings
- Claim: The Carmel editors date PN 44 to 28 Dec 1896 (Feast of the Holy Innocents), for herself; they say that in September 1896 she had made a memorial picture for her four brothers and sisters who died as children. The poem has 11 stanzas to the tune "La rose mousse" or "Le fil de la Vierge". She names them "happy little children", "holy Innocents", "little lilies", and says: "It is you whom the Lord gave me as a model, holy Innocents... I want to be your faithful image." Stanza 8: "Charming little imps, your childish audacity pleases the Lord; you dare to caress his Adorable Face." Stanza 11: "Yes, I want to increase the candid phalanx of the Innocents; my sufferings and joys I offer in exchange for children's souls."
- When / where: 28 Dec 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: the four Martin children who died young; Thérèse
- Source: PN 44, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-44/ (page title "A mes Petits Frères du Ciel")
- Source type: own-writing (date and context: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « C'est vous que Le Seigneur me donna pour modèle / Saints Innocents / Je veux être ici-bas votre image fidèle / Petits Enfants. »
- Translation: "It is you whom the Lord gave me as a model, holy Innocents; I want to be your faithful image here below, little children."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the text; the memorial picture of Sept 1896 is the editors' statement.
- Notes: The poem has no names. It connects the letter LT 182 (Feb 1896, the dead siblings' scene) with the feast of the Holy Innocents. She also offers Pri 18, "O Holy Innocents... O Saint Sebastian".

### F-OW-575 — PN 45 "My joy" (21 Jan 1897): "All my soul is there!"; and the scratched-out line in LT 216
- Claim: The Carmel editors say PN 45 "Ma joie" was written at Mère Agnès's request for her feast on 21 Jan 1897, and that Thérèse said as she gave it: "All my soul is there!" It has seven stanzas (tune "Rêve, parfum ou frais murmure"). Every stanza except the last begins "Ma joie, c'est..." ("My joy is to remain little", "to hide myself, to lower myself", "to love suffering"). Last stanza: "Long yet I am willing to live, Lord, if that is your wish; in Heaven I would like to follow you if that pleased you... What are death or life to me? Jesus, my joy is to love you!" The editors also say that in the original of LT 216 (9 Jan 1897) the second half of the phrase "since if there is a Heaven, it is for me" was scratched out, after the copy of her writings made for the Process in 1910, probably because the recipient thought it improper to write a doubt of Heaven; they note the letter's theme continues in PN 45.
- When / where: 21 Jan 1897 (poem); 9 Jan 1897 (letter)
- People: Mère Agnès (recipient of both); Thérèse
- Source: PN 45, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-45/ ; Carmel editors on LT 216 (in _work/lw_all.txt, line 15)
- Source type: own-writing (context and the scratching: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); editors' notes: unknown
- Original words: « Ma joie, c'est de rester petite » ; « Que me font la mort ou la vie? / Jésus, ma joie, c'est de t'aimer ! »
- Translation: "My joy is to stay little." ; "What do death or life matter to me? Jesus, my joy is to love you!"
- New to English readers: yes for "Toute mon âme est là" and the scratching; partial for the poem (in Taylor 1912)
- Confidence: medium (the scratching is the editors' report of the manuscript)
- Notes: The editors say the second half of the LT 216 phrase was erased from the original letter after the 1910 copy. Compare our card on LT 216. Who scratched it is the editors' inference ("the recipient").

- Translation: "I love that unbelieving shore too, which was the object of your ardent love; with happiness I would fly there if the good God called me one day." ; "Come down from Heaven at my last hour."

- New to English readers: yes (not in the baseline)

- Notes: Her last play, written in Feb 1897 when she was already ill. PS 3 "Depuis cinquante ans" (a poem for the same sister's anniversary of 1845/1846) is a second piece for this sister. The saint is a link to another young saint (Stanislas Kostka, a Jesuit novice who died at 17).

### F-OW-578 — The Diana Vaughan hoax and the Lisieux Carmel (Dec 1896 – Apr 1897), as told by the Carmel editors
- Claim: The Carmel site's chronicle of her last year (modern editorial text; facts only, our words) says: (1) on 6 Dec 1896 Mère Agnès asked her uncle Isidore Guérin in a post-script whether he had new documents on Diana Vaughan; (2) on 30 Jan 1897 the Lisieux newspaper "Le Normand" reported a Roman commission's doubts about Diana; (3) Diana's "Memoirs" (Jan–Mar 1897) were available in the Carmel, and Thérèse, seeing Diana criticise the bishops of the commission, thought "this does not come from the good God"; (4) on Easter Monday, 19 Apr 1897, Léo Taxil told a Paris press conference that Diana was a fiction; the Lisieux paper summarised it on 24 Apr; (5) Thérèse learned "that there are people who do evil only for amusement"; (6) the editors add that a photograph of Thérèse as Joan of Arc in prison (Saint Catherine appearing to her; "photo no. 14") had been sent to Diana in summer 1896 at Mère Agnès's suggestion, after she retouched it, that Diana answered, and that Taxil showed this picture at his conference. The editors then read Ms C 5v ("there are really souls who do not have faith") in this light (their interpretation).
- When / where: 6 Dec 1896 – 24 Apr 1897; summer 1896 for the photograph
- People: Léo Taxil; "Diana Vaughan" (fictional); Mère Agnès; Isidore Guérin; Thérèse; a Roman commission (Mgr Lazzareschi is named by the editors)
- Source: Carmel editors, "Dernière année de la vie de Thérèse" (in _work/lw_all.txt lines 17, 51–55, 246–247), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/oeuvres-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-de-therese/
- Source type: scholarship (Carmel editors; facts only, our words)
- Rights: unknown (modern editorial text)
- Original words: « Thérèse découvre qu'il y a des personnes qui ne font le mal que pour s'amuser. » (editors)
- Translation: "Thérèse discovers that there are persons who do evil only for amusement." (editors)
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium (modern chronicle; not checked against 1896–97 press; the photograph link needs a source)
- Notes: Do not present the photo-to-Diana story as fact without a second source. The editors' link with Ms C 5v is their reading. RP 7 (21 Jun 1896) shows Thérèse spoke of Diana as a convert before the hoax was exposed.

### F-OW-579 — PN 51 "A rose stripped of its petals" (19 May 1897) and PN 50 "To Joan of Arc" (May? 1897)
- Claim: PN 51 has five stanzas dated 19 May 1897; it was written at the request of Mère Henriette of the Carmel of Paris (Avenue de Messine). Image: Jesus, taking his first steps from his Mother's arms, and a rose stripped of its petals laid under his feet: "A stripped rose is simply thrown to the wind... like her I abandon myself to you, little Jesus." The editors say Mère Henriette, through Marie de la Trinité, asked for a last stanza on the reward in Heaven; Thérèse answered: "Let the good Mother make that verse herself." PN 50 "To Joan of Arc" (5 stanzas, "May ? 1897", for herself): the editors say she wrote it spontaneously, very ill, rereading her plays, on the theme of prison. It says Joan appears "brighter and more beautiful" in her dark prison than at the king's coronation, and that betrayal brought her the heavenly glow.
- When / where: 19 May 1897 (PN 51); May (?) 1897 (PN 50)
- People: Mère Henriette (Carmel of Paris, avenue de Messine); Marie de la Trinité (go-between); Joan of Arc; Thérèse
- Source: PN 51, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-51/ ; PN 50, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-50/
- Source type: own-writing (dates and context: Carmel editors)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Une rose effeuillée sans recherche se donne / Pour n'être plus. » ; « Jeanne tu m'apparais plus brillante et plus belle / Qu'au sacre de ton roi, dans ta sombre prison. »
- Translation: "A stripped rose gives itself without design, so as to be no more." ; "Joan, you appear to me brighter and more beautiful in your dark prison than at the crowning of your king."
- New to English readers: partial (PN 51 is well known; the request from Paris and the reply about the last stanza are not; PN 50 and its context are new)
- Confidence: high for the texts; medium for the editors' dating of PN 50 ("mai ?").
- Notes: The "stripped rose" is the image behind the "shower of roses" saying. PN 50 shows her final view of Joan: not the victorious knight but the prisoner.

### F-OW-580 — PN 54 "Why I love you, O Mary" (May 1897): the poem on all she would preach on the Virgin
- Claim: The Carmel editors date PN 54 to May 1897 and say Thérèse wrote it for herself, encouraged by Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur. They quote her words to Céline: "I have always dreamed of expressing in a song to the Blessed Virgin all that I think of her" (given as "PO 667", the process). The page gives the tune: "Pourquoi m'avoir livré l'autre jour, ô ma Mère". It is her longest poem in her last year (the text file is about 10,000 characters). On 21 Aug 1897 she said of the poem: "I have said in my Canticle 'Why I love you, O Mary!' all that I would preach about her" (CJ 21.8.3).
- When / where: May 1897, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (who encouraged her); Céline (told); Thérèse
- Source: PN 54, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/pn-54/ ; CJ 21.8.3 (Paroles retrouvées, 21 Aug)
- Source type: own-writing (date and context: Carmel editors)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Enfin j'ai dit dans mon Cantique : «Pourquoi je t'aime, ô Marie!» tout ce que je prêcherais sur elle. »
- Translation: "In the end I said in my Canticle 'Why I love you, O Mary!' everything I would preach about her."
- Other sources: The Carnet jaune, 21 Aug 1897, records her longer talk on how she would preach on the Virgin (existing card).
- New to English readers: partial (Taylor 1912 has the poem)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The poem date "May 1897" is the editors' date; the exact day is not given.

### F-OW-581 — PS 8, "You who know my extreme littleness": her last poem, composed in a sleepless night
- Claim: The Carmel editors date PS 8 to "a few days before 16 July 1897", for herself. It has one stanza (8 lines): "You who know my extreme littleness, you do not fear to lower yourself to me! Come into my heart, O white Host I love; come into my heart, it longs for you! Ah! I would like your goodness to let me die of love after this favour. Jesus! hear the cry of my tenderness. Come into my heart!" The Carnet jaune (13 Jul 1897, no. 4) says she composed the verse "during the night" for the Communion: "I composed it very easily, it is extraordinary; I thought I could no longer make verses." On the feast of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, 16 Jul 1897, Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie sang it before her Communion in the infirmary, and after Communion the verse of "Vivre d'amour" (stanza 14) (Carmel editors; LT 255).
- When / where: about 13 Jul 1897 (composed); 16 Jul 1897 (sung), infirmary
- People: Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin, singer); Thérèse; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: PS 8, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/ps8/ ; CJ 13.7.4, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/cj-juillet-1897/ ; LT 255 (16 Jul 1897)
- Source type: own-writing; eyewitness record (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: « Toi qui connais ma petitesse extrême / Tu ne crains pas de t'abaisser vers moi ! » ; « Je l'ai composé bien facilement, c'est extraordinaire ; je croyais que je ne pouvais plus faire de vers. »
- Translation: "You who know my extreme littleness, you do not fear to lower yourself to me!" ; "I composed it very easily, it is extraordinary; I thought I could no longer make verses."
- New to English readers: partial (the poem is in Taylor 1912; the circumstance is new)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The original is lost; the text is known from a facsimile of the Process (session 19, transcribed by the Benedictines of Bayeux), per the Carmel page.

### F-OW-582 — "Prayer to obtain humility" (Pri 20), 16 July 1897, copied for Sr Marthe
- Claim: Pri 20 is dated on the page "16 juillet 1897". It is a prayer beginning "O Jesus, when you were a Traveller on earth you said: Learn from me that I am meek and humble of heart." She asks to submit her will to her sisters "without asking whether they have the right to command me", as Jesus obeyed even his executioners; she sees his humility in the Host, obedient to all his priests, "whether they love you or are lukewarm or cold". She asks "that my sisters always put me in the last place and that I be truly convinced this place is mine", and asks Jesus "to send me a humiliation each time I try to raise myself above others". She notes the daily failure: "each morning I take the resolution... and in the evening I see I have committed many faults of pride; I am tempted to be discouraged, but... discouragement is also pride." She repeats: "O Jesus, meek and humble of heart, make my heart like yours." LT 256 (16 Jul 1897) tells Sr Marthe she will give her "the prayer on humility, not quite finished being copied".
- When / where: 16 Jul 1897, infirmary
- People: Sr Marthe de Jésus (for whom she was copying it); Thérèse
- Source: Pri 20, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/jesus/ ; LT 256, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-256-a-soeur-marthe-de-jesus-16-juillet-1897/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain; Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « je veux, afin de répondre à votre amour, désirer que mes soeurs me mettent toujours à la dernière place et bien me persuader que cette place est la mienne » ; « le découragement est aussi de l'orgueil »
- Translation: "To answer your love I want to desire that my sisters always put me in the last place and to be truly persuaded that this place is mine." ; "Discouragement is also pride."
- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT from the 1911 edition)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: It is one of the last long texts she wrote (the same week as the trembling-hand letters). The dedication to Sr Marthe is our link from LT 256 and the same date; the prayer page itself names no recipient.

### F-OW-583 — Her last written text (Pri 21), 8 September 1897: "If I were Queen of Heaven"
- Claim: The Carmel editors' note on Pri 21 says: "Last text in Thérèse's hand." The text, dated 8 Sep 1897 (the feast of the Nativity of Mary, and the seventh anniversary of her profession): "O Mary, if I were the Queen of Heaven and you were Thérèse, I would want to be Thérèse so that you might be the Queen of Heaven!" On the back is a picture of Our Lady of Victories, on which Thérèse had stuck the little saxifrage flower her father gave her when she asked to enter Carmel (the editors refer to Ms A 50v).
- When / where: 8 Sep 1897, infirmary of the Carmel of Lisieux (22 days before her death)
- People: the Virgin Mary (addressed); Louis Martin (the flower's giver, years earlier)
- Source: Pri 21, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/si-jetais-la-reine-du-ciel/
- Source type: own-writing (the note on "last text" and the flower: Carmel editors, facts only)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription and note: unknown
- Original words: « O Marie, si j'étais la Reine du Ciel et que vous soyez Thérèse, je voudrais être Thérèse afin que vous soyez la Reine du Ciel !.............. » ; « [Dernier texte de la main de Thérèse. Le verso est une image de Notre Dame des Victoires sur laquelle Thérèse a collé la petite fleur de saxifrage] » (editors)
- Translation: "O Mary, if I were the Queen of Heaven and you were Thérèse, I would want to be Thérèse so that you might be the Queen of Heaven!" ; "Last text in Thérèse's hand. The back is a picture of Our Lady of Victories, on which Thérèse stuck the little saxifrage flower."
- Other sources: Ms A tells of the little white flower her father gave her when she asked to enter Carmel (Pentecost 1887, 29 May by the calendar); she put it in her Imitation of Christ at the chapter "that Jesus must be loved above all things", and the stem broke near the root (Ms A, _work/msA.f.txt line 448).
- New to English readers: yes for the flower on the image and the "last text" status; partial for the words (they are also in the Carnet jaune and Novissima Verba)
- Confidence: high for the text and date; the "last text" claim is the editors'.
- Notes: The editors' note says the flower was moved from the Imitation to this picture. Compare the card on her last letter LT 266 (25 Aug 1897). The picture is Our Lady of Victories (a Paris shrine; other cards: a novena for her cure in 1883 and again in June 1897).

## Appended block K — index cards (connections to other saints; surprise facts), continued numbering

### F-OW-584 — Index: connections to other saints found in LT 182–266, Carnet jaune July 1897 and the poems and plays
- Claim: This is an index card, not a new claim. It lists the saints and holy persons her own texts link her to, with the F-OW cards where each is recorded. Joan of Arc: RP 1 (F-OW-565), PN 4 (F-OW-566), RP 3 (F-OW-567), RP 7 (F-OW-572, Diana Vaughan "a new Joan of Arc"), PN 50 (F-OW-579), her own account of the childhood dream (F-OW-482), Joan before her tribunal (F-OW-531), Joan's "jealousy of men" (F-OW-544), Pri 17 (prayer inspired by a picture of Joan; card not written). Théophane Vénard: PN 47 (F-OW-576), the farewell card (F-OW-499), letters to Roulland (F-OW-477, F-OW-485), Roulland's relics (LT 226; F-OW-485). Anne of Jesus: Ms B dream (F-OW-214), PN 33 (12 Jun 1896, the Carmel editors link it to Anne's vision). Aloysius Gonzaga: F-OW-231 and the shower-of-roses cards (earlier). Stanislas Kostka and Francis Borgia: RP 8 (F-OW-577). St Teresa of Avila: her sayings in LT 201 (F-OW-459) and LT 221 (F-OW-478). St John of the Cross: LT 188 (F-OW-443), LT 245 (F-OW-499), CJ 27.7.5 (F-OW-543). Margaret Mary Alacoque and Claude de la Colombière: LT 224 (F-OW-481). Mary Magdalene and Augustine: LT 247 (F-OW-501), LT 259 (F-OW-511), RP 4 (F-OW-570). St Francis of Assisi: "brother donkey" (F-OW-477), holy card to Bellière (F-OW-469). St Cecilia, Genevieve, Agnes, Celine, Sebastian and the Innocents: LT 182 (F-OW-438), PN 44 (F-OW-574), Pri 18. Mère Geneviève of the Lisieux Carmel: LT 182, 184 (F-OW-438, F-OW-441). Frederick Faber: LT 202 (F-OW-462). Mme Swetchine: CJ 23.7.5 (F-OW-536). Saint Michael: RP 7 and RP 1.
- When / where: 1894–1897
- People: as listed
- Source: cards F-OW-214, 231, 438–441, 443, 459, 462, 477–482, 485, 499, 501, 511, 531, 536, 543–544, 565–567, 570, 572, 574, 576–577, 579
- Source type: own-writing (index)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: —
- Translation: —
- New to English readers: yes for most links
- Confidence: high (each link is in a cited card)
- Notes: Pri 17 ("Seigneur, Dieu des armées", "Prayer inspired by a picture of the Venerable Joan of Arc") was read but not carded: "You alone are my shield... it is not on battlefields that I shall fight; my sword is Love", and Joan's saying "One must battle for God to give victory". PN 33 and Anne of Jesus: link is the editors', not on the page text.

### F-OW-585 — Index: surprise facts from this batch ("I never knew that")
- Claim: An index card for the writer of the biography. Surprises found in LT 182–266, CJ July 1897, and the poems and plays, each with its card: (1) In June 1896 she wrote Diana Vaughan into her play as a real convert and "new Joan of Arc" (F-OW-572); the Carmel later learned the hoax (F-OW-578). (2) She asked her sister not to believe what would be said after her death (F-OW-491). (3) The 1911 edition moved her farewell letters (9 Jun and 13 Jul 1897) to 26 Jul and 14 Aug and changed why she was glad to die (F-OW-498). (4) She played Jesus in a play, "Jesus at Bethany", 29 Jul 1895 (F-OW-570). (5) The two-children parable of 18 Jul 1897 is not in the 1911 edition (F-OW-509). (6) A daily 3 p.m. fever is recorded on 3 Apr 1897, and by mid-July she lived on milk (F-OW-479, F-OW-505). (7) She thought of herself as "a little thief" who would take things from Heaven (F-OW-561, F-OW-439). (8) She wanted a lock of Roulland's hair "as a relic of a future martyr" (F-OW-461). (9) She told the cook's lobster story to a missionary in China (F-OW-476). (10) Her last written text is a play on words about Mary and herself, with the saxifrage flower stuck to a picture (F-OW-583). (11) Her father offered Jerusalem if she delayed entry (F-OW-460). (12) A sister said at recreation she was no saint because she had not been humiliated (F-OW-551). (13) The Carmel editors report that the phrase "if there is a Heaven it is for me" was scratched out of the original of LT 216 (F-OW-575). (14) She wrote of Céline and herself as possible "foundresses" for Saigon (F-OW-463).
- When / where: 1895–1897
- People: as in the cards
- Source: the cards named above
- Source type: own-writing (index)
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel notes: unknown
- Original words: —
- Translation: —
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high (each item is in a cited card)
- Notes: The item on Diana Vaughan needs care: the RP 7 text and the June 1896 date are on the Carmel page; the photograph link is the editors' report only.
